# AI Life Manager

Built incrementally per the approved PRD, Architecture, Database, and API Design documents — one feature at a time, each fully working before the next starts.

## What's actually implemented

**Foundation:** authentication (real Clerk sessions or a documented local dev-auth bypass), the core identity data model (`User`/`Subscription`, Database Design Document §4.1), and the Today screen shell.

**Tasks feature:** the `Task`/`Goal`/`Tag` data model (Database Design Document §4.2) — create, update, complete, cancel tasks; subtasks via `parentTaskId`; tagging; optional goal linking. `TodayPlan.tasks` now returns your real open tasks (previously an honest placeholder). The Today screen renders the task list with a complete-on-tap checkbox and a quick-add input.

**Calendar feature (native events):** the `CalendarEvent` data model (Database Design Document §4.3) — create, update, delete events; `startTime`/`endTime`; an `isImmovable` flag the future AI scheduling layer must never violate. External sync (Google/Apple/Microsoft) is a deliberately separate follow-up increment — see "What's not built yet" below. `TodayPlan.events` returns today's events, bucketed by *your* timezone (not the server's), so an 11pm event doesn't silently land on the wrong day. A new `/calendar` day view lets you page backward/forward and add or remove events for any day.

- **`apps/backend`** — NestJS + GraphQL (code-first) + Prisma. `me`/`updateProfile`, `todayPlan`, `tasks` (cursor-paginated), `goals`, `calendarEventsInRange`/`calendarEvents`, and the full task/goal/tag/calendar-event mutation set. Every lookup is scoped to the authenticated user's internal id — verified with e2e tests that a second identity cannot edit someone else's task or event.
- **`apps/web`** — Next.js 14 (App Router) + Tailwind, styled from the UI/UX Design Document's exact color/type tokens. Apollo Client wired to the backend. Today screen showing the greeting, real task list, today's events, and quick-add for both. A `/calendar` page for browsing and editing any day. Clerk sign-in/sign-up pages scaffolded for when real Clerk keys are added.

**Google Calendar sync:** the `calendar_accounts` table (Database Design Document §4.3) — OAuth connect flow, encrypted token storage, and both an initial and incremental (`syncToken`-based) sync of your Google Calendar events into `calendar_events`. Synced events show up on the Today screen and `/calendar` day view right alongside native ones, tagged "Google." Started as pull-only (Google → app); see the two-way sync entry below for what changed. Google-only — see "What's not built yet" for Microsoft/Apple.

**Two-way calendar sync (deletes):** closes the biggest gap from the original pull-only sync — deleting a synced event in this app now really deletes it on Google's side too, instead of silently reappearing on the next "Sync now." Scoped deliberately narrow, per the approved plan: only deletes push back, not edits (the app has no edit UI for calendar events at all yet, native or synced, so "push edits back" wasn't actually buildable without a separate new-UI increment first). Under the hood: the Google OAuth scope had to widen from `calendar.readonly` to `calendar.events` (still narrower than the full `calendar` scope — no access to calendar settings/sharing, just events), which means **every already-connected account needs to reconnect once** to pick up write access; a brand-new connection gets it automatically. If you try to delete a synced event before reconnecting, you'll get a clear "reconnect to allow this" message rather than a silent failure or a confusing revert.

**Signal tracking (mood/energy/sleep):** the `mood_entries`/`energy_entries`/`sleep_entries` data model (Database Design Document §4.5) — this is the "state (energy/mood)" half of the Today screen's personal-dashboard promise (PRD §7.1). A 2-tap mood/energy check-in widget and a sleep log (hours + quality) now sit right under the Today header. Mood and energy can be logged more than once a day (each tap is its own entry — a lightweight check-in, not a form); sleep is one correctable entry per night, so logging again for the same morning updates it rather than duplicating it. Manual entry only in this increment — wearable sync (Apple Health/Google Fit/Oura/Whoop) and AI-inferred energy are PRD P1s the `source` enums already reserve values for.

**AI daily planning:** the `ai_plan_runs` table (Database Design Document §4.6) and a real call to Anthropic's Claude API (API Design Document §4.4). A "Generate my plan" button on the Today screen sends your open tasks, today's calendar, your latest mood/energy/sleep check-ins, and today's habits to the model, which proposes start times for a subset of your tasks. Before anything is shown, a deterministic policy layer rejects any proposal that references a task that doesn't exist, overlaps a fixed (`isImmovable`) calendar event, overlaps a habit's protected time (see Habits below), or overlaps another proposed change, or falls outside "now until end of day" — dropped suggestions are called out in the plan's summary text rather than silently disappearing. Nothing is written to your real tasks until you tap **Accept**; **Reject** discards the proposal. Accepting writes real `scheduledStart`/`scheduledEnd` onto the affected tasks and marks them `isAiScheduled`. Free-form AI chat (below) and persistent AI Memory (still not built) were the two things deliberately deferred out of this increment.

**Habits:** the `habits`/`habit_logs` data model (Database Design Document §4.4) — recurring commitments that show up as a daily checklist. "Simple patterns first" scope, per the approved plan for this increment: a habit repeats either every day or on specific days of the week (Mon/Wed/Fri, for example) — the underlying `rrule` column stores a real iCalendar RRULE string (same standard the doc calls for), so richer patterns later are an API change, not a database migration. A new `/habits` page lets you create habits (title, daily-or-specific-days, an optional preferred time, an optional protected duration) and deactivate ones you no longer want; the Today screen shows a checklist of only the habits actually due *today*, with a tap-to-toggle checkbox (unlike completing a task, a habit check can be un-checked — a misclick shouldn't need a workaround). Deactivating a habit hides it from Today but keeps its history. **The AI daily planner now takes habits into account** ("hard-protected, like calendar events" scope): a due-today, not-yet-completed habit with a preferred time becomes a real protected time block the policy layer enforces exactly like a fixed calendar event — the AI cannot schedule a task over it, and a proposal that tries gets dropped and called out in the plan summary. A habit with no preferred time is only ever advisory context in the prompt ("also needs to happen today, no fixed time"), never positionally enforced, since there's no specific time slot to protect.

**Chat:** the `ai_conversations`/`ai_chat_messages` data model (Database Design Document §4.6) and a new `/chat` page. Replies now stream token-by-token over a real GraphQL subscription, the exact subscription-based design the API Design Document originally called for (see the Real-time chat streaming entry below). The AI can now also take seven real actions when you actually ask for them — create a task, complete one, reschedule one, log a mood or energy check-in, add a calendar event, or save something to remember for later (see the Tool-calling actions in Chat and Expanded tool set for Chat entries below) — shown inline as its own distinct "✓ ..." system note in the conversation, not just claimed in prose. Anything outside those, it still says so plainly and points you back to the app. Every reply is grounded in your real, current data — the same open tasks, today's calendar, and latest mood/energy/sleep check-ins the AI daily planner sees — built fresh for every message, not cached. You can start a new chat or reopen a previous one from the same page; conversations are titled from their first message rather than a real AI-generated summary, a deliberate simplification to avoid a second AI call just to name a thread.

**AI Memory:** the `ai_memory_facts` data model (Database Design Document §4.6, PRD §9 — "the moat"). "Manual memory first" scope, per the approved plan for this increment: on a new `/memory` page, you directly tell the AI things to remember ("never schedule calls before 10am," "I'm a morning person") as a plain list you can add, edit, and remove — no automatic learning, no vector embeddings, no background workflow yet. What's real about it: both the AI daily planner and chat now fetch this list fresh on every request and inject it into the prompt as stated preferences, so it actually changes what the AI proposes and says, not just something stored and ignored. One honest limit worth knowing: memory is advisory context passed to the model, not a hard rule enforced by code the way the AI planner's fixed-event conflict checking is — a stated preference makes the AI much more likely to respect it, but (like any LLM instruction) isn't a guarantee the same way "don't schedule over this calendar event" is.

**Timezone auto-sync:** a small cross-cutting fix, not a PRD feature on its own. Every "today"/"now" calculation on the backend (Habits, Signals, the AI planner, Chat) reads `user.timezone`, which `getOrCreateFromAuth` hardcoded to `"UTC"` for every new account — meaning a brand-new user's "today" silently didn't match their real local day until they manually fixed it. There was already a working `updateProfile` mutation to change it, but nothing on the frontend ever called it. Fixed with a small, invisible `TimezoneSync` component (mounted once, app-wide) that reads the browser's real IANA timezone (`Intl.DateTimeFormat().resolvedOptions().timeZone`) and silently syncs it to the profile if it doesn't match what's stored — no settings screen, since there's nothing for a person to actually decide here.

**Microsoft (Outlook/365) calendar sync:** brings in your Outlook/365 calendar the same way Google's does — a "Connect Microsoft Calendar" card on `/calendar`, right below the Google one, and synced events show up tagged "Microsoft" alongside native and Google ones. Started pull-only (mirrors how Google sync itself started); deleting a Microsoft-synced event now pushes back to Outlook too — see the "Microsoft push-deletes" entry below for that follow-up. Under the hood it's structurally parallel to Google's integration but genuinely different in the details: Microsoft's OAuth login goes through `login.microsoftonline.com` instead of Google's accounts page, incremental sync uses a "delta link" (a full replay URL Microsoft hands back) instead of Google's sync token, deleted events show up as a `@removed` marker instead of a status field, and Microsoft rotates your refresh token on every use (so the app has to save the new one every single time, not just the access token) where Google mostly doesn't. Reuses the same `TOKEN_ENCRYPTION_KEY`/`OAUTH_STATE_SECRET` secrets Google sync already uses — no new secrets to generate, just a new Azure app registration (see "Picking up" below).

**Completed tasks view:** closes a real gap you ran into yourself — checking off a task on Today made it disappear with nowhere to see it again. The backend's cursor-paginated `tasks(status: ...)` query already supported filtering to `COMPLETED` (built and e2e-tested back in the Tasks increment), it just had no frontend consumer. "More" in the bottom nav is now a real link to a new page listing your completed tasks, most recent first, each showing when it was completed ("Today at 2:15 PM," "3 days ago," etc.), with a **Load more** button for anything past the first 20. Originally view-only; un-completing a task (below) closed that gap.

**Un-completing a task:** the undo button the completed-tasks view was missing — each completed task on `/more` now has an **Undo** link that moves it back to open (`PENDING`) and clears its completion data (`completedAt`, `actualDurationMinutes`), rather than making you re-add it from scratch as a new task. It reappears on the Today screen right away, same as any other open task. Scoped narrowly on purpose: this is specifically the undo for a *completion* — a new `reopenTask` mutation that refuses to do anything to a task that wasn't actually `COMPLETED` (a cancelled task, say), returning a clear error instead of silently changing something you didn't ask it to change.

**Automatic AI Memory learning (statistical, not ML):** the first real step toward PRD §9's "the moat" — the AI Memory system now learns one thing about you on its own, without you having to type it into `/memory` yourself. "Simple statistical learning first" scope, per the approved plan: no vector embeddings, no background workflow, no ML model — just real counting over data that already exists. Every time you Accept or Reject a proposed plan, the backend looks at your last 10 responded plan runs and, once there are at least 3, checks whether a clear pattern has emerged: if you're rejecting half or more of them, it writes a fact like "Often rejects proposed daily plans (3 of the last 3) — favor lighter, more conservative scheduling suggestions"; if you've accepted every single one, it writes "Consistently accepts proposed daily plans." A middling, in-between rate writes nothing — deliberately, so the AI isn't fed a vague, made-up-sounding fact just to have one. This fact isn't just stored — it's now injected into the AI planner and chat prompts alongside your manually-typed preferences from `/memory`, so a real pattern in how you respond actually starts shaping future proposals. It's intentionally *not* shown on the `/memory` page itself (which stays your manually-curated list) — this is a known, deliberate gap, not an oversight. `task_duration_accuracy` and `chronotype` — the other two signals the PRD envisions — were still not built as of this increment; both are now done, see their own entries elsewhere in this list.

**Microsoft push-deletes:** closes the same gap for Outlook/365 that the two-way sync increment closed for Google — deleting a Microsoft-synced event in this app now really deletes it in Outlook too, instead of silently reappearing on the next "Sync now." Same shape as the Google follow-up: the Microsoft OAuth scope had to widen, this time from `Calendars.Read` to `Calendars.ReadWrite`, so **every already-connected Microsoft account needs to reconnect once** to pick up write access (a brand-new connection gets it automatically), and trying to delete a synced event before reconnecting gets a clear "reconnect to allow this" message rather than a silent failure. `CalendarService.delete` now looks at which provider an event actually came from (Google vs. Microsoft) and routes the remote delete to the right one — worth calling out since it's new dispatch logic, not just a copy-paste of the Google path. Also fixed a small display bug found while building this: the "synced from" badge next to an event, and the reconnect message itself, were hardcoded to always say "Google" regardless of which provider the event actually came from — a Microsoft-synced event was silently mislabeled ever since Microsoft sync first shipped. Both now show the real provider.

**Focus sessions / Pomodoro timer:** a simple deep-work timer (PRD §7.1, P0), reachable from a **Focus** link on any Today task (pre-fills that task) or a standalone **Start a focus session** link on Today itself. Worth flagging: this domain was never actually modeled in the approved Database Design Document — its own table inventory (§4/§5) lists every other domain but skips this one, even though the PRD marks it P0. That's a real gap in the approved docs, not something deferred on purpose, so the `focus_sessions` table and its API were designed fresh this round rather than following a pre-approved spec, using the same conventions (UUID PK, `userId` scoping, `created_at`) every other table already follows. "Simple, one at a time" scope: a single countdown (no automatic work/break Pomodoro cycling yet), optionally linked to a task, and starting a second session while one's already running is refused with a clear message rather than silently abandoning the first. The countdown itself lives entirely in the browser (recomputed from the session's real start time + planned duration on a 1-second tick) so reloading the page mid-session picks the countdown back up rather than losing it.

**Apple (CalDAV) calendar sync:** the third and final calendar provider from the PRD's "Google/Apple/Outlook, non-negotiable" P0 list — a "Connect Apple Calendar" card on `/calendar`, below the Microsoft one, with events synced in tagged "Apple." Pull-only, same starting scope Google and Microsoft sync each began with. Genuinely different shape of work than the other two: CalDAV (RFC 4791) has no OAuth consent screen at all — connecting is a direct form (your Apple ID + an **app-specific password**, generated at appleid.apple.com; see "Picking up" below) submitted straight to our own server, not a redirect to Apple's site. Under the hood it's the most different of the three integrations: XML request/response bodies over WebDAV-style PROPFIND/REPORT methods instead of a JSON REST API, a multi-step discovery chain (principal → calendar-home-set → pick the first real calendar) since CalDAV has no fixed per-user calendar URL, HTTP Basic Auth instead of bearer tokens, and incremental sync via CalDAV's own `sync-collection` REPORT (RFC 6578) — functionally the same role as Google's syncToken or Microsoft's deltaLink, just a different mechanism. No general-purpose XML or CalDAV library was added as a dependency; the client hand-rolls the small number of specific requests this app needs (same "simple subset first" precedent as the habits `rrule.ts` file), which does mean it's narrower than a full CalDAV implementation — see "What's not built yet" for the specific gaps that leaves.

**Journaling:** the `journal_entries` data model (Database Design Document §4.5, listed under Signal Collection there since journal sentiment is meant to feed mood inference) — a new `/journal` page (finally a real link, not just a placeholder label in the bottom nav) where you write free-text entries, edit or delete past ones, and see them most-recent-first with **Load more** for anything past the first 20. "Write and read first" scope: a handful of tappable guided-prompt starters ("What went well today?" and similar) are there to nudge you if you're staring at a blank page, but they just insert a starting line into the composer, not a whole structured-prompt system. `sentimentScore` stays null on every entry for now — the doc's own note is that it gets "populated async after write by the AI Memory pipeline," and that pipeline doesn't exist yet (same honest gap as the still-unbuilt chronotype/task-duration-accuracy AI Memory signals). The field is still exposed in the API so it's there, unused, for whenever that pipeline lands.

**Daily reflection:** the `daily_reflections` data model (Database Design Document §5.1) — a new `/reflection` page (reachable via a **Reflect on today →** link on Today, same not-a-nav-tab reasoning as Focus sessions) with the PRD's fixed 3-question end-of-day ritual: what went well, what was challenging, what you want to carry into tomorrow. One reflection per calendar day — submitting again today corrects that day's answers rather than creating a duplicate, same upsert-by-date pattern the Sleep check-in already uses. Unlike journaling's sentiment analysis (still unbuilt), the "AI-summarized" half of this feature is real: submitting calls Claude synchronously (same `AnthropicClient.sendMessage` chat already uses, not a new AI integration) to write a short, warm one-or-two-sentence summary of your three answers, shown right above them. If `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` isn't configured, the reflection still saves successfully with no summary — same honest degradation the AI daily planner and chat already use for a missing key, not a hard failure.

**Morning/evening routines:** a `routines` table (Database Design Document §5.1) for the checklist template, plus a freshly-designed `routine_logs` table for day-to-day completion — worth flagging, since like Focus sessions before it, the approved Database Design Document specs the checklist itself but never actually modeled per-day completion tracking; that's a real gap in the approved doc, not something deferred on purpose, so `RoutineLog` was designed fresh this round using the same conventions (UUID PK, `userId`-scoped-via-parent, `@@unique` compound key) every other table already follows. A new `/routines` page (reachable via a **Set up routines →** link on Today, same not-a-nav-tab reasoning as Focus and Reflection) lets you build a morning checklist and an evening checklist — free-text steps, add/remove, one of each type per person (`@@unique([userId, type])` — creating a second "morning" routine replaces the first rather than creating two). Once set up, both checklists render directly on the Today screen as tap-to-toggle checkboxes, same interaction as Habits. The one real AI piece: turning on "Let AI reorder steps each day" has Claude (same `AnthropicClient.sendMessage` reflection/chat already use) resequence your steps around today's first calendar meeting each time you load Today — before trusting the response, the backend checks it's a genuine reordering of your exact steps (same multiset, nothing added, dropped, or reworded); anything else — a garbled reply, a network failure, no API key configured — silently falls back to your stored order rather than risking a checklist that lost or invented a step.

**Task duration estimation:** the PRD's "AI-assisted estimate" (§7.1) and "learns actual-vs-estimated time per user, improves over time" (§7.4) rows — both `estimatedDurationMinutes` and `actualDurationMinutes` were real `Task` columns since the very first Tasks increment, but honestly, until now, no screen ever actually set either one. Quick Add on Today now has a small optional duration field next to the title, plus an **AI** button that calls Claude for a one-shot suggested duration you can accept or overwrite — never automatic, never blocking task creation. Checking a task off now opens a small inline "How long did this actually take?" prompt (minutes, with a **Skip** if you don't want to bother) instead of completing immediately, which is what actually produces the real actual-vs-estimated data pairs this feature needs. The "improves over time" half is real, not just a slogan: once you've completed at least 3 tasks with both an estimate and a real actual time, the backend computes your average actual-vs-estimated ratio and — if it's clearly off in either direction — writes a `task_duration_accuracy` AI Memory fact (same statistical-pattern mechanism as the existing plan accept/reject learning, see the automatic AI Memory learning entry above), which then gets folded into every future AI duration estimate's prompt, so a person who consistently under-estimates starts getting nudged-up suggestions. This also closes one of the two remaining gaps the automatic AI Memory learning increment left open — see "What's not built yet" below for the one that's still open (chronotype).

**AI recommendations (breaks, workouts, meals):** the PRD's §7.4 "core to the 'automatically adjusts' promise" row. Worth flagging up front, same as Focus sessions and Routines before it: the approved Database Design Document's table inventory never actually modeled this domain even though the PRD marks it P0 — a real gap in the approved docs, so `ai_recommendation_runs` was designed fresh this round, one row per user per day (JSONB array of individual suggestions, same shape as `AiPlanRun.diff`). A new card right on the Today screen (below the AI daily plan card) lets you tap **Get recommendations**, and Claude — grounded in your real open tasks, today's calendar, mood/energy/sleep check-ins, due habits, and your AI Memory context block (manual `/memory` preferences plus the automatically-learned facts), the same context the daily planner already uses — suggests 1 to 3 short, specific things across break/workout/meal categories (not forced to always cover all three; a light day might only get one). Purely advisory, same "propose, never auto-commit" principle the AI daily planner already follows (Architecture Document §4.4's `send_recommendation` tool) — there's nothing to Accept, only **Dismiss**, since nothing here ever touches your real tasks or calendar. **Refresh** regenerates a fresh set for the day, replacing the previous one entirely (including anything you'd already dismissed) rather than merging — a deliberate simplification, same full-replace precedent `SetRoutineInput` already established, not an accident.

**Weekly/monthly AI plan generation:** the PRD's §7.4 "AI daily/weekly/monthly plan generation — generates and regenerates structured plans, not just suggestions" row, the other two-thirds of it. Rather than a separate system, this widens the exact same `requestReplan`/`AiPlanRun` machinery the daily planner already uses to a longer time horizon — `AiPlanRun` gained a `scope` column (`DAY`/`WEEK`/`MONTH`, defaulting to `DAY` so every plan run and every call site that predates this increment, including every existing e2e test, behaves exactly as it did before). A new **Longer-range plan** card on Today, below the daily plan card, has a Week/Month toggle plus the same Generate → review → Accept/Reject flow as the daily one, just with date-and-time labels since a multi-day plan can't rely on "today" being implied. Real constraints, not just a wider search space: WEEK looks 7 days out and MONTH 30 (a fixed rolling window, not real calendar-week/month boundaries — the simplest correct approximation for a first pass), every fixed calendar event across the *entire* window is still a hard constraint the policy layer enforces exactly like the daily planner already does for today, and — new for this increment — WEEK/MONTH proposals are also checked against a 7am–9pm local-time window per day, since without one a multi-day plan could otherwise legitimately place something at 3am four days out (the original daily planner never needed this check; a single day's own boundary already capped it, so DAY scope's policy is completely unchanged by this addition). One deliberate scope cut: habit protection stays today-only even for a week/month plan — see "What's not built yet" below.

**Chronotype AI Memory signal:** the third and last of the PRD §9 AI Memory signals — and the one the PRD is most explicit about: chronotype must be "learned empirically, not just self-reported" (§9). That bar matters here: this deliberately does *not* just average your self-reported energy check-ins and call it a day (a timestamped self-report is still self-report). Instead, `MemoryService.refreshChronotypePattern` requires two independent signals to agree before writing anything — when your energy check-ins score highest (morning/afternoon/evening), and, separately, when your *completed* Focus sessions actually cluster (a real, own-choice, finished-a-block-of-deep-work behavior, not a survey answer) — and only writes a fact once both signals point at the same daypart, with at least 5 energy samples and 3 completed focus sessions to draw from. If the two disagree, or there isn't enough data yet, it writes nothing, same "don't manufacture a fact from a coin-flip margin" discipline the other two automatic-learning signals already established. Triggered best-effort from `FocusService.complete()` — right when a completed session gives fresh data to compute from, the same "recompute right where the new data comes from" shape the other two signals use. Same as `intervention_response` and `task_duration_accuracy`: injected into the AI planner, chat, recommendations, and duration-estimate prompts alongside your `/memory` preferences, but deliberately not shown on the `/memory` page itself.

**Smart notifications:** the PRD's §7.1 "context-aware, quiet-hours respecting, batched not spammy" row, and the one clear exception to this whole project's usual "never modeled in the approved docs" gap-fill story — the `notifications` table itself is real, specced down to the column (Database Design Document §4.7); it was the *preferences* (quiet hours, channel opt-in) that were the gap this time, since the API Design Document names a real `updateNotificationPreferences` mutation with nowhere approved for it to write. Those five columns live directly on `User` now (a 1:1 settings shape, not worth a separate table). The honest, load-bearing simplification is on the delivery side: the approved design calls for a Temporal-driven dispatcher fanning out over FCM/APNs/Postmark/Twilio — none of which exist anywhere in this project, so this pass replaces all of that with the simplest correct in-process equivalent: a notification is created the moment something worth telling you about actually happens (generating an AI daily/weekly/monthly plan, generating AI recommendations — both real, existing triggers, not placeholders), deferred past your configured quiet hours if it would otherwise land during them, and delivered by nothing more than you opening the app — a new **Notifications** page (reachable via a small **Notifications (N) →** link on Today, same not-a-nav-tab pattern as Focus/Reflection/Routines) lists what's due, and that's the entire "push." Repeat notifications of the same type within 12 hours update the existing one instead of stacking a second — that's the real "batched, not spammy" behavior, done without any batching infrastructure at all. Quiet hours (HH:mm, wraps correctly across midnight) and a push/email/SMS opt-in toggle live on the same page — only the push toggle does anything real this pass, see below.

**Diagnostic onboarding:** the last item on the confirmed build order and the PRD §7.1's "includes a diagnostic onboarding that establishes baseline chronotype, work hours, priorities" row. Follows the UI/UX Design Document §10 flow exactly: Welcome → Diagnostic quiz → Connect calendar → First plan (Sign up itself isn't a step in the new `/onboarding` wizard — that's the existing Clerk-hosted `/sign-up` route a person already goes through to have a session at all before a global `OnboardingGate` component, mounted once in `Providers.tsx` right alongside the existing timezone auto-sync, ever redirects them here). The quiz is 5 large single-choice-card questions, every one optional — chronotype (feeds the existing self-report enum), typical work-day start/end, quiet hours (reuses the exact same columns Smart notifications already added, so answering this question here *is* setting your notification quiet hours, not a separate thing), and "biggest source of overload right now," which is the PRD's "priorities" baseline — stored as a real AI Memory preference fact (the same `MemoryService.create` surface the `/memory` page itself uses), so it shows up there too and is in every AI prompt immediately, not a new bespoke model. Work hours (`workHoursStart`/`workHoursEnd`, another gap the approved docs never modeled — same HH:mm-on-`User` convention as quiet hours) closes a real, previously-flagged gap: `planner.service.ts` used to hard-code 7am–9pm for every account, both in the WEEK/MONTH scope's actually-enforced working-hours check and in the exact hour named in the prompt text for every scope including DAY's own "only schedule between now and X" line. It now reads your real answer for both, falling back to that exact same 7am–9pm default for anyone who skipped the question or never onboarded at all — so nothing changes for any account that predates this increment, and DAY scope's actual policy validation (which never bounded by hour at all, only by `[now, windowEnd)`) is just as unenforced-by-hour as it always was; only the *stated* cutoff in its prompt text is now per-user. The Connect-calendar step just surfaces the three existing Google/Microsoft/Apple connect cards inline (no new integration code); the First-plan step calls the same `requestReplan` mutation the daily planner already uses and shows the resulting summary as the onboarding "aha moment," with an honest empty state (not an error) for the very common case of a brand-new account having zero tasks yet.

**Goals:** closes the one real gap a post-launch feature audit found after the confirmed build order finished — PRD §7.3's "long-horizon objectives that tasks and habits ladder up to." Unusually for this project, this wasn't a backend gap: the `Goal` model, `GoalsService`/`GoalsResolver` (`goals`/`createGoal`/`updateGoal`), and `Task.goalId`/`Task.goal` have existed since the very first Tasks increment — nothing on the frontend ever reached any of it. A new **Goals** page (linked from Today, same not-a-nav-tab pattern as Focus/Reflection/Routines/Notifications) lists goals by status (Active/Completed/Abandoned tabs), lets you create one (title, optional description, optional target date), and change its status — there's deliberately no delete mutation on the backend, so "done with this" is always a status change, not data loss. The actual linkage is wired end to end, not just a management screen sitting next to an unrelated task list: Today's quick-add box now has an optional goal picker (only shown once you have at least one active goal, to avoid clutter for everyone else) that sets `goalId` on `createTask`, and any task linked to a goal shows that goal's title right on its row.

**Scheduler / time-based reminders:** the single most-repeated line in this README's own "what's not built yet" — that nothing runs on a schedule, so reminders and automatic behavior only ever fire when a person taps a button. The Architecture Document's approved design (§4.3, §4.6) puts this on Temporal (a managed durable-workflow service) for calendar sync, notification delivery, and the daily re-plan pipeline — real infrastructure that doesn't exist anywhere in this project and would be its own significant undertaking to stand up. Same "simplest correct in-process equivalent" discipline the Smart notifications increment already applied to *delivery* itself, applied here to the *triggering* half: `@nestjs/schedule`'s `@Cron` decorator runs a real, genuinely-repeating job (every 15 minutes) inside the same NestJS process, no new infrastructure or process required. `SchedulerService.checkReminders` sweeps every active account and, per person (in *their* timezone, not the server's), checks four things and fires a real notification through the existing `NotificationsService` (same quiet-hours/push-toggle/batching behavior every other notification already gets) when each is true: a morning or evening routine isn't checked off yet, in a 30-minute window starting 8am/8pm local; today's reflection hasn't been submitted yet, in a 30-minute window starting 9pm local; or a habit with a preferred time is 15–120 minutes overdue and not completed (each overdue habit tracked as its own notification, `habit_reminder:<habitId>`, so two different overdue habits on the same day don't collide into one). This closes the specific gap the README already named by name — `habit_reminder`, the evening-routine nudge, the reflection reminder — not the separate, larger "automatic re-planning" gap (event-driven, not time-driven), which was still open as of this increment — see the entry below.

**Editing a proposed AI plan:** wires up the `EDITED` status and `EDIT` decision the API Design Document §4.4 always specified but this project had left as reserved-but-unimplemented since the very first AI daily planning increment — until now, a proposed plan was strictly accept-as-is or reject-outright. Each suggested change in a `PROPOSED` plan run now carries a stable `id`, and `respondToPlanRun` accepts an optional `edits` list alongside its `decision` — one entry per change someone wants to touch, either a new `proposedStart` to move it or `remove: true` to drop it from the plan entirely, before the (possibly-modified) plan gets applied the same way `ACCEPT` always has (real `scheduledStart`/`scheduledEnd` written onto each surviving task). On Today and the weekly/monthly plan card, every suggested change now has **Edit time** (opens a plain date/time picker) and **Remove** links; once anything's been touched, the usual Accept/Reject pair is replaced by **Save & apply changes** (this is the `EDIT` decision) and **Discard edits** (clears the local, not-yet-saved edits and returns to the normal Accept/Reject view) — nothing is sent to the server until **Save & apply changes** is actually tapped, same "propose, don't auto-commit" principle the original Accept/Reject flow already followed, just extended to cover the editing itself. An edited time is re-validated with the exact same rules a freshly-generated proposal already has to pass — can't overlap a fixed calendar event, a protected habit block, or another change in the same plan, must fall within the allowed time window (and, for WEEK/MONTH scope, within working hours) — computed fresh at edit time rather than trusted from whenever the plan was first generated, since the calendar or a habit's completion state may have changed in between. An edit that fails this check isn't silently dropped or rejected outright either: the affected change just keeps its original AI-proposed time, and the plan's summary says so explicitly (`"...2 edits applied, 1 edit couldn't be applied — conflicted with..."`) — the same explainability principle the original policy layer's dropped-suggestion messaging already established. One real, deliberate scope cut: `MemoryService.refreshInterventionResponsePattern`'s automatic accept/reject-pattern learning (see the Automatic AI Memory learning entry above) still only ever looks at `ACCEPTED`/`REJECTED` runs — whether an edited-then-applied plan should count toward that pattern the same way a plain accept does is a real open question this increment doesn't attempt to answer, so an `EDITED` run is simply invisible to that signal for now, same as it would've been before this increment existed.

**Automatic AI re-planning:** the other half of "nothing runs on its own" — closes the "automatic re-planning" gap the Scheduler increment above deliberately left open, since that one was entirely time-based (reminders) and this one is event-based. A plan run has always been something you generate by tapping a button (`triggerEvent: 'manual_request'`); now completing a task or changing your calendar can also trigger a fresh one, without a tap. Under the hood: `TasksService.complete()` and `CalendarService`'s native `create`/`update`/`delete` (deliberately not the sync-writer methods a Google/Microsoft/Apple pull uses — one "Sync now" can pull in dozens of events, and firing a re-plan per synced event would be a burst of AI calls for what a person experiences as one action) each emit a plain event (`task.completed` / `calendar.changed`, via `@nestjs/event-emitter`'s `EventEmitter2`) rather than calling `PlannerService` directly — `PlannerModule` already imports both `TasksModule` and `CalendarModule` for its own use of their services, so either module importing `PlannerModule` back would be circular; an event sidesteps that the same way this project's already-established module-cycle discipline would predict. `PlannerService` listens for both and runs `maybeAutoReplan`, which only ever produces a real DAY-scope plan — same `requestReplan` machinery every other planning increment uses, just with `triggerEvent` set to `'auto_task_completed'`/`'auto_calendar_changed'` instead of the default, visible on `AiPlanRun.triggerEvent` so you can always tell an auto-triggered plan apart from one you asked for. Two deliberate guardrails keep this from being noisy: a 10-minute cooldown (checked against the most recent DAY-scope plan's `generatedAt`, whether it was auto- or manually-triggered) means completing five tasks in a minute produces one fresh plan, not five AI calls; and an account with no open tasks left, or with `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` not configured, is a silent no-op — not an error, and not a notification either, since a re-plan you didn't ask for shouldn't also interrupt you to say "nothing to do." `eventEmitter.emit` (not `emitAsync`) is deliberately fire-and-forget — completing a task or saving a calendar event never waits on, or can be slowed down or broken by, the re-plan this triggers.

**Life analytics / trend views:** closes the single most-repeated remaining "not built yet" line in this README (PRD §7.3) — no history or charting UI over any of the data this app has been logging since Signal tracking, Habits, and Routines each shipped. Unlike almost every other increment in this project, nothing new is persisted: `AnalyticsService.getSummary` computes everything fresh, on read, from tables that already exist (`mood_entries`, `energy_entries`, `sleep_entries`, `habit_logs`, `routine_logs`) — a new `AnalyticsModule` reads through `SignalsService`/`HabitsService`/`RoutinesService` rather than owning any storage of its own. A new **Insights** page (linked from Today, same not-a-nav-tab pattern as Goals/Focus/Reflection/Routines/Notifications), with a 2-week/30-day/90-day window toggle, shows: a mood-and-energy trend chart (both 1-5, one averaged point per day — more than one check-in the same day is averaged, not just the latest kept); a sleep-duration trend chart; and, closing the two "no UI surfaces it yet" gaps this README has flagged since the Scheduler increment, a real current-streak and completion-rate card for every active habit and for each routine (morning/evening) you've set up. Both streak/consistency calculations are honest about what they can't know: a habit or routine's own `createdAt` clamps the window so a habit you created three days ago is never judged for the four days before it existed, and only a habit's actually-*due* days (via the existing `isDueOn` rrule check) count toward its streak — a day it was never due on is skipped, not counted as a miss. One deliberate, documented approximation: a routine's completion for any past day is judged against its *current* checklist length (there's no per-day historical snapshot of what the checklist looked like then, since `setRoutine` has always been a full-replace), most visible right after you change how many steps a routine has. Charts are a small hand-rolled inline SVG component, not a new charting-library dependency — the same "simple hand-rolled client over a library for a narrow need" judgment call the Apple (CalDAV) sync increment's own ICS parser already made.

**Free-form plan editing:** closes the "editing is per-change, not free-form" half of the gap the Editing a proposed AI plan increment left open (the other half — editing a task's title/priority/etc. from inside the review card — is still open, see below). A `PROPOSED` (and now `EDIT`-time) plan review can add a task the AI never proposed at all, not just move or drop what it did suggest: `respondToPlanRun` accepts a new optional `adds` list alongside `edits`, one entry per task-and-time someone picks themselves. On Today and the weekly/monthly plan card, an **+ Add a task** control (only shown when at least one of your open tasks isn't already on this plan) lets you pick from a dropdown of your remaining open tasks and a plain date/time picker; added tasks show up in the review list labeled "(added)" and count toward the same **Save & apply changes** / **Discard edits** flow every other edit already uses — nothing real happens until Save is tapped, same principle as always. A manually-added task is re-validated with the exact same rules a freshly-generated proposal or a moved edit already has to pass (no overlap with a fixed calendar event, a protected habit block, or another change already on the plan; must fall within the allowed time window and, for WEEK/MONTH, within working hours), computed fresh against whatever else survived the same response's edits and removals first — and a task that's already carrying a change on this same plan can't be added a second time. An add that fails validation is simply skipped, not silently ignored: the plan's summary says so explicitly (`"...1 task added; 1 added task couldn't be placed — conflicted with..."`), same explainability principle every other part of this feature already follows.

Found and fixed one real, separate pre-existing bug while building this: `LATEST_PLAN_RUN_QUERY` (the query the weekly/monthly plan card uses) never actually asked for `changes { id }` — every change on a WEEK/MONTH plan came back with `id: undefined`, so every row's local edit state collided under the same key, and `changeId: "undefined"` sent to the server never matched anything. In practice, no WEEK/MONTH edit or remove could ever actually apply, even though the DAY-scope Editing increment worked correctly (its query already asked for `id`). Fixed by adding the missing field; the existing "Editing a proposed AI plan" e2e coverage only ever exercised DAY scope, so this had gone uncaught until now.

**Editing a task's own details in-plan:** closes the other half of the "editing is per-change, not free-form" gap the Editing a proposed AI plan increment left open (the first half — adding a task the AI never proposed — was closed by Free-form plan editing above). Each change row on both the daily and weekly/monthly plan cards now has an **Edit task** control alongside **Edit time** and **Remove**, opening a small inline form for that task's title, priority, and estimated duration. Unlike a time edit, this writes the task immediately via the already-real (but, until now, never actually wired up on the frontend) `updateTask` mutation — the task's own details aren't part of the "propose, then apply" plan flow, so there's no reason to hold them back until Save. The one place a task-detail edit *does* interact with the plan is duration: a task's duration is exactly what determines a change's `proposedEnd`, so editing it while its plan is still under review needed a real fix, not just a UI — `respondToPlanRun`'s `EDIT` decision now re-derives every surviving change's end time from that task's *current* duration (fetched fresh, not trusted from generation time) and re-validates it against everything else already on the plan, every time you tap **Save & apply changes** — previously, an untouched change was never re-checked at all once it survived generation, since nothing could change about it in between; now something can. A duration edit that grows a task's slot into a genuine new conflict with another change gets dropped, not silently applied over something else, with the summary saying so explicitly (`"...1 change dropped — a task's edited duration no longer fits without overlapping something else on the plan"`) — same explainability principle every other part of this plan-review feature already follows. Editing a task's details always marks its row as "touched" (so the footer switches from Accept/Reject to Save & apply changes/Discard edits, even if you didn't also move its time), which is what actually triggers this re-validation — a plain **Accept** (no edits of any kind) still applies the plan exactly as generated, unaffected, a deliberate scope cut to keep that already-well-tested path untouched.

**Tasks list/edit screen:** closes the longest-standing real gap flagged in this README — `updateTask`, `cancelTask`, and `createTag` have all been genuine, working, e2e-tested mutations since the very first Tasks increment, but there was never a dedicated screen to use any of them beyond the narrow, plan-scoped "Edit task" control above. A new **Tasks** page (linked from Today, same not-a-nav-tab pattern as Goals/Focus/Reflection/Routines/Notifications/Insights) lists your tasks under an Open/Cancelled tab pair — Completed tasks intentionally stay on the existing `/more` page rather than being duplicated here — and each row expands into a full editor: title, description, priority, due date, estimated duration, a goal-link dropdown (every goal regardless of status, not just active ones, so you can see and clear a link to a goal you've since finished or abandoned), and a plain comma-separated tags field. Tags resolve through the same upsert-by-name `createTag` the very first Tasks increment already built — typing a tag that already exists reuses it instead of creating a duplicate, so this needed no new "list my tags" query to check against first. One plain, unfiltered query (the 100 most-recently-created tasks across every status, split into tabs client-side) rather than a separate cursor per tab — the simplest version that avoids juggling multiple pagination cursors at once, same "simple cap first" precedent `TodayPlan.tasks`'s own 50-task cap already set.

Found two real, narrow gaps in existing (but until now, never actually exercised) backend behavior while wiring this up: `updateTask`'s `goalId: null` (to unlink a goal) and `tagIds: []` (to clear every tag) had been structurally supported by `TasksService.update`'s Prisma call since the original Tasks increment, but no e2e test and no frontend caller had ever actually sent either shape before — both now have regression coverage (see below), confirming they work as expected rather than just assuming so.

**PWA + offline support:** closes the platform gap the PRD's §10/§14 call out as part of the MVP definition itself, not a later polish pass — "PWA on web" and an explicit offline requirement ("viewing today's plan, adding/completing tasks, and journaling must work with no connection and sync on reconnect"), neither of which existed before this increment; the app was a normal web app with no manifest, no service worker, and no offline data story at all. Three real, separate pieces, deliberately hand-rolled rather than reaching for a bundler plugin (`next-pwa`/Workbox) — same "small purpose-built solution over a library for a narrow need" judgment call this project already made for its Apple CalDAV/ICS parser and its Insights charts:

1. **Installable.** A real `manifest.webmanifest` (name, generated icon set at 192/512px plus a maskable variant, `display: standalone`, theme color pulled from the same design tokens `tailwind.config.ts` already defines) wired into the root layout's metadata — a browser can now genuinely offer "Add to Home Screen"/"Install," which did nothing before since there was no manifest for it to find.
2. **The app shell loads offline.** A hand-rolled `public/sw.js` (no precache manifest of Next's hashed asset filenames, since there's no bundler plugin generating one) uses a stale-while-revalidate runtime cache: the first online visit to a route caches it, so a later fully-offline visit to that same route still loads. Deliberately never intercepts the GraphQL POST endpoint — that's not what it's for (see next point).
3. **Data and the three named write actions work offline.** `apollo3-cache-persist` persists the normalized Apollo cache to `localStorage`, so whatever's already been fetched (Today's plan, tasks, journal entries — exactly the views the PRD names) survives a full offline reload, not just a client-side route change in one still-open tab. For the three actions the PRD specifically calls out — adding a task, completing a task, and writing a journal entry — a small hand-written offline queue (`lib/offlineQueue.ts`) takes over when `navigator.onLine` is false (or a request that looked fine fails with a real network error): it applies a narrow, purpose-built optimistic patch directly to the relevant cached query so the change appears to take effect immediately, and queues the real mutation in `localStorage`. A `SyncManager` component (mounted globally, same invisible shape as `TimezoneSync`) replays the queue in order the moment the browser's `online` event fires (or on next app load, in case the tab closed while still offline), refetching once at the end to reconcile every optimistic patch with real server data. A queued item that comes back genuinely rejected (not just "still offline") is removed rather than retried forever and recorded as a visible, dismissible error via a new `OfflineSyncBanner` on Today and Journal — same "never fail silently" discipline the routines-saving bug fix earlier in this README already established, just applied to a new code path from the start instead of needing a post-ship fix.

Deliberately scoped to exactly the three actions the PRD names, not a generic "everything works offline" framework — editing a task's full details, plan generation/response, chat, and every other mutation in the app still require a live connection, same as before this increment. This is a real, honest v1 of the offline requirement, not a full implementation of every possible offline interaction.

**Real notification delivery (web push + email):** closes the biggest remaining piece of the PRD §7.1 "Smart notifications" P0 row — until this increment, "delivery" meant nothing more than a notification becoming visible the next time you happened to open `/notifications` or glance at the unread count on Today; there was no real push, email, or SMS behind either opt-in toggle. Web Push (RFC 8030 + VAPID) rather than a native FCM/APNs SDK — the one push mechanism a plain browser-based PWA can use with no per-platform app-store account, matching this app's actual shipped platform (see the PWA + offline support entry above). A new `PushSubscription` table holds one row per browser/device; a **"Turn on browser notifications"** button on `/notifications` walks through the real permission-request → subscribe → `registerPushSubscription` flow, and `sw.js` now has real `push`/`notificationclick` handlers so a notification shows up as an actual OS-level notification (even with the tab closed) and focuses/opens the right page on click. Email delivery goes through Resend's plain REST API via `fetch` (no SDK dependency added — one endpoint, one header, one JSON body), gated behind the already-existing `emailNotificationsEnabled` preference, which finally does something now. Delivery is attempted automatically from `NotificationsService.create()` the moment a notification isn't deferred by quiet hours, and a new 15-minute sweep (`NotificationsService.deliverDueNotifications`, same `@Cron` pattern the Scheduler/reminders increment already established) catches anything that *was* quiet-hours-deferred once its time actually arrives. A new `deliveredAt` column tracks "a real delivery attempt has been made" — deliberately separate from `status`/`sentAt`, which still track the older "has this become visible in the in-app list" concept — so the sweep never re-sends the same row forever. Both channels are best-effort by design: a dead push subscription is pruned automatically (the push service itself says so via a 404/410), a down email provider just logs a warning, and neither can ever block or break the real action that triggered the notification in the first place. The in-app `/notifications` list remains the permanent fallback of last resort no matter what happens with push or email.

**Real browser-automation test suite (Playwright):** closes this project's single biggest testing gap — every increment since PWA + offline support has had at least one piece of real behavior (service worker registration, offline mode, an install prompt, a push permission grant) that the backend's supertest-based e2e harness structurally cannot exercise, since that harness only ever talks to the backend over HTTP and has no browser in it at all. A new `apps/e2e` workspace (`@ailm/e2e`) adds `@playwright/test` and nine tests across five spec files, run against a real Chromium instance driving the actual running frontend: **Today** loads and shows a greeting; **Tasks** — adding one from Today, editing every field through the `/tasks` screen's full editor, and completing one through the actual-duration prompt; **Journal** — writing and editing an entry; **PWA** — the manifest is linked and the service worker reaches `activated`; **offline app shell** — a page visited online still loads with the browser context set fully offline; **the offline mutation queue** — adding a task while offline shows it immediately, shows the "waiting to sync" banner, and the change is confirmed to have really reached the server (not just the local cache) once back online; and **push notifications** — granting permission and completing a real `PushManager.subscribe()`/`registerPushSubscription` round trip, then unsubscribing. Deliberately does not use Playwright's `webServer` auto-start option — the backend needs a real Postgres instance already running, which no test-runner config can stand up on its own — so it assumes both dev servers are already running, exactly like every other "verify yourself" section in this README already does. A `global-setup.ts` completes onboarding for the shared dev-auth account first (over the GraphQL API directly, not through the UI) if it hasn't been already, so `OnboardingGate` never silently redirects every single spec to `/onboarding` before it gets to test anything.

**Accessibility (WCAG AA) pass:** the app had zero accessibility work behind it before this increment — no semantic headings anywhere (every page title and section label was a styled `<p>`), several color-contrast failures, several unlabeled form inputs, a `<div>` standing in for the bottom navigation's `<nav>` landmark, and no skip-to-content link. All real, all fixed, not just flagged:

1. **Color contrast**, computed for real (not eyeballed) against every text/background pair in `tailwind.config.ts`: `ai-accent` (goal-title text, tag labels) measured 4.38:1/4.09:1 on light backgrounds — under the 4.5:1 AA minimum for normal text — darkened to `#5E3FD0` (6.78:1/6.34:1). `danger` (error messages, the "Urgent" badge) never had its own dark-mode value at all, so every error message rendered at 4.06:1/3.75:1 in dark mode — added a real `dark` value (`#F87171`, 7.09:1/6.55:1) and paired every `text-danger` usage in the app with `dark:text-danger-dark` to actually use it. `border` failed WCAG 1.4.11's 3:1 non-text-contrast minimum outright (1.26:1/1.31:1 — every input, card, and button edge in the app was close to invisible) — darkened to `#8C8C99`/`#6A6A7A` (3.32:1/3.69:1+), a real, visible change to the whole app's look, made after checking with you first since it's a genuine visual tradeoff, not a bug fix with only one right answer.
2. **Heading structure**, added everywhere it didn't exist: every page's title (`Today`, `Tasks`, `Journal`, and so on) is now a real `<h1>`, and major section labels inside pages (`Preferences`, `Proposed plan`, `Longer-range plan`, the four Insights chart titles, each calendar provider's connected-state title) are now `<h2>` — a screen reader's "jump by heading" navigation actually works on this app for the first time.
3. **Form labels**, added to every input/textarea/select that only had a placeholder or an unassociated visible label text before — over 30 across the app (Quick Add's title/duration fields, the full Tasks editor's seven fields, the in-plan task/time editors, Journal's composer and edit box, Goals, Habits, Reflection's three questions, Chat, the calendar-connect forms, and more) — via `aria-label` where there's no visible label text in the design, or a real `htmlFor`/`id` pair where visible label text already existed but wasn't actually wired up to its input (Reflection's three questions looked labeled but weren't).
4. **Toggle-button state**, via `aria-pressed`: Week/Month, the Insights time-window picker, Open/Cancelled tabs, habit frequency, day-of-week pickers, and the onboarding quiz's choice cards now announce their selected state, not just show it visually.
5. **Landmarks and a skip link**: `BottomNav` is a real `<nav aria-label="Primary">` now (was a plain `<div>`), its active tab carries `aria-current="page"`, and every page's `<main>` now carries `id="main-content"` so a new, always-present "Skip to main content" link (visually hidden until focused, mounted once in `Providers.tsx`) actually has somewhere to jump to.
6. **Live regions**, so a screen reader actually announces things that appear without a page navigation: every dynamically-appearing error message in the app (~30 of them) now carries `role="alert"`, and the offline-queue's "N changes waiting to sync" strip carries `role="status"`.
7. **Focus visibility**, audited rather than assumed: every place in the app that suppresses the browser's default focus outline (`focus:outline-none`) already, correctly, replaces it with a real visible ring (`focus:ring-2 focus:ring-accent`) — confirmed by checking every occurrence, not just spot-checking a few.
8. **A real, repeatable regression check**: `apps/e2e/tests/accessibility.spec.ts` runs axe-core (the same engine behind Chrome DevTools' own Accessibility panel) against every major page's live, rendered DOM, scoped to WCAG 2.0/2.1 A and AA rule tags specifically, failing on any critical or serious violation — so a future change that quietly drops a label or reintroduces a contrast failure gets caught automatically instead of waiting for the next manual pass.

**Extended Playwright coverage:** the original Playwright increment covered 5 core journeys (Today, Tasks, Journal, PWA/offline, push notifications); this pass adds 10 more spec files, one per remaining major screen that previously had zero browser-level interaction coverage: **Goals** (create a goal, mark it complete, confirm it moves off the Active tab); **Habits** (create a daily habit and a specific-days habit, confirm both list correctly); **Calendar** (quick-add a native event, confirm it survives a reload); **Focus sessions** (start a session, confirm the countdown and its "of N min planned" label render, complete it, confirm it clears back to the start screen and shows as Done in Recent sessions); **daily check-in and reflection** (log mood/energy/sleep on Today and confirm the picks persist past a reload; submit the three-question reflection on `/reflection` and confirm all three answers render back); **Routines** (add a step to the morning routine, confirm it shows up on Today's checklist, check it off there); **Chat** (send a message, confirm a real AI reply comes back and a conversation is created); **AI Memory** (add a fact, edit it in place); **Insights** (confirm the default 30-day window is really selected via `aria-pressed`, switch to 2 weeks, confirm no error appears); and **the AI plan review flow** (generate a real daily plan, confirm the proposed changes render, accept it, confirm the card falls back to its "plan was applied" state). Between this and the original 5, the suite now spans every major reachable screen in the app except the AI recommendations card specifically (already exercised indirectly, since Today's other specs load the same page) and Onboarding (global-setup already completes it directly over GraphQL, so there's no UI flow left to click through). The AI plan review and Chat specs both make a real, un-mocked call to the AI provider — deliberately, since the whole point of an end-to-end suite is confirming the real integration works, not a mocked stand-in for it — so they're written with generous timeouts (up to 60 seconds) rather than assuming a fast, canned reply.

**Subtask UI:** `Task.subtasks`/`parentTaskId` have been real, working Prisma columns and a real GraphQL field (`CreateTaskInput.parentTaskId`, `Task.subtasks`) since the very first Tasks increment — the backend has quietly supported this the entire time (`TasksService`'s `TASK_INCLUDE` already eager-loads one level of subtasks, every top-level list query already filters `parentTaskId: null` so a subtask never double-counts as its own row), but no screen anywhere in the app ever surfaced them until now. A new `SubtaskList` component, wired into the Tasks screen's `TaskEditRow`, lets you add a subtask under any task, check it off, uncheck it, and remove it — a real "N/M done" count included. Deliberately simpler than a top-level task's own row in two specific ways: completing a subtask skips the "how long did this actually take?" prompt (that flow exists to protect the AI's `task_duration_accuracy` learning signal specifically — a subtask is a lightweight checklist item, not something that signal needs to track), and "Remove" is really `cancelTask` under a friendlier label — the same "no hard delete for a task, ever" rule every other task action in this app already follows, so a removed subtask can't be un-removed from the UI (it's just filtered out of view, same as a cancelled top-level task never shows on the Active tab). Today's own task rows stay read-only about this on purpose — a small "N/M subtasks" badge, not the full add/check/remove list, keeping that screen's compact, at-a-glance design intact; the real management UI lives on `/tasks` where a task already gets its full editor.

**Tasks pagination:** closes the Tasks screen's own hardest cap — its `TasksList` query used to fetch one plain, unfiltered `first: 100` page and filter Open vs. Cancelled entirely client-side, which meant an account with more than 100 tasks across every status (including COMPLETED ones this screen never even shows) would silently stop seeing its oldest tasks with no way to reach them. Replaced with two real, independently cursor-paginated queries, one per tab (`OPEN_TASKS_QUERY`, `CANCELLED_TASKS_QUERY`), each asking the server for only the statuses that tab actually needs and paging 20 at a time with a "Load more" button — the exact same pattern the completed-tasks view on `/more` already established, just applied here too. The one real backend change this required: `tasks.resolver.ts`'s connection query gained a new `statuses: [TaskStatus!]` list arg alongside its older singular `status` (kept, not replaced — `COMPLETED_TASKS_QUERY` still uses the singular form), since the Open tab needs `PENDING` and `IN_PROGRESS` together and the schema had no way to ask for more than one status at once before this. There's now no hard ceiling on how many tasks an account can have and still reach every one of them from this screen.

**Push calendar edits back to Google/Microsoft:** closes the last named gap in this project's two-way calendar sync story — deletes have pushed back to both providers since the earlier two-way-sync increments, but editing a synced event's title or time from this app never reached Google or Outlook, and there was no edit UI for *any* calendar event, native or synced, at all until now. `CalendarEventRow` gets a real inline editor (title, start time, end time — matching the Tasks screen's own edit-row pattern), and `updateCalendarEvent` — a mutation that's existed since the very first Calendar increment but was purely local until now — pushes the same edit to the real provider first for a Google- or Microsoft-sourced event, and only writes the local row once that succeeds. Both `GoogleCalendarClient`/`MicrosoftCalendarClient` gained a real `updateEvent` (a PATCH, sending only the fields that actually changed — never a field this app doesn't itself track, like a synced event's own description) and both write services gained an `updateRemoteEvent` that mirrors their existing `deleteRemoteEvent`'s exact refresh-and-retry shape, including `RECONNECT_REQUIRED` for an account connected before either provider's write scope widened. Apple isn't covered — CalDAV write support doesn't exist at all yet (see below) — so an Apple-sourced event's edit stays local-only, same as before this increment.

**SMS delivery:** closes the last of the three notification channels the PRD names — the SMS opt-in toggle on `/notifications` has existed since the Smart notifications increment but always did nothing, since there was nowhere on the `User` model to even store a phone number, and no service to actually send one. Both are real now: a new `phoneNumber` column (E.164 format, validated server-side in `UpdateNotificationPreferencesInput` and loosely pre-checked client-side before that), and a new `SmsService` — plain `fetch` against Twilio's REST API (Basic Auth, one form-encoded body) rather than the `twilio` npm SDK, the exact same "a few lines of fetch beats a dependency for a narrow need" call `EmailService` already made for Resend. Wired into `NotificationsService.attemptDelivery` alongside push and email — attempted whenever `smsNotificationsEnabled` is on *and* a phone number is actually on file, best-effort and never blocking whatever real action triggered the notification, same delivery discipline every other channel already follows. SMS has no separate subject line the way email does, so a notification's title and body are combined into one plain message. `/notifications` gets a real phone number field (E.164, with inline format help text) right next to the now-unlabeled "not connected yet" SMS checkbox, which just says "SMS" now like the other two channels.

**Extending auto-replanning to WEEK/MONTH scope:** closes the other half of the Automatic AI re-planning increment's own documented gap — completing a task or changing a native calendar event has triggered a real DAY-scope re-plan since that increment shipped, but WEEK and MONTH plans stayed manual-button-only until now. `PlannerService.maybeAutoReplan` (the shared gate both `task.completed` and `calendar.changed` already go through) now loops over all three scopes on every trigger instead of hardcoding `DAY`, with each scope gated by its own independent cooldown and its own independent `generatedAt` check — DAY keeps its original tight 10-minute window, but WEEK gets 3 hours and MONTH gets 12, since a single task completing is a much weaker signal that a whole week or month needs rethinking than that today does, and a WEEK/MONTH regeneration is a meaningfully heavier AI call to be firing off on every task completion the way DAY's short cooldown reasonably allows. Each scope is also wrapped in its own try/catch, so one scope legitimately having nothing to plan (a real, silent, non-error outcome — see `NOTHING_TO_PLAN`) can never stop the other two from still being attempted. No new triggers were added — this increment is specifically about *which scopes* the two existing triggers reach, not adding a third trigger; see below for what's still open there. `WeeklyPlanCard` needed no changes at all: it already queries `latestPlanRun` by its own scope independently of what triggered the plan it finds, so an auto-generated WEEK or MONTH plan shows up there exactly the same way a manually-requested one always has.

**Cross-metric correlation in Insights:** closes the "every chart on the page stands alone" gap the Life analytics increment's own README entry named. `AnalyticsService.getSummary` already built `dailyMoodEnergy`/`dailySleep` as two day-aligned series (same `dates` backbone, one entry per day, index-for-index comparable) — this increment adds a real Pearson correlation coefficient computed over each of six candidate metric pairs (sleep duration vs. mood, sleep duration vs. energy, sleep quality vs. mood, sleep quality vs. energy, and a new daily habit-completion-rate series — the fraction of that day's due habits actually completed, aggregated across every habit — vs. both mood and energy), using only the days where *both* metrics in a pair have a real logged value, never a fabricated one. A pair only gets reported at all once it clears two bars: at least 5 day-pairs of real data (`MIN_CORRELATION_SAMPLE_SIZE`), and an absolute coefficient of at least 0.3 (`MIN_CORRELATION_ABS_R`) — a deliberately loose bar for a personal-reflection tool, not a research claim, chosen specifically so a thin or near-zero relationship never surfaces as a false "insight." The Pearson formula itself (`computeCorrelation` in `analytics.service.ts`) was independently verified against `numpy.corrcoef` on five test vectors — including a zero-variance series, which correctly returns `null` rather than a misleading `0` — before being trusted in the real code (see "What's verified" below for the exact numbers). Each qualifying pair comes back as a new `MetricCorrelation` GraphQL type (`metricALabel`, `metricBLabel`, `coefficient`, `sampleSize`, a plain-English `description`) on a new `correlations` field on `AnalyticsSummary`, phrased as a tendency ("higher sleep duration tends to come with higher mood in this window") rather than a causal claim — the math genuinely can't distinguish "short sleep causes a low mood" from "a low mood causes bad sleep" from "something else is behind both," and the wording says so. `/analytics` gets a new "Patterns worth noting" card, sorted strongest-first, that — unlike the habit-streak and routine-consistency cards above it — always renders, with an honest "not enough data yet" empty state rather than disappearing entirely when nothing qualifies.

**Lagged correlation (yesterday's X vs. today's Y):** closes the specific gap the Cross-metric correlation increment's own "What's not built yet" entry named — every correlation check so far paired a metric with another metric *on the same day*, never asking whether yesterday's value predicts today's. Each of the same six base pairs is now checked twice: once same-day (`lagDays: 0`, unchanged from before), and once with metric A shifted one day earlier than metric B (`lagDays: 1`) — "does yesterday's sleep duration/quality/habit-completion predict today's mood/energy," computed by a new `shiftForLag` helper that realigns the two day-indexed series before handing them to the same, already-verified `computeCorrelation`. Only the forward direction (A leading B) is checked, not the reverse (mood predicting tomorrow's sleep) — a different hypothesis, deliberately out of scope to keep six relationships from turning into twelve different *questions* rather than just twelve candidate checks of the same six. A same-day and a one-day-lag result for the same metric pair are reported completely independently — either, both, or neither can appear, and seeing only one is itself informative (a relationship that's real only one day out, say, or one that fades by the next day). `MetricCorrelation` gained a `lagDays` field, and `describeCorrelation`'s phrasing changes accordingly ("...one day tends to come with... the next day..." instead of "...tends to come with... in this window..."), with the lagged version's sample-size wording changed from "days" to "one-day-apart pairs" to avoid implying it's counting the same thing as the same-day version sitting right next to it. `/analytics`'s "Patterns worth noting" card shows a small text "Next-day" badge (not color-only) next to any lagged entry so it's never mistaken for the same-day version of the same pair. The new formula path (`shiftForLag` + the existing `computeCorrelation`) was independently re-verified against `numpy.corrcoef` with a synthetic dataset built so metric A leads metric B by exactly one day — the lag-1 check correctly found `r = 1.0`, while the same-day check on the identical raw data found something else entirely (`r ≈ -0.61`), concretely proving the two checks are answering genuinely different questions, not just relabeling the same number (see "What's verified" below).

**New auto-replanning triggers:** closes the specific gap the Extending auto-replanning to WEEK/MONTH scope increment's own README entry named — `task.completed` and `calendar.changed` were the only two signals that could ever trigger an automatic re-plan; three more real, plausible ones are wired in now: completing a habit (`HabitsService.completeLog`), logging a mood *or* energy check-in (`SignalsService.logMood`/`logEnergy`, sharing one `checkin.logged` event since either is the same kind of "something about today just changed" signal for this purpose), and fully finishing a routine (`RoutinesService.setTodayCompletion`, gated on every current checklist step actually being complete — checking off one of several steps doesn't count, matching this increment's own "a routine finished" framing). Each new source emits its own plain event (`habit.completed`, `checkin.logged`, `routine.completed`) rather than calling `PlannerService` directly — the same decoupling `task.completed`/`calendar.changed` already established, necessary for `HabitsService`/`SignalsService` specifically since `PlannerModule` already imports both of their modules (a direct call back would be circular), and used for `RoutinesService` too for consistency even though `PlannerModule` doesn't import `RoutinesModule` today. All three route through the exact same `maybeAutoReplan` gate the original two triggers use — same per-scope cooldowns (10 min / 3 hr / 12 hr), same silent no-op when there's nothing to plan — so this increment added zero new re-planning logic, purely new trigger wiring. Sleep logging deliberately does *not* trigger a re-plan (see the code comment on `logSleep`) — it's usually logged about *last* night, a materially weaker "today's plan should change" signal than a fresh mood/energy reading.

**Further auto-replanning triggers:** closes the three remaining plausible signals the New auto-replanning triggers increment's own README entry named — writing a journal entry (`JournalService.create`, new entries only, not edits), completing a focus session (`FocusService.complete`, a genuine completion only — `cancel` never fires this), and submitting a daily reflection (`ReflectionService.submit`, including a same-day correction/resubmit, same "don't special-case a re-completion" reasoning `HabitsService.completeLog`'s trigger already uses) each now emit their own event (`journal.entryCreated`, `focusSession.completed`, `reflection.submitted`) straight into the same `maybeAutoReplan` gate every other trigger already uses. Unlike `HabitsService`/`SignalsService`, `PlannerModule` doesn't actually import `JournalModule`/`FocusModule`/`ReflectionModule` today, so there's no real circular-import risk forcing the event-based approach for these three specifically — it's used anyway, purely for consistency, so every trigger source in this app looks and behaves the same way rather than having three of them be a special direct-call case. With this increment, every screen in the app that logs something meaningful about how the day's actually going now has a real path to triggering a fresh plan.

**Insights: task/focus-session/journal trends:** closes the last of the three data sources the Life analytics increment's own README entry named as missing — task completion, focus-session consistency, and journal activity now each get a real chart on `/analytics`, computed the same way every other Insights series is: fresh, on read, from tables that already exist, with no new persistence at all. `TasksService.listCompletedInRange`, `FocusService.listCompletedInRange`, and `JournalService.listCreatedInRange` are three new lightweight, unhydrated queries (mirroring `HabitsService`/`RoutinesService`'s own `listRawForAnalytics`) that `AnalyticsService` buckets into the same per-day `dates` backbone every other series already uses — `AnalyticsModule` now imports `TasksModule`/`FocusModule`/`JournalModule` purely to read from them, no cycle risk since nothing imports `AnalyticsModule` back.
