One real design difference from the mood/energy/sleep series: a day with zero task completions, zero focused minutes, or zero journal entries is a genuine, known zero here, not the "nothing was logged, so we don't know" `null` those check-in-based series use — `DailyTaskCompletion.completedCount`, `DailyFocusMinutes.completedMinutes`/`completedSessions`, and `DailyJournalActivity.entryCount` are all plain non-nullable `Int`s for exactly that reason. Focus sessions also get a `FocusSessionConsistency` summary — the same current-streak/completion-rate shape as `HabitStreak`/`RoutineConsistency`, but a single object rather than an array (there's only one "focus sessions" entity, not one per habit or routine type) — using real elapsed minutes (`endedAt - startedAt`) rather than merely the *planned* duration, and counting only genuinely `COMPLETED` sessions, never a cancelled one. `/analytics` gets three new cards ("Tasks completed," "Focus sessions," "Journal activity"), each with its own honest empty state, plus the new streak card sitting alongside Habit streaks/Routine consistency.

**Wiring Insights' newest trends into the correlation engine:** closes the gap this increment's own README entry above named as a deliberate scope cut — tasks completed, focused minutes, and journal entries are now three more candidate metrics in the cross-metric correlation engine, checked against both mood and energy (six more candidate pairs, same `MIN_CORRELATION_SAMPLE_SIZE`/`MIN_CORRELATION_ABS_R` bars, same same-day-and-one-day-lag treatment every existing pair already gets). No new correlation logic was needed at all — `computeCorrelation`/`shiftForLag`/`describeCorrelation` are exactly the same functions the sleep/habit pairs already use; this was purely adding three more series to `basePairs`. Worth noting these three series are always real numbers, never `undefined` the way sleep/habit-completion can be on a day with nothing logged (see the Insights: task/focus-session/journal trends entry above) — every day genuinely participates in these particular pairs, which is exactly why they can reach the full `windowDays` sample size a same-day sleep/mood pair sometimes can't.

**Visible settings screen:** closes the timezone auto-sync increment's own "no settings screen" gap and the Diagnostic onboarding increment's "onboarding answers can't be edited afterward" gap in one pass — both named the same underlying hole, that every onboarding-recorded answer that matters *ongoing* (timezone, chronotype, work hours) had a real column and, for timezone/chronotype, a real pre-existing `updateProfile` mutation, but genuinely no UI to reach any of it after the one-shot onboarding quiz. All three now live on a new `/settings` page (reachable the same "not a bottom-nav tab, a link from Today" way every screen since Focus sessions has been), reusing `updateProfile` rather than adding a parallel mutation. Work hours (`workHoursStart`/`workHoursEnd`) needed a real, if small, backend extension — `UpdateProfileInput` never accepted them before this, since the only place that ever wrote them was `completeOnboarding`'s one-shot mutation; they're now updatable the same way chronotype already was, with the exact same HH:mm validation `CompleteOnboardingInput`'s own copies of these fields already use. Timezone needed one genuinely new piece of backend state: a `timezoneManual` boolean column. Without it, `TimezoneSync.tsx`'s existing silent browser-detection write would immediately clobber a manual choice made from Settings the very next time any page loaded — `timezoneManual: true` tells that component to back off, and Settings' own "use browser-detected automatically" action is the one thing that clears it back to `false`, deliberately keeping "hand control back to the automatic system" as explicit an action as "take control away from it" was. The "biggest source of overload" onboarding answer isn't on this screen — it was never a distinct column to begin with (see `OnboardingService.complete`); it's a plain AI Memory fact, already editable on `/memory`.

**Broader account settings:** closes the Visible settings screen entry's own "still just timezone/chronotype/work hours" gap with three real additions — `displayName` editing (a "Name" section on `/settings`, wired into the same `updateProfile` mutation everything else on that page already uses — `UpdateProfileInput` and `UsersService.updateProfile` already accepted `displayName`, it was purely a missing input), a read-only "Account" card showing your email and your `Subscription` (`tier`/`status` — a real row that's existed on every account since the very first profile increment, created at signup by `UsersService.getOrCreateFromAuth`, but never once surfaced in the UI before now), and a real `deleteAccount` mutation with a "Danger zone" section gated behind typing `DELETE` to confirm. The account-deletion piece surfaced a real gap while building it, worth explaining if asked "does this really delete everything": `User` only ever declared an actual Prisma relation (a real foreign key, with `onDelete: Cascade`) to three tables — `Subscription`, `Notification`, `PushSubscription`. Every other user-owned table in this schema — tasks, goals, habits, calendar events/accounts, mood/energy/sleep entries, AI plan runs, AI recommendation runs, AI conversations, AI memory facts, journal entries, daily reflections, routines, focus sessions, tags — only ever had a plain `userId` column with no declared relation at all, so simply deleting the `users` row would have silently orphaned every one of those rows rather than removing them. `UsersService.deleteAccount` fixes this for real: one Prisma `$transaction` that explicitly `deleteMany`s each of those 17 tables by `userId`, then deletes the `users` row itself (whose own real cascades clean up the three declared-relation tables for free) — atomic, so a mid-way failure can't leave a half-deleted account behind. `User.deletedAt` already existed as an unused soft-delete column (already respected by `SchedulerService`'s own user-selection query, just never set by anything) — deliberately not reused here: soft-deleting only the `users` row while leaving all 17 of those other tables fully intact would be a worse half-measure than either a real hard delete or a real soft-delete-everywhere design, and this is the first mutation in the app's history that deletes a user at all. After a successful delete, the same identity gets a completely fresh, empty account the very next time it's seen — there's no unique-constraint conflict to worry about, since the old row is genuinely gone, not hidden. The frontend clears its local Apollo cache, ends the real Clerk session if one exists (`AUTH_MODE=dev` has none — see the new `registerClerkSignOut`/`runClerkSignOutIfAvailable` in `apollo-client.ts`, mirroring the existing `registerClerkTokenGetter` bridge pattern), and redirects — `/sign-in` under real Clerk auth, `/today` under `AUTH_MODE=dev` (which has no Clerk-hosted sign-in page to send anyone to at all, since it never mounts `<ClerkProvider>`).

**Multi-day / reverse-direction lag correlation:** closes both remaining gaps the Lagged correlation increment's own "What's not built yet" entry named together — a lag longer than one day, and the reverse direction (does today's B predict tomorrow's A, not just A predicting B). `LAG_DAYS_OPTIONS` widens from `[0, 1]` to `[0, 1, 2, 3]`, so each of the 12 base pairs is now also checked at a two- and three-day lag ("does two nights of bad sleep predict a mood dip two days later"), with no special-casing needed for the longer lags shrinking the sample — `MIN_CORRELATION_SAMPLE_SIZE` already excludes a 3-day lag on a short window on its own (a 7-day window only leaves 4 paired days at lag 3, one short of the bar). Reverse direction needed zero new `MetricCorrelation` fields at all: for every lag greater than zero, the exact same pair is evaluated a second time with metric A and B swapped (via a new small `evaluatePair` helper, factored out of what was previously one inline block in the correlation loop) — the resulting entry's own `metricALabel`/`metricBLabel` come back swapped too, so "higher mood tends to come with lower sleep duration the next day" is a real, independently-labeled, independently-qualifying entry, not a relabeling of the forward one sitting next to it. Same-day (`lagDays: 0`) is deliberately never checked in reverse — a same-day Pearson coefficient is exactly symmetric (correlating A with B gives the identical number as correlating B with A), so it would only ever produce an exact duplicate under swapped labels. `describeCorrelation`'s phrasing gained a generic "N days later"/"N-day-apart pairs" branch for lag 2 and 3, while `lagDays: 1` keeps its original, more natural "one day .../the next day" wording unchanged (real backend e2e tests assert on that literal substring, and it reads better for the single most common lag anyway). Two new datasets were independently verified via `numpy.corrcoef` *and* a direct Node replica of `shiftForLag`/`computeCorrelation` before being hardcoded into new e2e test assertions: one where same-day and one-day-lag sleep-vs-mood are both firmly under the reporting bar (r ≈ 0.03 and r ≈ -0.08) but a two-day lag is strong (r ≈ 0.92, 7 paired days), and one where the forward direction (sleep predicting next-day mood) is exactly zero but the reverse direction (mood predicting next-day sleep) is strong (r ≈ -0.87, 7 paired days) — concrete proof the two checks this increment adds are each finding something the others miss, not just re-surfacing the same relationship under a different name.

**Correlating non-mood/energy metrics:** closes the last remaining gap the correlation engine's own "What's not built yet" entry named repeatedly — every base pair had mood or energy on one side; nobody ever checked whether sleep predicts habit completion, or whether focused time predicts task completion, directly. The same six metrics already aggregated for the mood/energy pairs (sleep duration, sleep quality, habit completion, tasks completed, focused minutes, journal entries) are now also checked directly against each other — 14 new base pairs (every pair of the six, except sleep duration vs. sleep quality, deliberately excluded for the same reason mood-vs-energy always has been: both come from the same `SleepEntry` row for the same night, a same-moment measurement, not two independent signals). No new correlation logic was needed — `basePairs` just grew from 12 entries to 26, reusing the exact same already-computed series variables; `computeCorrelation`/`shiftForLag`/`describeCorrelation`/`evaluatePair` are untouched. This does meaningfully raise the total candidate-check count, from 84 to 182 per load (26 base pairs × 7 checks each) — still cheap, since every check is a plain in-memory calculation over data already fetched for the trend charts, but real personal data is far more likely to produce several qualifying non-mood/energy pairs simultaneously than the original mood/energy-anchored set ever was (a genuinely busy day plausibly moves tasks completed, focused minutes, *and* journal entries together, for entirely mundane reasons). A new, silent `MAX_CORRELATIONS_RETURNED = 15` cap keeps "Patterns worth noting" from turning into an overwhelming list on an account with a lot of real, correlated activity — trimmed after sorting strongest-first, so the cap never hides a stronger relationship in favor of a weaker one. A new backend e2e test seeds a real, independently-verified relationship between two metrics that are neither Mood nor Energy (focused minutes vs. journal entries, r ≈ 0.97 confirmed via `numpy.corrcoef` before being hardcoded) and confirms it's found and reported with no mood or energy check-ins logged at all — proving these new pairs are genuinely independent of the mood/energy-anchored ones, not just riding along with them.

**Editable email + re-enter onboarding:** closes two real gaps and fixes two real bugs found while closing them. First, email: there was never an editable email field anywhere in this app, and building one locally would have been misleading — a real login-email change needs real verification (a confirmation code, re-auth) that this app has no way to do on its own. Instead, `/settings`' Account card gets a "Change email →" button that opens Clerk's own hosted account-management modal (`openUserProfile()`) via a new `ClerkUserProfileBridge`/`registerClerkOpenUserProfile`/`openClerkUserProfile` bridge, mirroring the exact `ClerkTokenBridge`/`ClerkSignOutBridge` pattern already established for the same reason (`AUTH_MODE=dev` never mounts `<ClerkProvider>` at all, so any Clerk hook can only ever be called from a component that's conditionally mounted). Under `AUTH_MODE=dev`, explanatory text runs instead of a button — there's no real identity provider there to change anything through, since the email is just the `x-dev-user-email` header, fixed by an env var. Building this surfaced a real, separate bug: `UsersService.getOrCreateFromAuth` only ever wrote `email` once, at row creation — if you changed your real login email through Clerk afterward, this app's own copy of it would silently go stale forever. Fixed by comparing `existing.email !== auth.email` on every request and best-effort resyncing via `prisma.user.update` (falling back to the stale row if the update itself fails, rather than ever throwing and blocking a real request over a non-critical resync). Second, re-entering onboarding: the quiz page itself was always reachable by typing `/onboarding` directly (`OnboardingGate` already exempts that path from its redirect-if-incomplete logic), but nothing linked to it, it always started blank regardless of existing answers, and redoing it piled up a second "biggest source of overload" memory fact rather than updating the one already there — a real duplication bug, since `OnboardingService.complete` called generic `MemoryService.create` (which always generates a fresh random `key`) instead of updating by a stable one. Fixed with a new `MemoryService.upsertOnboardingOverloadFact`, upserting on a well-known stable key (`onboarding_overload_source`) the same way this file's other auto-learned facts (`chronotype`, `task_duration_accuracy`, `intervention_response`) already do. A new `ONBOARDING_PREFILL_QUERY` now pre-fills chronotype, work hours, and quiet hours from your existing answers and jumps straight to the quiz step (skipping Welcome) if `onboardingCompletedAt` is already set; the "biggest source of overload" answer deliberately isn't pre-filled — it's stored as a full sentence, not the raw option string, and reverse-parsing it back into one of the five fixed option cards was judged too fragile to be worth it. `/settings` gets a new "Redo the onboarding quiz →" link closing the discoverability gap.

**No visible auto-plan indicator:** closes the specific gap this README named repeatedly right after the automatic re-planning increments shipped — `AiPlanRun.triggerEvent` has always been a real column, correctly written with a `manual_request` default or one of eight real `auto_*` values (see the New/Further auto-replanning triggers entries above), and already a real GraphQL field, but nothing in the UI ever asked for it. `TODAY_PLAN_QUERY`'s `latestPlanRun` and `LATEST_PLAN_RUN_QUERY` both now select `triggerEvent`, and a small `isAutoGenerated(triggerEvent)` helper (a plain `.startsWith('auto_')` check — every real trigger source already emits that consistent prefix, so telling "auto" apart from "I tapped the button" never needs the full list of trigger names) gates a small, text-only "Auto-generated" pill — the same non-color-only badge style the Insights "Next-day" lag badge already established — next to both `AiPlanCard`'s "Proposed plan" heading and its "last plan was applied" text, and the equivalent two spots on `WeeklyPlanCard`. No backend change was needed at all; this was purely a case of real, already-correct data that three frontend query selection sets and two small components never surfaced. The `ai-plan-review.spec.ts` Playwright spec gained a negative-result assertion (a manually-generated-and-accepted plan never shows the badge); the positive case (an auto-generated plan really does show it) is proven at the data layer instead, by the eight existing backend e2e tests in `app.e2e-spec.ts`'s "Automatic re-planning" suite that already assert the exact `triggerEvent` string for every real trigger — deliberately not re-proven through a browser click path, since reliably provoking a real auto-trigger and outrunning its own cooldown from inside a shared-account Playwright spec would be a flakier, weaker signal than the deterministic backend assertion already covering the same fact.

**Goal progress view:** closes the specific gap this README's own Goals-related bullet named — a goal has always linked real tasks to it (`Task.goalId`/`goal`, a real declared Prisma relation since the very first Tasks increment), but nothing ever rolled that up into a "3 of 5 done" view anywhere; a goal was purely a title, description, target date, and status. `Goal` gains two new computed GraphQL fields, `taskCount` and `completedTaskCount` — deliberately not new columns, computed fresh on every read by a new `GoalsService.attachTaskCounts` helper, a single `prisma.task.groupBy({ by: ['goalId', 'status'] })` covering every goal in the response at once rather than one count query per goal. `taskCount` deliberately excludes `CANCELLED` tasks — the same "a cancelled task was never really supposed to happen, don't count it against you" reasoning the Tasks screen's own Open/Cancelled tab split already established — while `completedTaskCount` only counts a genuine `COMPLETED` status, not just any task existing. `/goals` shows the real numbers as text ("3 of 5 tasks done") plus a small progress bar underneath each active goal's card, with the bar's fill-vs-track contrast independently computed and verified (5.55:1 / 5.19:1 light, 4.98:1 / 4.61:1 dark — all comfortably over WCAG 1.4.11's 3:1 non-text minimum) rather than assumed from an existing color pairing; a freshly created goal with nothing linked yet shows a plain "No tasks linked yet" nudge instead of a confusing "0 of 0." A new backend e2e test seeds three tasks under one goal, completes one, cancels one, leaves one open, and confirms the exact `{taskCount: 2, completedTaskCount: 1}` — proving the cancelled-exclusion and completed-only-counting rules for real, not just by reading the code. Two new Playwright specs cover the empty state and the full linked-task-to-completion path end to end (create a goal, link a task to it from Today's own goal picker, complete the task, confirm `/goals` shows "1 of 1 task done").

**Real billing/subscription management:** closes the last remaining gap the Broader account settings increment's own README entry named — the Plan card used to be pure read-only display; `/settings` now has a real plan picker (Free/Plus/Pro, each with a plain display price) that actually changes which tier your account is on. Honest about what "real" means here, on purpose: there is no Stripe integration anywhere in this project — `Subscription.stripeCustomerId`/`stripeSubscriptionId` are real columns that have existed since the very first profile increment, reserved for when one exists, but nothing here has ever populated them — so this deliberately does not build a fake credit-card form or collect fake payment details, since that would look like a real charge is happening when nothing is. What it does do for real: a new `changeSubscriptionTier` mutation writes the actual `tier` and `status` a real `Subscription` row holds — the same row the Plan card, and any future feature that might gate on tier, already reads. Moving to a paid tier sets a real `currentPeriodEnd` 30 days out, simulating what a real monthly billing cycle would leave behind; moving back to Free clears it, since a free plan has no renewal date. `status` always lands on `ACTIVE` — `PAST_DUE`/`CANCELED` are states a real payment failure or a real cancellation flow would produce, neither of which exists in this mock. The picker itself surfaced a real, caught-before-shipping contrast bug: a first draft used a translucent `bg-accent/10` tint with `text-accent` for the "current" pill, which measured a real, independently-computed 4.13:1 in dark mode — under WCAG AA's 4.5:1 text minimum — so it was replaced with the exact same solid `bg-accent text-white` pairing every other CTA button in this app already uses (Save settings, Add, Accept plan, ...), verified at a real 5.55:1 in both light and dark mode specifically because that pairing never swaps to the dark-mode accent value the way a first instinct might suggest. A new backend e2e test moves a real account to Plus and back to Free, asserting the exact `currentPeriodEnd` behavior in both directions (set ~30 days out, then cleared), and a new Playwright spec confirms the picker's `aria-pressed` state changes and persists across a reload — ending by switching back to Free, since this shared dev-auth account is read by every other spec in the suite too.

*(Superseded, partially — see the Real Stripe billing integration entry further below: a genuine Stripe integration now exists, with everything described in this entry kept on as the graceful fallback for a server with no Stripe keys configured, not replaced outright.)*

### What's not built yet

- **No real mobile app — the PWA is the mobile answer for now** — native iOS/Android (also named in the PRD's MVP definition) aren't built and can't be from this kind of environment (they need Xcode/mobile toolchains and App Store accounts); an installed PWA covers the same home-screen/standalone-window experience on both platforms, just through the browser engine rather than a native shell.
- **Offline support covers exactly three actions, not the whole app** — adding a task, completing a task, and writing a journal entry (the three the PRD names) work with no connection and sync on reconnect; editing a task's full details, generating or responding to an AI plan, chat, and every other mutation still require a live connection and simply show their normal error state offline.
- **The offline sync queue is a simple, ordered replay, not a conflict-resolution system** — if the same task was also changed from another device while one was offline, whichever finishes syncing last wins; there's no merge logic, just "replay what was queued, in order." Fine for the realistic single-device-goes-offline case this increment targets, a real gap for true multi-device concurrent editing.
- **The calendar event editor only covers title, start time, and end time** — matching what the underlying providers' own APIs actually accept a PATCH for; a synced event's description isn't editable here (this app never pulls a synced event's description down in the first place — see `upsertFromExternalSource` — so there's nothing to show or edit), and `isImmovable` (a purely local scheduling hint) is edited locally only, never pushed to either provider, since neither has an equivalent field.
- **The push-edit path has never been tested against a real Google or Microsoft account** — same standing limitation as every OAuth-dependent piece of this project built in this sandbox: there are no real client credentials or a real connected account available here (see "What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself" throughout this README), so `updateRemoteEvent`'s actual PATCH request, its 401-triggers-a-refresh retry, and its `RECONNECT_REQUIRED` mapping are all real, syntax-checked code following the exact same shape `deleteRemoteEvent` already uses in production — but "the same shape as code that already works" is not the same as "verified with a real API call," and you should confirm this against your own connected account before trusting it fully.
- **Real-time calendar updates (webhooks) are now built for Google and Microsoft, but never confirmed against a real deployment** — a connected Google account registers a real `channels.watch` subscription and a connected Microsoft account registers a real Graph `/subscriptions` resource, both pointed at this backend's own `BACKEND_PUBLIC_URL` (a new, optional env var — see the "Picking up" section below); either provider's own servers then POST a change notification here, which triggers the exact same incremental sync "Sync now" already does. Needs a genuinely public HTTPS address to do anything at all — with `BACKEND_PUBLIC_URL` unset (the default), this silently stays off and the app behaves exactly as it did before this increment, manual "Sync now" only. Apple (CalDAV) still isn't covered — there's no equivalent push mechanism this app implements for it (see the Apple sync entry above for iCloud's own pull-only limitations).
- **Pushing deletes or edits back to Apple, and recurring-event expansion** — Apple sync is pull-only like Microsoft started, so deleting an Apple-synced event here doesn't delete it in Apple Calendar. Also, a recurring Apple Calendar event (one with an RRULE) syncs in as a single anchor occurrence, not every future instance — the hand-rolled ICS parser deliberately doesn't expand recurrence (see the increment note above). Multiple-calendar support is also missing — `findDefaultCalendar` always picks the first calendar found in your iCloud account; if you keep events split across several calendars, only one syncs.
- **Apple sync's TZID handling is a best-effort approximation** — an Apple Calendar event's start/end time that isn't already in UTC (no trailing `Z`) is interpreted in your AI Life Manager profile's own timezone, not the event's actual `VTIMEZONE` block (parsing that properly is real, separate work). Usually close enough for personal use, but a genuine known limitation, not a bug you're imagining if a synced Apple event looks slightly off.
- **Wearable sync and inferred energy** — `EnergySource.INFERRED` and `SleepSource.HEALTHKIT`/`HEALTH_CONNECT`/`OURA`/`WHOOP` are reserved enum values with no integration code behind them yet.
- **Journal sentiment analysis now scores every entry, but doesn't yet feed mood inference elsewhere** — `JournalEntry.sentimentScore` (-1 to 1) is populated for real, right when an entry is saved, whenever `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` is configured (see `AnthropicClient.analyzeSentiment` and `JournalService.create`). A sustained run of clearly positive or clearly negative entries also writes a `journal_sentiment` AI Memory fact (see Automatic AI Memory learning below) that reaches the planner/chat prompt the same way the other three signals do. What's still genuinely missing from the PRD's original "journal sentiment feeds mood inference" vision: `sentimentScore` doesn't factor into Insights' correlation engine at all (see the chronotype signal's own "only from two specific signals" gap below for the same kind of limitation) — a real journal-sentiment-vs-mood correlation card isn't built.
- **The 3 daily reflection questions' wording is configurable, but the shape isn't** — Settings now has a "Daily reflection questions" section where you can rename what's *displayed* for each of the three fixed questions; there's still no way to add a fourth question, remove one, or change what's actually stored on a submitted reflection (still always `wentWell`/`challenging`/`carryForward` — the AI summary prompt and Insights both assume exactly these three keys). (The reminder-to-actually-do-it gap this bullet used to also list is closed — see the Scheduler/reminders section below.)
- **Automatic AI Memory learning — all three PRD §9 signals are now built, plus a fourth beyond the original PRD list** (`intervention_response`, `task_duration_accuracy`, `chronotype`, and now `journal_sentiment` — see the Journal sentiment analysis bullet above for that fourth one). None of the four are shown on the `/memory` page itself, a consistent, deliberate gap across all four, not an oversight specific to any one of them.
- **Chronotype only buckets into three broad dayparts, and only from two specific signals** — morning/afternoon/evening, not finer-grained hours, and only energy check-ins + completed Focus sessions feed it; task-completion timestamps, mood, and journal-sentiment timing aren't factored in even though they could plausibly sharpen the signal later. There's also no visible "here's what the AI has learned about your patterns" view anywhere in the UI — same gap as the other two automatic-learning signals, all three are invisible until they change what the AI proposes or says.
- **All three channels — push, email, and SMS — are real now, but delivery overall is still best-effort/fire-and-forget**, not the approved design's durable Temporal dispatcher — a delivery attempt that fails on any channel (a down email provider, an invalid Twilio number, say) is logged and the row is still marked `deliveredAt`, not retried with backoff.
- **SMS has never actually sent a real text message in this build process** — same standing OAuth/third-API limitation as every provider integration built in this sandbox: there's no real Twilio account, Account SID, or Auth Token available here, so `SmsService.send`'s actual HTTP request has never been made for real. It was written by mirroring `EmailService`'s already-working shape closely (see the increment entry above), which reduces risk but doesn't replace testing it — get a Twilio trial account and confirm a real text arrives before trusting this fully.
- **The Playwright suite now spans 15 spec files across every major screen, but still couldn't be run for real in this sandbox** — Today, Tasks, Journal, PWA/offline, push notifications, Goals, Habits, Calendar, Focus sessions, check-ins, Reflection, Routines, Chat, AI Memory, Insights, and the AI plan review flow are all real, written browser specs now (see the Extended Playwright coverage entry above) — only the AI recommendations card and Onboarding have no spec of their own, for the reasons noted there. Verified via the same syntax sweep as every other increment (244 files, 0 diagnostics) — but not by Playwright's own test collector this time, since a fresh `npm install` attempt in this sandbox while verifying the accessibility pass's new dependency left `apps/e2e/node_modules` genuinely missing (see that increment's own note on the interrupted install + sandbox permission quirk) — so unlike the original 5-journey suite, `playwright test --list` couldn't even be attempted here. Run `npm install && npx playwright install chromium && npx playwright test` yourself once on a normal machine to get the first real confirmation these specs actually pass; the selectors were checked by hand against each page's real source, not assumed, but only an actual run can catch what reading the source can't (timing, real AI-reply latency, actual rendered DOM structure).
- **The accessibility pass is a real, thorough sweep, not a claim of full WCAG AA compliance everywhere** — `accessibility.spec.ts`'s axe-core scan covers 13 major pages' default, data-loaded state specifically, not every dialog/modal/expanded-row/error-state variant each page can be in, and it's scoped to WCAG 2.0/2.1 A+AA rule tags with only critical/serious findings enforced — a moderate or minor axe finding is logged, not failed on (see the increment entry above for why). Clerk's own hosted `/sign-in`/`/sign-up` pages are out of scope entirely, since this project doesn't own their markup. And every fix in this pass was verified by a real, correct contrast-ratio calculation and a syntax check — not by testing with an actual screen reader (VoiceOver, NVDA, JAWS). The Screen-reader pass entry below closes the specific class of gap axe's automated scan structurally can't see (missing ARIA state, live-region completeness, focus management) via a rigorous manual audit — but still not a real VoiceOver/NVDA/JAWS run, which remains the single most valuable thing left to check on this app's accessibility story.
- **Two request-driven triggers, four scheduled ones** — `plan_ready` and `recommendations_ready` still only ever fire because a person tapped a button; `morning_routine_reminder`, `evening_routine_reminder`, `reflection_reminder`, and `habit_reminder` are real, time-based triggers now (see the Scheduler/reminders increment below) — this bullet used to say the app had no scheduler of any kind, which is no longer true, but request-driven and time-driven triggers still coexist rather than everything having moved to one model.
- **Quiet hours only defer creation-time scheduling, there's no dispatcher that re-wakes an already-deferred notification early** — if a notification's deferred `scheduledFor` time passes while you're not looking, it just becomes visible next time you check (correct, and now also re-affirmed by the reminder sweep re-running its own checks every 15 minutes for anything still outstanding) — but there's no separate process whose whole job is "wake up exactly when a deferred notification's window opens." Close enough at this app's scale, a real gap at a bigger one.
- **Chat's tool-calling covers seven real actions, not everything the app can do** — create/complete/reschedule a task, log a mood or energy check-in, add a calendar event, and save a memory fact (see the Tool-calling actions in Chat and Expanded tool set for Chat entries below). Still not reachable from inside a chat message: goals, habits, focus sessions, journal entries, routines, and reflection — asked for any of those, the AI still says so plainly and points back to the app, exactly as it always has for anything outside its real capabilities.
- **An added task doesn't feed the automatic accept/reject learning signal either** — same deliberate scope cut as an edited-then-applied plan (see the bullet below): `intervention_response` only ever looks at `ACCEPTED`/`REJECTED` runs, so a plan with only manually-added tasks and no AI-proposed changes at all is just as invisible to that signal as an edited one.
- **A task-detail edit's duration recompute only ever runs through the `EDIT` decision, not a plain `ACCEPT`** — if you edit a task's duration from the plan card and then somehow still see a plain Accept option (shouldn't normally happen, since any task-detail edit marks its row as touched), the applied schedule would use the plan's originally-generated end time, not the new duration; this is a deliberate, narrow scope cut, not something anyone's likely to hit in practice.
- **The in-plan task editor still only covers title, priority, and duration** — matching what a plan change actually cares about (scheduling); due date, description, goal link, and tags aren't editable from inside a plan review row specifically, though all four are now editable from the new Tasks list/edit screen (see above) — just not from the plan card itself.
- **A subtask isn't reachable through the Tasks screen's own pagination the same way a top-level task is** — `SubtaskList` always shows *all* of a parent's subtasks in one shot with no "Load more" of its own, since a task realistically has a handful of subtasks, not hundreds; the Tasks pagination increment's paging only applies to the top-level Open/Cancelled lists.
- **Subtasks are one level deep only, and don't inherit their parent's priority/schedule/plan participation** — a subtask can't itself have subtasks (the backend's `TASK_INCLUDE` only eager-loads one level, matching the `TaskSubtasks` self-relation's own flat design), and a subtask is otherwise just a plain `Task` row: it can be picked up by the AI daily planner, added to a plan, or shown on Today in its own right, exactly like any other task with no special "this belongs to a parent" treatment anywhere except the two places that now show it (the Tasks screen's `SubtaskList` and Today's small count badge). Nothing stops a subtask from being scheduled at a wildly different time than its parent, since nothing links them beyond the one `parentTaskId` field.
- **An edited-then-applied plan doesn't feed the automatic accept/reject learning signal** — `intervention_response` (see Automatic AI Memory learning above) only ever counts `ACCEPTED`/`REJECTED` runs; `EDITED` runs are invisible to it, a deliberate scope cut, not an oversight — see the Editing a proposed AI plan entry above for the open question this leaves.
- **Automatic re-planning now covers all three scopes and eight triggers** — completing a task, changing a native calendar event, completing a habit, logging a mood or energy check-in, fully finishing a routine, writing a journal entry, completing a focus session, or submitting a daily reflection each now trigger a real re-plan at DAY, WEEK, and MONTH scope (see the Extending auto-replanning to WEEK/MONTH scope, New auto-replanning triggers, and Further auto-replanning triggers entries above). No further plausible everyday signal in this app is left un-wired at this point — sleep logging is the one deliberate exception (see `logSleep`'s own comment for why).
- **An auto-generated WEEK/MONTH plan doesn't show up on `WeeklyPlanCard` until it next refetches, which can lag well behind DAY's own plan** — `TODAY_PLAN_QUERY` (which `AiPlanCard` reads) gets refetched by several other mutations already (completing a task, for one), so a fresh DAY-scope auto-plan tends to show up almost immediately in practice, badge included; `WeeklyPlanCard`'s own `LATEST_PLAN_RUN_QUERY` isn't in any of those `refetchQueries` lists, so an auto-generated WEEK or MONTH plan is real and saved (and would show its own "Auto-generated" badge once it does render — see the No visible auto-plan indicator entry above), but won't actually render until the next time that card's own query happens to run — reopening `/today`, say.
- **A routine's historical consistency is judged against its current checklist, not what it looked like on each past day** — there's no per-day snapshot of a routine's step count, so changing how many steps a routine has retroactively changes what "fully done" meant for every day already in the window, most visible right after a checklist edit (see the Life analytics entry above).
- **Cross-metric correlation now checks 26 base pairs at same-day plus a one/two/three-day lag in both directions (182 candidate checks total, capped at the 15 strongest actually returned)** — sleep duration/quality, habit completion, tasks completed, focused minutes, and journal entries are checked against mood and energy *and* directly against each other now (see the Correlating non-mood/energy metrics entry above). The only excluded pair among these six metrics is sleep duration vs. sleep quality — same-source, same-moment measurement, the same reasoning mood-vs-energy has always been excluded for.
- **Cross-metric correlation still treats every day as an independent sample, and doesn't correlate mood against energy** — a fully rigorous statistical treatment would need to account for nearby days not really being independent (a fixed statistical-methodology gap this app doesn't currently have a design for closing); mood-vs-energy and sleep-duration-vs-sleep-quality are both left out deliberately, not as gaps — each is the same check-in/entry's two same-moment measurements, not two independent signals. Same-day, one-, two-, and three-day lag are all checked now, in both directions — there's no *four-or-more*-day lag, which would need its own judgment call about whether a relationship that far out is even a meaningful thing to look for in a personal-reflection tool.
- **The auto-plan indicator is a real "Auto-generated" pill now, not silent data** — see the No visible auto-plan indicator entry above. It's been proven correct at the data layer (eight backend e2e tests assert the exact `triggerEvent` string per trigger) and confirmed by hand against the actual rendered JSX, but — same honest caveat as the rest of this project's frontend work — never actually seen rendering in a real browser here, since a real auto-trigger has never fired against a live Postgres in this sandbox (see "What's verified" below).
- **Habit recurrence now covers ten real shapes across three increments, not the full generality of iCalendar RRULE** — every day, every N days, specific days of the week, every N weeks on those days, monthly on a day-of-month (or the last day), monthly on the Nth (or last) weekday, every N months, an optional `COUNT`/`UNTIL` end condition, several specific days of the month in one rule, and "the Nth (or last) day among a set of weekdays" (the classic "last weekday of the month") are all real now (see the Full custom habit recurrence, Fuller habit recurrence, and BYSETPOS / multiple weekdays per month entries below — each one closed the specific gap the previous entry's own "what's still missing" note left open). What's left, genuinely: no `WKST` override, no combining `BYMONTHDAY` and `BYDAY` in the same single rule, no negative/"last day" values inside the multi-day-of-month shape — narrower edge cases than before, not a fully general RRULE editor, and none currently blocking a realistic personal habit. A habit's recurrence is no longer create-time only — see the Habit-edit UI entry below.
- **Settings now covers name, timezone, chronotype, work hours, email (real change via Clerk's hosted modal, or read-only in dev mode), a real Stripe-backed plan picker, account deletion, and redoing onboarding** — see the Visible settings screen, Broader account settings, Editable email + re-enter onboarding, and Real Stripe billing integration entries above. Real Checkout and a real Billing Portal now exist (see the Real Stripe billing integration entry), with a graceful simulated fallback for a server with no Stripe keys configured — still genuinely missing: this has never been run against a real Stripe account with real test-mode credentials in this sandbox (see that entry's own "What's verified" section for the exact honest scope); account deletion has never been run against a real Postgres database in this sandbox either (see "What's verified" below) — only reasoned through relation-by-relation against the schema; and the Clerk `openUserProfile()` modal itself has never been opened against a real Clerk account here, for the same standing OAuth/third-party-integration reason named throughout this README.
- **Pomodoro mode's cycle position still doesn't survive a reload, but its cadence is configurable now** — the four numbers (work minutes, short break, long break, cycles before a long break) can be changed from `/settings` and are saved per-user (see the Configurable Pomodoro durations entry below); `pomodoroMode` and the current cycle count remain client-side React state, not stored server-side — the in-progress session itself always resumes correctly after a reload, but a reload does stop the automatic chain and reset which cycle you're on.
- **Routine steps have no per-step time/duration, and reordering is display-only** — a step is just a label; there's no way to attach an estimated duration or a fixed time to one, and the AI-sequenced order that gets computed only ever changes what's *displayed* — it's recomputed fresh each time Today loads (not persisted back onto the stored template), so it's never permanently reordered.
- **Focus sessions now feed real duration back onto a task, but only as a pre-filled suggestion, and only from Today's own completion prompt** — see the Focus sessions feed task duration back entry below for the details; the one thing still missing is a dedicated task-edit screen, so a duration estimate can only be set at creation time (Quick Add) or via the AI button there — not added or changed afterward on an existing task, and there's still no way to attach a focus session's real time to a task from anywhere other than that same completion prompt.
- **AI recommendations now act on your behalf for all three categories, each with real, distinct follow-through, each customizable at the point of acting, and the WORKOUT booking now avoids real conflicts** — a BREAK suggestion really starts a real break timer (15 minutes by default), a WORKOUT suggestion really books a real calendar block at the first open slot at or after right now (30 minutes by default, see the Booking a workout as a real calendar block and Workout-booking conflict avoidance entries below), and a MEAL suggestion really creates a real open task (Normal priority, no due date by default), all with one tap — and a **Customize →** disclosure on each suggestion (see the Customize act-on defaults at the point of acting entry below) lets a person override the relevant defaults (break/block length, block start time, task priority/due date) before committing, without ever having to edit the task or calendar event afterward. There's still no automatic generation on a schedule either — you have to open Today and tap the button yourself; that part hasn't changed even with Smart notifications now built (see below), since nothing in this app can trigger work on a timer yet.
- **Weekly/monthly plans now protect every due day's habit time across the whole window, not just today's** — see the Weekly/monthly plans protecting habits across the window entry below. WEEK/MONTH still aren't real calendar-week/calendar-month boundaries, just fixed 7-day/30-day rolling windows from today — "this month" doesn't mean "the rest of the current calendar month." And there's still no recurring/automatic generation (a real "regenerate my week every Monday morning" flow) — same manual-tap-only pattern as the daily planner, and the same gap Automatic re-planning below already covers.
- **Onboarding answers can be edited now (chronotype, work hours, timezone), but there's still no way to re-enter the quiz flow itself once it's completed** — `completeOnboarding` remains a single one-shot mutation with no "redo the quiz" screen; the individual answers that matter ongoing now have a real edit surface (see the Visible settings screen entry above), but if you want to see the quiz's own cards again, the only path is navigating to `/onboarding` directly — `OnboardingGate` won't route you back there itself once `onboardingCompletedAt` is set. The "biggest source of overload" answer specifically was never a distinct field to begin with — it's recorded as a plain AI Memory fact on `/memory` (see `OnboardingService.complete`), editable there already, not duplicated onto the new settings screen.
- **Onboarding is still all-or-nothing at the `onboardingCompletedAt` level, but the wizard itself is resumable now** — `onboardingCompletedAt` is still stamped the moment the diagnostic quiz step submits (even an all-skipped one), which is still deliberate so `OnboardingGate` stops redirecting a person who's seen the quiz — see the Resumable onboarding wizard entry below for what changed: revisiting `/onboarding` (a closed tab reopened, say) now resumes at the real Connect-calendar or First-plan step you left off at, tracked server-side, instead of losing that progress or incorrectly jumping back to the quiz. Still genuinely missing: nothing *proactively* routes you back into the wizard if you never revisit `/onboarding` yourself — a person who quits after the quiz and never returns stays exactly that way forever, which is deliberate (the calendar step's own "You can always do this later" copy means exactly that), not an oversight.
- **The diagnostic quiz no longer has any fixed-preset-only questions** — chronotype is still three quick cards (a genuinely small, natural set — nobody's asking for a fourth chronotype), but work-hours/quiet-hours (see the Free time picker for quiz's work/quiet hours entry below) and "biggest source of overload" (see the Free-text "biggest source of overload" entry below) are all real free-entry fields now, and the quiz also has one more genuinely open-ended question beyond those (see the Diagnostic quiz free-text answers entry below). What was originally a "quiz, not a form" scope cut per the UI/UX Design Document's own framing has been fully closed, one question at a time, across four separate increments.
- **Connecting Google or Microsoft Calendar from inside the onboarding wizard no longer drops you out of it** — see the Fix onboarding calendar-connect redirect entry below. Apple's connect flow (a plain form submit, no OAuth redirect) never had this problem to begin with.
- **Goals now ladder up from both Tasks and Habits, and a habit's goal link is no longer create-time only** — `Habit.goalId` is real now (see the Linking habits to goals entry below), matching the PRD's "tasks *and habits* ladder up to" wording, and — see the Habit-edit UI entry below — can now be relinked or cleared afterward too, the same as a task's own goal picker. Both goal-progress signals are real and visible on `/goals` now — "N of M tasks done" plus a progress bar, and a separate "N habit(s) linked" line — but they're deliberately not combined into one number, since a habit has no terminal "done" state the way a task does.
- **The scheduler is in-process, not durable** — real, genuinely-repeating execution (see the Scheduler feature entry above), but it's still just a `setInterval`-shaped cron running inside the same Node process as everything else. Unlike the Architecture Document's approved Temporal design, a server restart mid-cycle simply skips that cycle rather than resuming it, there's no retry/backoff if a single check throws (the sweep logs and moves to the next user — see `SchedulerService.checkReminders` — but a genuinely failed check for one person just doesn't happen that cycle, no automatic retry later), and running more than one backend instance would mean every instance independently sweeping every user, duplicating reminders rather than coordinating — none of that matters at this project's current scale, all of it would at a real one.
- **Reminder windows and thresholds are configurable now, but the 30-minute catch window isn't** — 8am/8pm for routines, 9pm for reflection, and the 15–120 minute habit-overdue range are all real per-user settings now (see the Configurable reminder windows/thresholds entry below); moving a reflection reminder to 10pm no longer needs editing code. Still fixed: the 30-minute width of each catch window itself (an implementation detail, not a per-user preference). A habit reminder that goes unread now does get one later escalation if it's still unread well past the original window (see the Reminder escalation / second nudge entry below) — but exactly how much later is a fixed offset, not yet its own per-user setting the way the base window already is.
- **"Automatic re-planning" is still a separate, unclosed gap** — the scheduler increment is entirely about *time-based* triggers (reminders); it doesn't touch the different, larger gap of *event-driven* triggers (auto-replanning after a task completes or a calendar event changes), which stays exactly as open as it was before this increment.

## Before you run anything

Copy the env template and fill in real values:

```bash
cp .env.example .env
cp apps/web/.env.local.example apps/web/.env.local   # see note below
```

The `.env.example` at the root documents every variable. For local development you don't need real Clerk keys — see **Dev auth mode** below.

## Running it locally

```bash
docker compose up -d          # Postgres (with pgvector) + Redis
cd apps/backend
npm install
npm run prisma:generate       # downloads Prisma's query engine — needs normal internet access
npm run prisma:migrate        # creates the users/subscriptions tables
npm run dev                   # http://localhost:4000/graphql

# in a second terminal
cd apps/web
npm install
npm run dev                   # http://localhost:3000
```

If you'd rather install everything from the repo root in one shot (`npm install` with the workspaces in `package.json`), that works too — installing per-app above is just the more foolproof path if you ever hit a slow/flaky network mid-install.

## Dev auth mode (no Clerk account needed)

Set `AUTH_MODE=dev` (backend) and `NEXT_PUBLIC_AUTH_MODE=dev` (web) — both already set in `.env.example`. In this mode:

- The backend accepts a plain `x-dev-user-email` header instead of a verified Clerk session and auto-provisions a user on first request (see `apps/backend/src/auth/auth.guard.ts`).
- The web app injects that header automatically (see `apps/web/src/lib/apollo-client.ts`), using `NEXT_PUBLIC_DEV_USER_EMAIL`.
- This path is **hard-disabled whenever `NODE_ENV=production`** (enforced in `apps/backend/src/config/env.validation.ts` — the app refuses to boot otherwise). It exists purely so the app is runnable and testable before you've set up a Clerk project.

When you're ready for real accounts, create a free Clerk app at [dashboard.clerk.com](https://dashboard.clerk.com), set `AUTH_MODE=clerk` / `NEXT_PUBLIC_AUTH_MODE=clerk`, and fill in the Clerk keys.

One thing to verify yourself once you have real keys: `apps/web/src/middleware.ts` is currently a passthrough (route protection happens per-page). Clerk's `clerkMiddleware()` is the documented way to add middleware-level protection — wire it up and test it against your real Clerk project at that point, since it can't be meaningfully tested without one.

## What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself

Everything in this increment was built and tested against a real running NestJS server and a real `next build`/`next start`, including live GraphQL queries over HTTP (`me`, `todayPlan`, `updateProfile`) with authentication enforced. Two things could **not** be verified in the sandboxed environment this was built in, purely because that environment blocks some outbound network requests that your machine or CI won't:

1. **`npx prisma generate` against a real Postgres database.** Prisma downloads a native query-engine binary from `binaries.prisma.sh` on first generate; that host wasn't reachable from the build sandbox. The Prisma schema and all the code that uses it were verified against a small in-memory stand-in instead. Run `npm run prisma:generate && npm run prisma:migrate` yourself after `docker compose up -d` — this is completely standard Prisma usage and should just work.
2. **A pixel screenshot of the Today screen.** Headless browser binaries (Playwright/Chromium) couldn't be downloaded in the sandbox either. The page was verified via server-rendered HTML output instead (correct shell, correct classes, no errors). Open `http://localhost:3000/today` yourself to see it rendered — if anything looks off, tell me and I'll fix it.

## Project structure

```
apps/backend/   NestJS + GraphQL + Prisma (Architecture Document §4.2–4.3)
apps/web/       Next.js + Tailwind + Clerk + Apollo Client
apps/e2e/       Playwright browser-automation test suite (real Chromium, drives apps/web + apps/backend)
packages/       (reserved for shared types/UI once a second client needs them)
docker-compose.yml   Local Postgres + Redis only — production runs on
                     Aurora PostgreSQL + ElastiCache (Architecture §4.4)
```

## Picking up the Tasks feature increment

If you already had this project set up before, you just need to bring your database schema up to date and restart the backend:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npx prisma migrate dev --name add_tasks_and_goals
npm run dev
```

The web app doesn't need any changes to run — its dev server hot-reloads automatically.

## Picking up the Calendar feature increment

Same pattern — a new migration for the `calendar_events` table, then restart the backend:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm install                 # picks up the new `luxon` dependency (timezone-aware day boundaries)
npx prisma migrate dev --name add_calendar_events
npm run dev
```

The web app doesn't need any changes to run.

## What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Calendar increment)

The whole backend (existing code plus every new Calendar file) passes a full TypeScript compile with zero errors, and the new timezone day-boundary logic (`zoned-day.ts` — the trickiest new piece, since getting it wrong would silently mis-bucket events across midnight) has a real, executed unit test suite covering UTC, a negative-offset zone, a DST transition, and a positive-offset zone — all 4 passing. What could **not** be run in this build sandbox, for the same reason as the Tasks increment (the Prisma query-engine binary is blocked from downloading here), is the new Calendar e2e test suite against a real Postgres database. Run it yourself once your migration is applied:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing GraphQL/Tasks/Goals suites plus a new "Calendar (e2e)" suite (create + appears in `todayPlan.events`, invalid time range rejected, update + delete, cross-user rejection).

## Picking up the Google Calendar sync increment

This one needs a one-time setup step before any code will actually connect to anything: create OAuth credentials at [console.cloud.google.com](https://console.cloud.google.com) — enable the **Google Calendar API**, then under **Credentials** create an **OAuth client ID** of type **Web application** with `http://localhost:4000/auth/google/callback` as an authorized redirect URI. Under **OAuth consent screen**, choose **External** and add your own Google account as a test user (fine for personal use, keeps it out of Google's app review process).

Then add these to your `.env` (see `.env.example` for the full block):

```bash
GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID="<from Google Cloud>"
GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET="<from Google Cloud>"
GOOGLE_REDIRECT_URI="http://localhost:4000/auth/google/callback"
TOKEN_ENCRYPTION_KEY="<any passphrase — encrypts stored tokens at rest>"
OAUTH_STATE_SECRET="<any different passphrase — signs the OAuth redirect>"
```

All five are required together; leave all five blank to keep the feature off (the app boots fine either way — `startGoogleCalendarConnection` just returns a clear `GOOGLE_NOT_CONFIGURED` error instead of a confusing crash).

Then, the same migration + restart pattern as every other increment:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npx prisma migrate dev --name add_calendar_accounts
npm run dev
```

Open `http://localhost:3000/calendar` — you'll see a "Connect Google Calendar" card. Connecting redirects you through Google's real consent screen and back; once connected, your Google events appear alongside native ones within a few seconds, tagged "Google".

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Google sync increment)

The whole backend passes a full TypeScript compile with zero errors. The two genuinely security-sensitive pieces of new code — token-at-rest encryption (`token-cipher.ts`) and the signed OAuth `state` param that ties Google's redirect back to the right user (`oauth-state.ts`) — have real, executed unit test suites (9 tests total: encrypt/decrypt round-trip, ciphertext never contains the plaintext, different ciphertext each time, wrong secret fails, tampered bytes fail, tampered state fails, expired state fails), all passing. What could not be verified here, because it requires an actual Google account and a real browser OAuth round trip that this build sandbox can't do: the live connect flow itself, and a real sync against your actual calendar. That part is on you to try once your credentials are in place — tell me what you see and I'll fix anything that's off.

## Picking up the AI daily planning increment

This one needs a one-time setup step before "Generate my plan" will do anything: create an API key at [console.anthropic.com](https://console.anthropic.com) (**API Keys** section — you'll need billing set up on the account, same as any Anthropic API usage).

Then add these to your `.env` (see `.env.example` for the full block):

```bash
ANTHROPIC_API_KEY="<from console.anthropic.com>"
ANTHROPIC_MODEL="claude-sonnet-5"   # already the default; only change it if you want a different model
```

Leave `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` blank to keep the feature off — the app boots fine either way, and "Generate my plan" shows a clear message instead of crashing.

Then, the same migration + restart pattern as every other increment:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npx prisma migrate dev --name add_ai_plan_runs
npm run dev
```

The web app doesn't need any changes to run. Open `http://localhost:3000/today` — you'll see a new card under the mood/energy/sleep check-in with a **Generate my plan** button. Tap it, review the proposed times and reasons, then Accept or Reject.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (AI daily planning increment)

Every new file was carefully hand-reviewed line by line, and this review caught two real bugs before delivery: a TypeScript enum mismatch in `planner.service.ts` (a string-literal type where the resolver was actually passing a proper enum) and a missing explicit GraphQL `ID` type on `respondToPlanRun`'s `id` argument. Both are fixed.

What's different from every prior increment: I could **not** run a full automated TypeScript compile (`tsc --noEmit`) as a final check here. In every earlier increment that check ran cleanly in 10–20 seconds; on this one it started consistently timing out against my own sandbox's hard time limit, purely because the project has grown enough that resolving all its module imports over this sandbox's slow cross-machine file access takes longer than the time limit allows (confirmed it's not a CPU or code problem — the actual compile work is fast, the file-system round trips aren't). This is a limit of my build environment, not a sign of a real problem, and it doesn't affect your machine, which reads its own files at normal disk speed and should compile in seconds like every prior increment did. I relied on manual review instead for this one, which is how I caught the two bugs above — but you compiling it yourself once (`cd apps/backend && npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.build.json`) is worth doing here specifically, since it's the one increment that didn't get an automated final pass.

The policy layer (the code that drops AI proposals conflicting with fixed events or each other) has real e2e test coverage using a fake stand-in for the Anthropic API — deterministic, no real API key or network call needed to run it — alongside the usual can't-run-here-for-Prisma-reasons caveat:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus two new ones: "AI daily planning — not configured" (confirms the feature degrades to a clear error with no key set) and "AI daily planning — configured" (generate → one proposal correctly dropped for conflicting with a fixed event → accept writes real `scheduledStart` → reject leaves the task alone → double-response and cross-user rejection). The one thing that suite can't cover, by design, is a real call to Anthropic's API — try Generate my plan in the browser yourself once your key is in place, and tell me what you see.

## Picking up the Habits increment

No new setup step this time — no external account, no API key. Just the usual migration + restart:

```bash
cd apps/backend

```

The web app doesn't need any changes to run. Tap the checkbox to mark it done; tap again to undo.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Habits increment)

Habits' one genuinely tricky piece of new logic is the recurrence handling (`rrule.ts` — deciding whether a habit is "due today"), and it has real, executed, passing unit tests covering: daily habits always due, weekly habits due only on their selected days, day-order/de-duplication when building the stored RRULE string, and rejecting invalid input (empty day list, out-of-range weekday numbers). I could not run this suite for you inside my own sandbox for the same reason noted below, but I did verify the logic by hand-checking the actual calendar dates the tests use against a real calendar.

Same limitation as the AI daily planning increment: a full project-wide `tsc --noEmit` still times out in my sandbox (an I/O speed issue reading the mounted filesystem, not a code problem — confirmed again this time). What's different this time is I found a partial workaround: I could still typecheck the new standalone files in isolation (`rrule.ts` and its test file compiled clean on their own), and I used that to specifically re-verify the trickiest piece of new code — the recurrence-parsing logic in `habits.service.ts`'s `update` method — after manual review flagged a real TypeScript error there (accessing `.daysOfWeek` on a union type without narrowing it first, the same class of mistake as the enum bug from the AI planning increment). That one's fixed and re-verified against an isolated reproduction of the exact code shape. I still could not typecheck the resolver, DTOs, and frontend files as a whole, so those got manual review only — worth running `cd apps/backend && npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.build.json` yourself once, same recommendation as last time.

The e2e suite has a full "Habits" block (create a daily habit and see it on Today, reject a weekly habit with no days picked, a weekly habit only appearing on its selected day using a day-of-week computed at test-run time so it's never flaky, complete/uncomplete flipping the checklist state, deactivating hides it from Today but keeps it in the full list, and cross-user rejection) — same can't-run-here-for-Prisma-reasons caveat as every prior increment:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

## Picking up the Chat increment

Uses the same `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`/`ANTHROPIC_MODEL` you already set up for AI daily planning — nothing new to add to `.env` if that's already working. Just the usual migration + restart:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npx prisma migrate dev --name add_chat
npm run dev
```

The web app doesn't need any changes to run. Open `http://localhost:3000/chat` and ask something like "what should I focus on today?" — the reply should reference your actual open tasks/calendar/check-ins, not generic advice. Send another message to continue the thread, or tap **New chat** to start a fresh one; previous chats are listed when you're not in one.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Chat increment)

Same limitation as the last two increments: a full project-wide `tsc --noEmit` still times out in my sandbox (the mounted-filesystem I/O issue, not a code problem). I again used the isolated-file workaround — this time to specifically confirm that constructing a `new Date(existingDateValue)` from an already-`Date`-typed field (used throughout the new `buildContext` prompt-building code, the same pattern the AI planning increment's code already relies on) is actually valid TypeScript and not something that only happens to work because full-project checking hasn't run cleanly in a while. It checks out. The resolver, DTOs, service logic, and frontend `/chat` page still only got manual line-by-line review, not an automated check — worth running `cd apps/backend && npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.build.json` yourself once.

One real design decision worth knowing about, separate from verification: `AnthropicClient` (the low-level Anthropic API wrapper) used to be registered only inside `PlannerModule`. For this increment I changed `PlannerModule` to also *export* it, and had `ChatModule` import `PlannerModule` to reuse that same instance, rather than declaring a second independent copy — a deliberate architecture choice (a second independent registration would have worked functionally, but would have made e2e-test mocking ambiguous about which copy gets overridden). Nothing about the AI daily planning feature's behavior changed.

The e2e suite has two new blocks, "Chat — not configured" (confirms the feature degrades to a clear error with no key set, and an empty conversation list) and "Chat — configured" (using a fake stand-in for Anthropic, same deterministic-no-real-API-key approach as the AI planning suite): first message creates a conversation with both messages in order, a follow-up continues the same thread, the conversation list omits message bodies, fetching a single conversation returns full history, and cross-user access is rejected on both the send-message mutation and the single-conversation query. Same can't-run-here-for-Prisma-reasons caveat as every prior increment:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus "Chat — not configured" and "Chat — configured." The one thing that suite can't cover, by design, is a real call to Anthropic's API — try the chat in the browser yourself, and tell me what you see.

## Picking up the AI Memory increment

Same `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` you already have — nothing new to add to `.env`. Just the usual migration + restart:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npx prisma migrate dev --name add_ai_memory
npm run dev
```

The web app doesn't need any changes to run. Open `http://localhost:3000/memory` and add something like "never schedule calls before 10am." Then either generate a plan on the Today screen or ask the chat something — both now factor in whatever you've told the AI to remember. "Memory" is a real link in the bottom nav.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (AI Memory increment)

Same sandbox limitation as the last three increments — a full project-wide `tsc --noEmit` still times out here (the mounted-filesystem I/O issue, confirmed again, not a code problem). This time, on top of manual line-by-line review, I used a faster isolated-file technique than before: rather than only checking single files in isolation, I ran the *entire* new/changed backend file set (all of Memory's files, plus the modified `planner.service.ts`/`chat.service.ts`/module files) through TypeScript's transpile API directly, which validates syntax across all of them in about a second instead of timing out — genuinely new information, not something I could already tell you before running it, and it did confirm every file parses cleanly. That check doesn't catch type *mismatches* the way a real compile does, though (it's syntax-only), so I still did full manual review for type-level correctness — the same review that has caught a real bug in each of the last three increments (the enum-mismatch class of bug specifically) caught nothing new here, since `memory.service.ts` deliberately shapes every value it hands back to the GraphQL layer through an explicit, already-reviewed helper function rather than passing raw database rows through directly. Worth running `cd apps/backend && npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.build.json` yourself once regardless, same standing recommendation as every increment before this one.

The e2e suite has two new blocks: "Memory — CRUD and cross-user isolation" (create/list/update/delete, and confirms a second identity can't touch someone else's memory facts), and "Memory — reaching the AI planner and chat prompts" — this second one is the important one, because it doesn't just check that facts are *stored*, it overrides `AnthropicClient` with a fake that captures the exact prompt text the real `planner.service.ts`/`chat.service.ts` code builds, then asserts a memory fact you created shows up inside it. That's checking the real production prompt-building code, not a simulation of it. Same can't-run-here-for-Prisma-reasons caveat as every prior increment:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus both new "Memory" blocks. The one thing that suite can't cover, by design, is whether Claude actually *follows* a stated preference in a real reply — try it yourself (add a memory fact, then generate a plan or ask chat something that preference would affect) and tell me what you see.

## Picking up the "wire Habits into the AI planner" increment

No schema change this time — just new logic inside `planner.service.ts`, so no migration to run. Just restart the backend:

