```bash
npm run dev
```

The web app doesn't need any changes to run or restart. To see it: on `/habits`, create a habit with a preferred time (say, today's actual current time plus a few minutes, and a protected duration of 30+ minutes) and add a task on the Today screen for around that same time. Tap **Generate my plan** — the AI should either avoid that time slot for the task, or if it tried to schedule over it anyway, the proposal gets dropped and the summary text will say so (now mentioning "protected habit time" specifically, not just fixed events). A habit with no preferred time doesn't block anything positionally — it's just mentioned to the AI as something else to fit in today.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Habits→Planner increment)

Same standing sandbox limitation as every increment this session — a full project-wide `tsc --noEmit` times out here (confirmed again, the mounted-filesystem I/O issue, not a code problem). I used the same two workarounds as the last few increments: an isolated single-file `tsc --noEmit` attempt against just `planner.service.ts` (also timed out, so no new information there beyond confirming the limitation is real, not something I could dodge this time), and TypeScript's `transpileModule` API against the full changed file set (`planner.service.ts`, `planner.module.ts`, and the updated `test/app.e2e-spec.ts`), which validates syntax in about a second and came back clean (0 diagnostics) for all three. That check is syntax-only, not full type-checking, so on top of it I did a manual field-by-field comparison between what `planner.service.ts` now reads off a `Habit` (`preferredTime`, `protectedDurationMinutes`, `todayCompleted`, `title`) and the real `Habit` GraphQL model's field types in `habits/models/habit.model.ts` — they match exactly, so this shouldn't hit the enum-mismatch class of bug that's bitten a couple of earlier increments. Worth running `cd apps/backend && npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.build.json` yourself once regardless, same standing recommendation as always.

The e2e suite has a new "Habits — reaching the AI planner" block, following the same pattern as the Memory increment's prompt-capture tests: it overrides `AnthropicClient` with a fake that captures the exact prompt text `planner.service.ts` builds, then (1) creates a task and a habit whose protected time is deliberately made to overlap the task's proposed time, generates a plan, and asserts the change gets dropped with a summary that says so, and (2) asserts the captured prompt text actually contains both the protected habit (formatted as a FIXED block) and a second, no-time habit (formatted as advisory text) — so this is checking real production prompt-building and conflict-checking code, not a simulation of it. Same can't-run-here-for-Prisma-reasons caveat as every prior increment:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus the new "Habits — reaching the AI planner" block.

## Picking up the timezone auto-sync fix

No migration, no new env vars — the `updateProfile` mutation and `timezone` column already existed. Just restart the frontend (the backend doesn't even need restarting, since nothing changed there):

```bash
cd apps/web
npm run dev
```

To see it take effect: open (or refresh) any page in the app once. Within a second or two, the `TimezoneSync` component fires a `me` query, compares it to your browser's real timezone, and — if they differ — sends a real `updateProfile` mutation. There's nothing to click and nothing visibly changes on screen; the only way to confirm it worked is to check the value directly. Easiest way: open your browser's dev tools (F12), go to the Network tab, refresh the page, find the `graphql` request whose payload contains `"updateProfile"`, and look at the response — `user.timezone` should now be a real IANA zone name like `"America/Los_Angeles"` or `"Asia/Kolkata"`, not `"UTC"`. If you'd rather not dig through dev tools, just try the Habits→Planner test from before again (a habit with a preferred time a few minutes from now, plus a task) — this time the AI's stated scheduling window in the plan summary should roughly match your real wall-clock time instead of being hours off.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (timezone auto-sync)

This one's a small, low-risk change (one new component, two new GraphQL operations reusing an already-existing, already-tested mutation), so verification was lighter than a full increment: manual review of `TimezoneSync.tsx`/`Providers.tsx`/`queries.ts` against the real `me`/`updateProfile` schema (field names match exactly — `me { id timezone }`, `updateProfile(input: { timezone })`, confirmed against `users.resolver.ts` and `update-profile.input.ts`), plus a `transpileModule` syntax check on all three changed/new files (0 diagnostics on each). No new e2e test — the mutation itself already has full backend coverage from the Foundation increment, and this is purely "does the frontend call it," which is easier and more honest to verify by watching it happen once in your own browser (via the Network tab, as above) than by mocking a browser's `Intl` API in a backend e2e suite. Worth doing that one check yourself to be sure.

## Picking up the completed-tasks view

No backend changes, no migration — this increment only touched `apps/web`. Just restart the frontend:

```bash
cd apps/web
npm run dev
```

Tap **More** in the bottom nav — it's a real link now instead of a placeholder label. Complete a task or two on Today first if you haven't already, then check that they show up here with a "completed X ago" line, most recent first. If you have more than 20 completed tasks, a **Load more** button appears at the bottom.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (completed-tasks view)

Frontend-only change reusing an already-built, already-e2e-tested backend query (`tasks(status: COMPLETED, ...)` has had cross-user-isolation and pagination coverage since the original Tasks increment), so there's no new backend surface to test. I manually reviewed the new `/more/page.tsx`, the `BottomNav.tsx` edit, and the new `COMPLETED_TASKS_QUERY` against the real `TaskConnection`/`TaskEdge`/`PageInfo` schema shapes (`edges { cursor node { id title completedAt } } pageInfo { hasNextPage endCursor }`) — field names match exactly. Ran a `transpileModule` syntax check on all three changed/new files (0 diagnostics each). One thing worth knowing, not a bug: the "Load more" pagination uses Apollo Client's `fetchMore({ updateQuery })` pattern, which is deprecated (though still functional) as of the Apollo Client version this project pins — you may see a console warning about it in dev tools. It works correctly; migrating to Apollo's newer field-policy-based pagination would be a nice-to-have cleanup, not something blocking this feature. Worth clicking through **More** yourself once to confirm the list and "Load more" button look right on your machine.

## Picking up the two-way calendar sync (deletes) increment

No schema/migration changes — every column this needed (`calendarAccountId`, `externalEventId`, `source` on `calendar_events`) already existed. Restart both:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run dev

# in a second terminal
cd apps/web
npm run dev
```

**The one real step here: reconnect Google Calendar.** Your existing connection only ever consented to read-only access, and there's no way to silently upgrade that — go to `/calendar`, click **Disconnect**, then **Connect Google Calendar** again. Google's consent screen should now mention calendar *events* access (not just "view your calendar"), confirming the new scope took. If you skip this and try to delete a synced event anyway, you'll see a message telling you to reconnect rather than a silent failure — so nothing breaks either way, it just won't work until you do.

To verify: after reconnecting, delete a synced ("Google"-tagged) event from `/calendar` or Today. It should disappear immediately. Then check Google Calendar itself (in a browser or the Google Calendar app) — that event should be gone there too, not just locally. That second check is the one that actually proves the push worked; without it you'd only know the local delete succeeded, which was already true before this increment.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (two-way calendar sync)

Same standing sandbox limitation as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out here; I syntax-checked every changed/new file via `transpileModule` (0 diagnostics across all seven backend files, one frontend file, and the expanded e2e spec) and did a manual field-by-field review of the new `GoogleCalendarWriteService`/`GoogleCalendarClient.deleteEvent` against the real `CalendarAccount`/`CalendarEvent` schema.

Bigger honest gap than usual, worth flagging clearly: **this is the first Google Calendar sync code in the whole project — pull or push — that's ever had automated test coverage.** The original pull-sync increment shipped with none (confirmed while researching this one — no e2e block, no unit test for `calendar-accounts.service.ts`/`google-calendar-client.ts` existed before today). What I added this time is a new e2e block ("Two-way calendar sync — deleting a synced event") that overrides `GoogleCalendarWriteService` with a fake — same proven pattern as every `AnthropicClient` override elsewhere in this suite — and proves three things against real production code: a synced event's delete really calls out to Google before removing the local row, a simulated "read-only account" rejection returns `RECONNECT_REQUIRED` and leaves the local event untouched (no silent data loss), and a native event's delete never touches Google at all. What that override *can't* prove is whether the real HTTP call — the exact URL, method, and header shape in `GoogleCalendarClient.deleteEvent` — actually works against Google's live API, since nothing in this sandbox can reach the real internet with real credentials. I read the Calendar API v3 reference carefully to build that call, mirroring the existing (working) `listEvents` method's request shape as closely as possible, but the "delete a real synced event and confirm it's gone from actual Google Calendar" step above is the one piece of this increment only you can verify.

## Picking up the Microsoft (Outlook/365) calendar sync increment

Like Google sync, this needs a one-time setup step before anything will actually connect: register an app at [portal.azure.com](https://portal.azure.com) → **Azure Active Directory** (may show as "Microsoft Entra ID") → **App registrations** → **New registration**. When creating it:

- Under "Supported account types," choose **"Accounts in any organizational directory and personal Microsoft accounts"** — this matches the app's code, which uses Microsoft's `common` tenant so both personal (Outlook.com/Hotmail) and work/school (Office 365) accounts can connect.
- Add a **Web** platform redirect URI: `http://localhost:4000/auth/microsoft/callback`.
- After it's created, copy the **Application (client) ID** from the Overview page.
- Go to **Certificates & secrets** → **New client secret**, and copy the secret's **Value** (not its ID — that column looks similar but is the wrong one, and Azure only shows the Value once).
- Go to **API permissions** → **Add a permission** → **Microsoft Graph** → **Delegated permissions**, and add `Calendars.ReadWrite`, `User.Read`, and `offline_access` if it isn't already listed.

**If you already went through this for the original Microsoft sync increment:** your app registration currently only has `Calendars.Read` (read-only), which this push-deletes increment widens to `Calendars.ReadWrite`. Go back to your existing app in Azure Portal → **API permissions** → **Add a permission** → **Microsoft Graph** → **Delegated permissions** → add `Calendars.ReadWrite` (you can leave the old `Calendars.Read` there too, or remove it — `ReadWrite` covers everything `Read` did). No new client ID/secret needed, same app registration. After that, **you'll also need to reconnect Microsoft Calendar once from `/calendar`** — disconnect and reconnect the card — since your existing connection's tokens were only ever granted the old read-only scope; the app will tell you this via a "reconnect" message if you try to delete a synced event before doing so.

Then add these to your `.env` (see `.env.example` for the full block):

```bash
MICROSOFT_CLIENT_ID="<from Azure — Application (client) ID>"
MICROSOFT_CLIENT_SECRET="<from Azure — the secret's Value>"
MICROSOFT_REDIRECT_URI="http://localhost:4000/auth/microsoft/callback"
```

No new `TOKEN_ENCRYPTION_KEY`/`OAUTH_STATE_SECRET` needed — Microsoft sync reuses the same two you already set up for Google. Leave all three Microsoft variables blank to keep the feature off; the app boots fine either way.

No schema/migration changes — every column this needed (`syncToken`, `calendarAccountId`, `externalEventId`, the `MICROSOFT` enum values) already existed, reserved since the original Google sync increment. Just restart the backend:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run dev
```

The web app doesn't need any changes to run. Open `http://localhost:3000/calendar` — you'll see a new "Connect Microsoft Calendar" card below the Google one. Connecting redirects you through a real Microsoft sign-in and consent screen and back; once connected, your Outlook/365 events appear alongside native and Google ones within a few seconds, tagged "Microsoft."

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Microsoft calendar sync)

Same standing sandbox limitation as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out here; I syntax-checked every changed/new file via `transpileModule` (0 diagnostics across all nine backend files and three frontend files) and manually reviewed the new `MicrosoftOAuthService`/`MicrosoftCalendarClient`/`MicrosoftCalendarAccountsService` against the Microsoft Graph API v1.0 reference, and against the real `CalendarAccount`/`CalendarEvent` Prisma schema.

Same honest gap as the original Google sync increment (and the two-way-sync one after it): **the actual OAuth round trip and a real sync against a real Outlook calendar can't be exercised in this sandbox** — no live internet access with real credentials. What I *did* add is a new e2e block ("Microsoft calendar sync — pulling events") that overrides `MicrosoftCalendarClient` with a fake and proves the real `MicrosoftCalendarAccountsService.sync()` logic through the actual `syncMicrosoftCalendarNow` mutation: a synced event lands in `calendar_events` tagged `MICROSOFT` with its title and times correct, a later sync reporting that same event as `@removed` deletes it locally, and syncing with no connected account returns a clear error instead of crashing. One of those assertions is deliberately picky — it checks the exact UTC timestamp after parsing, specifically because Microsoft Graph's datetime strings don't include a timezone marker (unlike Google's, which are real ISO-8601 with an offset), so the sync code has to manually append `Z` to force UTC interpretation; getting that wrong would silently shift every synced event by however many hours your server's own timezone differs from UTC, and I wanted a test that would actually catch that class of mistake rather than just checking "an event exists."

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus "Microsoft calendar sync — pulling events." The one thing that suite can't cover, by design: connect a real account and confirm it actually works end to end — the same "reconnect Google, delete a synced event, check it's gone on Google's side" style verification we just did together, but for Microsoft. Try it once you have Azure credentials in place and tell me what you see.

## Picking up the automatic AI Memory learning increment

No new environment variables, no schema migration — this reuses the existing `ai_memory_facts` table and the `intervention_response` `factType` value that was already reserved for it. Just restart the backend:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run dev
```

The web app doesn't need any changes to run, and there's no new screen to look at — this one's invisible by design, the same way the timezone auto-sync fix was. To actually see it working:

1. Generate a plan (**Generate my plan** on Today) and either **Accept** or **Reject** it. Do this at least 3 times total (any mix of accept/reject — but see the next step for how to get the two clear-pattern facts specifically).
2. To see the "often rejects" fact: **Reject** at least 3 plan proposals in a row (out of your last 10 responses). To see the "consistently accepts" fact instead: **Accept** every one of your last 3+ responses.
3. Open `/chat` and ask the AI something like "what do you know about how I respond to your plans?" — if a pattern fact was written, it'll show up in the AI's answer, since chat (like the planner) now pulls this into its prompt automatically.

There's no UI to directly view the fact's raw text (by design — it's not on `/memory`, see above), so asking the AI or checking a freshly-generated plan's tone is the way to confirm it's working.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (automatic AI Memory learning)

Same standing sandbox limitation as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out here; I syntax-checked both changed files (`memory.service.ts`, `planner.service.ts`) via `transpileModule` (0 diagnostics), and double-checked the Prisma upsert's compound-key name (`userId_factType_key`) against the actual `@@unique([userId, factType, key])` constraint in `schema.prisma` to make sure it'd compile against the real generated client, not just look plausible.

Three new e2e blocks exercise the real `refreshInterventionResponsePattern` logic through the actual `respondToPlanRun` mutation, using the same `AnthropicClient`-override-and-capture-the-prompt pattern as every other prompt-grounding test in this file: responding to only 2 plan runs writes no fact and the next prompt contains neither pattern sentence; responding REJECT three times in a row produces "Often rejects proposed daily plans (3 of the last 3)" in the next captured prompt; responding ACCEPT three times in a row (with a fake proposal that schedules nothing, so the same task stays open and reusable) produces "Consistently accepts proposed daily plans (all of the last 3)." Run them with:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus the three new "Automatic AI Memory learning" blocks. What I couldn't verify here: I picked the reject-rate threshold (≥50%) and sample size (last 10, minimum 3) as reasonable defaults, not something tuned against real usage data — if it ends up feeling too trigger-happy or too slow to notice a real pattern once you've been using this for a while, that's a tuning conversation, not a bug.

No `.env` changes, no schema migration — every column this needed already existed.

Restart the backend:


Then, in the app itself: open `/calendar`, and if you already have Microsoft connected, **disconnect and reconnect it once** — your existing connection's tokens were only ever granted the old `Calendars.Read` scope, so a delete attempt before reconnecting will show a "Reconnect Microsoft Calendar above..." message rather than silently failing. A brand-new connection gets write access automatically. Once reconnected, deleting a Microsoft-synced event (tap **Remove** on it, same button as any event) should make it disappear both here and in Outlook — check Outlook directly to confirm it's really gone there too, same "trust but verify" check we did for Google's two-way sync.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Microsoft push-deletes)

Same standing sandbox limitation as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` still times out here (confirmed again this round, ~40+ seconds with no output before the timeout); I syntax-checked every changed/new file via `transpileModule` (0 diagnostics across all six backend files and the one frontend file), and manually cross-checked the type-sensitive spots specifically because a real type error slipped past `transpileModule` earlier this session (the `StoredValue` cast in `memory.service.ts`, caught by your own editor's `tsc --noEmit` and fixed) — so this time I also confirmed `CalendarEventSource`'s actual Prisma enum values (`NATIVE`/`GOOGLE`/`MICROSOFT`/`APPLE`) match the string literals `calendar.service.ts`'s new dispatch logic compares against, rather than assuming.

New e2e block, "Microsoft calendar sync — deleting a synced event," mirrors the Google two-way-sync tests exactly (both `MicrosoftCalendarWriteService` and `GoogleCalendarWriteService` are faked, so no live network calls): deleting a Microsoft-synced event pushes to the fake Microsoft service first, then removes it locally; a fake `RECONNECT_REQUIRED` failure leaves the event intact; deleting a native event never touches either fake; and — specific to this increment's new dispatch logic — deleting a *Google*-sourced event calls only the Google fake and never the Microsoft one, guarding against the new source-based routing accidentally sending every synced event down the wrong path. Run them with:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus "Microsoft calendar sync — deleting a synced event." Same honest gap as every OAuth-dependent increment: the real round trip against a live Outlook account can't be exercised in this sandbox — reconnect and delete a real synced event yourself (steps above) and let me know what you see.

Also worth knowing: while building this I found and fixed a small pre-existing bug, not something this increment introduced — `CalendarEventRow.tsx`'s "synced from" badge and its reconnect message were both hardcoded to say "Google" no matter which provider an event actually synced from, so every Microsoft-synced event has been mislabeled "Google" in the UI since Microsoft sync first shipped. Both now read the event's real `source` field.

## Picking up the focus sessions increment

This one needs a real schema migration — a brand-new `focus_sessions` table, not a reuse of existing columns like most recent increments. From `apps/backend`:

That first command creates the migration file (from the `schema.prisma` changes already made) and applies it to your local Postgres — same as every other schema change this project has needed, and completely standard Prisma usage. No new environment variables.

Two ways to try it: open `/today` and click **Focus** next to any task (pre-fills that task and takes you to the timer), or click the small **Start a focus session →** link near the top of Today for a standalone session with no task attached. Pick a duration, hit Start, and you should see a live countdown. **Complete** or **Cancel** it, then check that it shows up under "Recent sessions" on the same page with the right status.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (focus sessions)

Same standing sandbox limitation as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out here, and this round I also couldn't run `npx prisma validate` (blocked by the same `binaries.prisma.sh` fetch restriction noted in "Before you run anything" — not a new problem, the same one that's applied since the very first migration). I syntax-checked every new/changed file via `transpileModule` (0 diagnostics across seven backend files and four frontend files) and manually checked `schema.prisma` for brace balance and that the new `FocusSession.task` relation has a matching back-reference on `Task` (Prisma requires both sides) — but the migration itself (the thing `prisma migrate dev` generates) is unverified until you run it, same honest gap as `prisma generate` always has been in this sandbox.

New e2e suite, "Focus sessions": starting a task-linked session and completing it shows up correctly in `recentFocusSessions` with the right task title and status; starting a session with no task at all leaves `taskId`/`taskTitle` null rather than erroring; starting a second session while one's already active is refused with an `ALREADY_ACTIVE` error (and the first session is left running, untouched); trying to complete or cancel a session that's already ended returns `NOT_ACTIVE` rather than silently rewriting history; and a second identity can't start a session linked to someone else's task. Run them with:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus "Focus sessions." One design note worth knowing: this whole domain isn't in the approved Database Design Document at all — a real gap in that document, not something I deferred on purpose — so I designed the table and API fresh, following the same conventions everything else here uses, rather than working from a pre-approved spec like every other increment so far.

## Picking up the Apple (CalDAV) calendar sync increment

Another real schema change — a new nullable `calendar_url` column on the existing `calendar_accounts` table (not a new table this time):

```bash
cd apps/backend
npx prisma migrate dev --name add_apple_calendar_url
npm run dev
```

Unlike Google/Microsoft, there's no cloud console app to register and no client ID/secret to add to `.env` — Apple's CalDAV sync doesn't use OAuth at all. What you do need is an **app-specific password** (Apple's term — a one-off password scoped just to this app, not your real Apple ID password):

1. Go to [appleid.apple.com](https://appleid.apple.com) and sign in with the Apple ID whose calendar you want to sync.
2. Under **Sign-In and Security**, find **App-Specific Passwords** and click it (you may need two-factor authentication enabled on the account first — Apple requires it for this).
3. Click **Generate an app-specific password**, give it a label like "AI Life Manager," and copy the password it shows you (a string like `abcd-efgh-ijkl-mnop`) — Apple only shows it once.

Then, in the app itself: open `/calendar`, find the new "Apple Calendar" card, click **Connect Apple Calendar**, and enter your Apple ID (the email) and the app-specific password you just generated (not your real Apple ID password) into the form. If it connects, your iCloud calendar's events should show up alongside native/Google/Microsoft ones within a few seconds, tagged "Apple."

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Apple calendar sync)

Same standing sandbox limitation as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out here, and `npx prisma validate`/`prisma generate` are blocked by the same `binaries.prisma.sh` fetch restriction as every prior migration. I syntax-checked every new/changed file via `transpileModule` (0 diagnostics across nine backend files and three frontend files).

This increment has a bigger honest gap than any calendar-sync increment so far, worth being direct about: not only can the real network round trip against a live iCloud account not be exercised here (same gap Google/Microsoft/Apple's own OAuth-based cousins all share), but **the XML request-building and response-parsing logic in `apple-caldav-client.ts` itself is entirely unverified against a real Apple CalDAV server response** — the e2e tests (below) fake `AppleCaldavClient` at the method level (`discoverPrincipal`, `listEvents`, etc. return canned values directly), which proves `AppleCalendarAccountsService`'s orchestration logic is correct, but not that the actual XML this client sends is well-formed CalDAV or that the regex-based parsing correctly handles whatever exact multistatus shape `caldav.icloud.com` really returns. I did write and run (successfully, standalone — no DB or network involved) a real unit test suite for the one piece that's pure, testable logic regardless of a live server: `ics-parser.spec.ts`, covering UID/SUMMARY/DTSTART/DTEND extraction, ICS text-escaping, folded-line unfolding, and — most importantly, matching the exact class of bug the Microsoft sync increment's own test guarded against — that a UTC-suffixed (`Z`) datetime parses to the exact right instant while a bare local datetime is interpreted in your configured timezone rather than silently assumed to be UTC. That test suite could not actually be executed in this sandbox either (`npx jest` hangs indefinitely here — traced to `@prisma/client` never having been generated, the same root cause as the Prisma binary-download restriction, cascading into `ts-jest`'s type-checking); I verified it via the same `transpileModule` syntax check as everything else, so the test logic itself is unexecuted, not just the live-server integration. This is genuinely the least-verified increment of this whole project — connecting a real Apple ID and confirming events actually sync correctly (titles, times, especially any non-UTC-timezone events) is the single most important thing to check yourself here, more so than usual.

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus "Apple (CalDAV) calendar sync": connecting discovers the calendar and syncs an initial event correctly tagged `APPLE`; a later sync reporting that event as removed deletes it locally; disconnecting cleans up; syncing with no connected account returns a clear `SYNC_FAILED` rather than crashing; and Apple rejecting the credentials during connect returns `APPLE_AUTH_FAILED` with no account left behind. If your own sandbox can run Jest normally (most local machines can — this hanging issue is specific to this build sandbox's blocked Prisma binary download), you can also run the pure-logic suite directly:

```bash
npx jest ics-parser
```

## Picking up the task duration estimation increment

No new tables and no migration needed — this increment is entirely new behavior on top of the `Task.estimatedDurationMinutes`/`actualDurationMinutes` columns that already existed:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run dev
```

No new environment variables — the AI estimate reuses the same `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`/`ANTHROPIC_MODEL` every other AI feature in this app already uses, and simply leaves the duration field for manual entry if it's not configured. On Today, try adding a task with a duration typed in manually, and separately try the **AI** button next to the duration field (needs a real Anthropic key) — it should fill the field with a suggested number you can still edit before saving. Then check off a task: instead of completing immediately, you should see an inline "How long did this actually take?" prompt — try both entering a number and hitting **Skip**, both should complete the task. To see the "improves over time" half actually working, complete three or more tasks in a row with both a set estimate and a real actual time that's consistently off (say, actual is 1.5x the estimate each time) — the next AI estimate you request afterward should come back adjusted, since the model's prompt now includes your real accuracy pattern.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (task duration estimation)

Same standing sandbox limitations as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out, and `prisma generate`/`validate` are blocked by the binary-download restriction (moot for this one specifically, since there's no schema change, but the general Jest-hangs-in-this-sandbox limitation still applies). I syntax-checked all nine new/changed files via `transpileModule` (0 diagnostics), and specifically checked for a module cycle before choosing where the AI estimate call lives: `PlannerModule` already imports `TasksModule`, so a naive `TasksModule` importing `PlannerModule` back (for `AnthropicClient`, following the usual Reflection/Routines pattern) would have been circular — the estimate call lives on `PlannerService` instead, specifically to avoid that, while the (separate, one-directional) `task_duration_accuracy` memory-fact refresh still lives on `TasksService` importing `MemoryModule`, which has no such conflict.

Six new e2e tests across two suites. With a fake AI configured: a valid numeric reply is validated and returned; a non-numeric reply and an out-of-range reply (9999 minutes) both come back `null` rather than being trusted as-is; and — the one that actually proves the "learns over time" claim end to end, not just that the two halves exist separately — completing three tasks at a consistent 1.5x actual-vs-estimated ratio is shown to make the very next `estimateTaskDuration` prompt actually contain the real `1.5x` figure, captured directly from the fake client's received prompt. With AI unconfigured: `estimateTaskDuration` returns `null` immediately, asserted by making the fake `sendMessage` throw if it's ever called at all — proving the short-circuit, not just the outcome. Run them with:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus "Task duration estimation — AI configured" and "Task duration estimation — AI not configured." No live-network gap to call out — same already-verified Anthropic pattern as every prior AI increment.

Try **×** on one to dismiss it, and **Refresh** to regenerate the whole set.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (AI recommendations)

Same standing sandbox limitations as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out, and `prisma generate`/`validate` are blocked by the binary-download restriction. I syntax-checked all eleven new/changed files via `transpileModule` (0 diagnostics), and specifically checked `RecommendationsModule`'s import list for the same module-cycle risk the Task duration estimation increment ran into: `RecommendationsModule` imports `TasksModule`/`CalendarModule`/`SignalsModule`/`HabitsModule`/`MemoryModule` directly (mirroring `PlannerModule`'s own import list) plus `PlannerModule` itself only for its exported `AnthropicClient` — safe, since none of those modules import `RecommendationsModule` back.

Six new e2e tests across two suites. With a fake AI configured: a well-formed two-line response produces exactly two validated recommendations, and `todayRecommendations` returns the same set back; a response mixing valid lines with an invalid category and a line with no `|` separator at all is proven to drop only the bad lines, not fail outright; dismissing one recommendation is shown to mark only that one `dismissed: true`, leaving the other untouched; and regenerating the same day is shown to fully replace the previous set — a previously-dismissed item's id is confirmed gone entirely after a second `generateRecommendations` call, not just re-marked not-dismissed. With AI unconfigured: `generateRecommendations` returns a clear `AI_NOT_CONFIGURED` error (asserted via a fake `sendMessage` that throws if ever called, proving the short-circuit) and `todayRecommendations` stays `null` since nothing was ever written. Run them with:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites plus "AI recommendations — AI configured" and "AI recommendations — AI not configured." No live-network gap here either — same already-verified Anthropic integration pattern as every other AI feature in this app.

## Picking up the Scheduler / reminders increment

One new dependency, no schema change, no migration:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm install
npm run dev
```

(`npm install` picks up the new `@nestjs/schedule` entry in `package.json` — nothing else changed in the schema, so no `prisma generate`/`migrate` needed this time.) No new environment variables either — every reminder rides through the exact same `NotificationsService.create` path plan/recommendation notifications already use, so it respects the same quiet hours and push toggle automatically.

This one's hard to demo live end to end, on purpose — real usefulness here means firing at 8am/8pm/9pm local or a habit's own preferred time, not on demand. Three ways to actually see it work:

1. **Wait for a real window.** If it's genuinely close to 8am, 8pm, or 9pm your local time, set up an incomplete morning/evening routine or skip today's reflection, leave the backend running, and check `/notifications` within about 15 minutes of the hour turning over.
2. **Set a habit's `preferredTime` to a few minutes in the past** (e.g. if it's 2:15pm, create a habit with `preferredTime: "14:00"`) and leave it unchecked — wait up to 15 minutes for the next sweep, then check `/notifications` for a `habit_reminder` (15–120 minutes overdue is the window, so this only works between about 2:15 and 4:00pm for a 2:00pm habit).
3. **The honest, fastest way (what the e2e suite itself does):** call `SchedulerService.checkRemindersForUser(userId, timezone)` directly rather than waiting on the real cron — e.g. from a throwaway script or the Nest REPL — passing whatever synthetic timezone puts "now" inside the window you want to see fire.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Scheduler / reminders)

Same standing sandbox limitations as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out, `prisma generate`/`validate` are blocked by the binary-download restriction, and `@nestjs/schedule` itself was never actually installed or run in this sandbox (network access to the npm registry worked for a version lookup, but I didn't install it into the real project — that's what your own `npm install` above does). I syntax-checked all seven new/changed files (`scheduler.service.ts`, `scheduler.module.ts`, `routines.service.ts`'s new `isCompleteToday` method, `app.module.ts`, `package.json`, the e2e spec, and this README) via `transpileModule`, then re-ran the full backend sweep afterward (159 files, 0 diagnostics). I double-checked the module-cycle reasoning before wiring `SchedulerModule` up: it imports `RoutinesModule`, `ReflectionModule`, `HabitsModule`, and `NotificationsModule` — all four already-existing "leaf" modules relative to this one, none of which have any reason to ever import a scheduler back, so there's no cycle risk regardless of how many more reminder types get added later. I also deliberately gave `RoutinesService` a new lightweight `isCompleteToday` method rather than having the scheduler reuse the existing `getTodayFor`/`listTodayForUser` — those call the AI to re-sequence an `aiSequenced` routine on every read, which is right for a person opening Today but would mean a real Claude API call for every AI-sequenced routine, every 15 minutes, for every active user, just to check a completion count.

Three new e2e tests in a new "Scheduler / reminder sweep" suite, each calling `SchedulerService.checkRemindersForUser` directly (bypassing the real 15-minute cron entirely — nothing waits 15 minutes in a test) against a synthetic fixed-offset timezone computed so "now," read in that zone, lands at whatever local time each assertion needs, regardless of the real wall-clock time the suite happens to run at: the morning-routine reminder fires when incomplete inside its window and doesn't fire a second time once complete; the evening-routine and reflection reminders each respect their own separate windows and stay silent outside them (including a check that firing one doesn't also fire the other); and habit reminders only fire within the 15–120-minute overdue range, with two independently-overdue habits on the same account correctly getting two separate tracked notifications rather than colliding into one. Getting the timezone math right here mattered more than usual — see the suite's own `timezoneForLocalTime` comment for a real bug I caught and fixed before it ever became a flaky test: dev-auth accounts always have `user.timezone` fixed at `"UTC"` (real accounts sync a real browser timezone, dev-auth never does), so every completion mutation the tests call stores its "today" against the real UTC calendar date — an earlier draft of the synthetic-timezone helper could land on a *different* calendar date than UTC depending on the real time the suite ran at, which would have made the reminder logic look up the wrong day's data and report a false "incomplete" intermittently. The fixed version constrains the synthetic offset so its calendar date always matches today's real UTC date, not just the target clock time. Run the suite with:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites, unchanged, plus "Scheduler / reminder sweep." No live-network gap — none of the reminder paths call Anthropic.

Creating a new calendar event does the same thing. If you complete a second task inside that 10-minute window, nothing new happens — that's the cooldown working, not a bug.

Same standing sandbox limitations as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out here, and `@nestjs/event-emitter` was never actually installed or run in this sandbox (only version-checked against the npm registry, same as `@nestjs/schedule` before it) — that's what your own `npm install` above does. I deliberately scoped the calendar trigger to native `create`/`update`/`delete` only, not the sync-writer methods (`upsertFromExternalSource`/`deleteByExternalId`) a Google/Microsoft/Apple pull uses, so one "Sync now" pulling in a dozen events can't burst a dozen AI calls — the cooldown would absorb most of that burst anyway, but skipping it at the source is simpler than relying on the cooldown to paper over it.

A new "Automatic AI re-planning" e2e suite (four tests) and a sibling "— AI not configured" suite (one test) cover this, using the same fake-`AnthropicClient`-override pattern every other AI-backed suite in this project already uses: completing a task via the real `completeTask` mutation eventually shows up as a new plan run with `triggerEvent: 'auto_task_completed'` (polled for, since `eventEmitter.emit` is genuinely fire-and-forget — there's no promise to await from outside it); creating a calendar event the same way produces `'auto_calendar_changed'`; calling `PlannerService.maybeAutoReplan` twice in quick succession (made `public` specifically so tests can call it directly, same pattern as `SchedulerService.checkRemindersForUser`) produces only one plan run, proving the cooldown; and an account with zero open tasks is a silent no-op, not an error. Run it with:

### Post-ship bug fix: "Cannot return null for non-nullable field PlanDiff.summary"

Found live, in a real local run of the flow described above, after this increment first shipped — not something the e2e suite had caught, since every fake `AnthropicClient` override in the test suite always returned a well-formed `summary`. Root cause: `requestReplan` already treated every proposed *change* from Anthropic as fully untrusted input (re-validated against real data before anything was kept — see the policy layer above), but trusted the response's top-level `summary` text blindly, writing it straight into the database's non-nullable `PlanDiff.summary` field. If a real API response ever came back with a missing, empty, or malformed `summary` — a truncated tool-use call being the most likely real-world cause — that bad value got silently persisted, and the crash only surfaced later, when that plan run was read back on a page load, not when it was generated (which made it look unrelated to whatever action actually caused it).

Fixed in `planner.service.ts`: `proposal.summary` is now validated the same way `proposal.changes` already was — a non-empty string is used as-is, anything else (missing, empty, or whitespace-only) falls back to a generic `"Plan updated."` rather than ever reaching the database unvalidated. Covered by a new regression test in the "AI daily planning — configured (e2e)" suite that feeds a fake `AnthropicClient` a missing, empty, and whitespace-only summary in turn and asserts `requestReplan` still succeeds with the fallback text rather than erroring. Syntax-verified afterward (160 files, 0 diagnostics).

If you were seeing this error before pulling this fix: it should be gone on your next `npm run dev` restart. One thing worth knowing — the *already-broken* plan run that triggered your crash is still sitting in your database with a bad `summary`; you won't hit it again once a newer, valid plan run takes its place as "latest" (tap **Generate my plan** once, or complete a task/add a calendar event to trigger an automatic one), but it's not retroactively cleaned up.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Editing a proposed AI plan)

Same standing sandbox limitations as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out here, and (same as every increment since the AI daily planning one) `prisma generate`/`migrate` against a real Postgres database couldn't be run in this sandbox, though this increment doesn't actually need either since nothing in the schema changed. I syntax-checked every changed/new file (`plan-change.model.ts`, `ai-plan-run.model.ts`, `plan-change-edit.input.ts`, `planner.service.ts`, `planner.resolver.ts`, the e2e spec, `AiPlanCard.tsx`, `WeeklyPlanCard.tsx`, `datetime-local.ts`, `queries.ts`) via `transpileModule`, then re-ran the full combined backend+frontend sweep afterward (204 files, 0 diagnostics). I deliberately kept `requestReplan`'s own already-shipped, already-tested policy-layer logic completely untouched rather than refactoring it to share code with the new EDIT path — `computeImmovableIntervals` (the new helper the EDIT branch uses to re-derive fixed events/protected habit time) duplicates a meaningful chunk of what's already inline in `requestReplan`, accepted deliberately as the lower-risk choice over touching a working, tested code path for an unrelated feature. Every per-change `id` is a real UUID generated once at plan-generation time (`randomUUID() as string` — same branded-`crypto.UUID`-type cast the recommendations increment's real bug fix already established the pattern for); a plan generated before this increment has no `id` on its stored changes at all, so `hydrate()` backfills a `legacy-<index>` placeholder that keeps the GraphQL field non-null without pretending it's a real, edit-targetable id — editing simply can't reach a change from a plan that predates this shipping, only the account's own next freshly-generated plan.

New e2e coverage in the existing "AI daily planning — configured (e2e)" suite: editing a change to a valid new time moves it and writes that exact time onto the real task (not the AI's original suggestion); editing a change to a time that overlaps the suite's fixed calendar event leaves it at its original time instead of applying the bad edit, with the summary explaining why; and removing a change drops it from the plan without touching whatever schedule the affected task already had (proven by capturing the task's schedule immediately before the remove-only edit and asserting it's unchanged after, not asserting a specific value — since by that point in the suite the task had already been scheduled by earlier tests). A regression test also covers the separate `PlanDiff.summary` crash fixed above (missing/empty/whitespace-only AI response summary now falls back to a generic string instead of crashing). Run everything with:

```bash

npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites unchanged, with the new assertions living inside "AI daily planning — configured (e2e)" rather than a new suite of its own, since they're testing the same already-running app instance and fake `AnthropicClient` that suite already sets up.

### Post-ship bug fix: proposed times silently dropped for accounts not on a UTC server

Found live, testing this exact increment on a real account with a real Anthropic API key — a plan generated for a completely empty test account (no calendar events, no habits, a single open task) still came back with every suggestion skipped, which shouldn't have been possible with nothing left to conflict with. Root cause, in the original AI daily planning increment's `requestReplan`, not anything from this increment: `new Date(change.proposedStart)` parsed Anthropic's raw response directly, and the tool schema only ever said "ISO 8601 datetime" — it never actually required a UTC offset. When a real response omits one (which happened here), JavaScript's `Date` constructor parses an offset-less datetime string as local time *to wherever the Node process itself is running*, not local time to the person using the app. On a server not itself set to the user's timezone (the ordinary case — this project's own dev-auth accounts default to `UTC`, and any real deployed server commonly does too), that silently shifts the intended instant by however many hours separate the two zones — enough, for a US timezone, to push a "this afternoon" proposal to *before* "now" from the server's point of view, which the (correctly-working) policy layer then correctly rejected as invalid. The proposal wasn't actually broken; the server was misreading what time it described.

Fixed with a new `parseAiDateTime` helper in `planner.service.ts`: a string that already carries explicit UTC/offset info (a trailing `Z` or a numeric `+HH:mm`/`-HH:mm`) is trusted as an absolute instant, same as before; anything else is now interpreted as local wall-clock time in the *user's real stored timezone* via Luxon — the same pattern `habitProtectedInterval` already used for a habit's `preferredTime`, just extended to cover the AI's own response too. The tool schema's `proposedStart` description was also tightened to explicitly ask for a plain local datetime with no offset, so this is the expected case going forward, not just a fallback being papered over — though the fix works correctly either way, regardless of what the model actually sends back.

Covered by a new "AI daily planning — timezone-naive proposed times (e2e)" suite: a real (non-`UTC`) account timezone, `America/New_York`, with a fake `AnthropicClient` response deliberately formatted as an offset-less local datetime string — asserts the resulting change survives the policy layer and lands within seconds of the intended real-world instant, not hours off. This is the one bug in this project so far that every prior e2e suite's fake `AnthropicClient` responses structurally couldn't have caught: every one of them builds its fake `proposedStart` with `.toISOString()`, which always carries a trailing `Z` — exactly the one shape immune to this bug. Syntax-verified afterward (161 backend files, 0 diagnostics).

If you were seeing plans come back with everything skipped even on an account with nothing to conflict with: restart the backend (`npm run dev` in `apps/backend`) to pick up the fix — no install, no migration needed.

### Post-ship bug fix #2: DAY-scope plans never told the AI today's actual date

Found immediately after the fix above, in the same live testing session, on the same real account — plans kept coming back with everything skipped even after that fix. Turned on temporary diagnostic logging (see below) to find out why, and the real answer was stranger than a parsing bug: the model's proposed date was **over a year in the past** (`2025-05-27` for a request made on `2026-08-04`). Root cause, also in the original AI daily planning increment, and also nothing to do with the Editing or Automatic re-planning increments: `buildPrompt`'s DAY-scope intro line only ever stated the current *time* (`HH:mm`) — never the actual calendar date. The WEEK/MONTH branch of that same function always has included the date; DAY scope's, written earlier and for a same-day-only plan, apparently seemed not to need it — reasonable when `proposedStart` used to implicitly mean "today," but not once the tool schema requires a full date+time string (see bug fix #1 above), at which point a model given only a time and no date has nothing to go on but a guess. Bug fix #1 was real and worth keeping, but it wasn't *the* bug causing this specific symptom — the timestamp was landing over a year in the past regardless of which timezone it got interpreted in.

Fixed by stating today's real date explicitly in the DAY-scope prompt — both up front ("Today's date is 2026-08-04 (Tuesday)...") and again right next to the instruction for what `proposedStart` must contain, so there's no date left to guess. Also added permanent diagnostic logging in `requestReplan`'s validation loop (a `this.logger.warn` for every dropped change, spelling out exactly which of the five possible reasons applied — unknown task, unparseable time, before "now," after the plan window, outside working hours, or a real overlap) — previously a dropped change was silent besides the generic UI summary text, which made this bug take much longer to actually pin down than it should have; now it's a `Dropped AI-proposed change for taskId "...": ...` line in the backend's own console the moment it happens.

Covered by a new "AI daily planning — DAY-scope prompt states the real date (e2e)" suite, asserting the literal prompt text sent to the model contains today's real date — the most direct test of the actual root cause, since a fake `AnthropicClient` can't meaningfully simulate "a model guessing the wrong date" the way a real one can. Syntax-verified afterward (161 backend files, 0 diagnostics). If your plans were coming back with unusually old-looking or otherwise implausible proposed dates: restart the backend to pick up this fix, same as above.


Try the 2 weeks / 30 days / 90 days toggle at the top — the charts and streak cards recompute for whichever window you pick. If you have no data at all yet, the page shows honest empty states rather than blank-looking charts.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Life analytics)

Same standing sandbox limitations as every increment — full-project `tsc --noEmit` times out here, and `prisma generate`/`migrate` against a real Postgres database couldn't be run in this sandbox, though this increment doesn't need either since nothing in the schema changed. I syntax-checked every new/changed file (`analytics.module.ts`, `analytics.service.ts`, `analytics.resolver.ts`, `models/analytics-summary.model.ts`, the three domain services' new range-query methods, `app.module.ts`, `queries.ts`, `TrendChart.tsx`, `analytics/page.tsx`, `today/page.tsx`, the e2e spec) via `transpileModule`, then re-ran the full combined backend+frontend sweep afterward (210 files, 0 diagnostics). I double-checked the module-cycle reasoning before wiring `AnalyticsModule` up, same discipline as every other module addition in this project: it imports `SignalsModule`, `HabitsModule`, and `RoutinesModule` purely to read from their services, and nothing else in the app has any reason to import `AnalyticsModule` back, so it's a pure leaf consumer — the one wrinkle worth naming is that `RoutinesModule` itself already imports `PlannerModule` (for `AnthropicClient`, used by AI-sequenced routines), so `AnalyticsModule` transitively depends on `PlannerModule` too; since `PlannerModule` has no reason to ever import `AnalyticsModule` back either, this is still just a longer chain, not a cycle. Every `@db.Date` column this increment reads (`habit_logs.scheduled_date`, `routine_logs.scheduled_date`, `sleep_entries.sleep_date`) is compared using the same UTC-midnight-anchored `Date` construction `toDateOnly` already established in three other files — getting this wrong (using an arbitrary local-midnight instant instead) would have silently miscompared near any timezone that isn't UTC, the same category of bug fix #1 above just fixed for a different code path, so it got extra attention here specifically because of that.

Six new e2e tests in a new "Life analytics (e2e)" suite: a completely empty account gets a real per-day point for every day in the window with every value null (not an error, not an empty array); same-day mood/energy check-ins are correctly averaged into one point and sleep is correctly bucketed by date; a habit's streak and completion rate are correct and correctly clamped to when the habit was actually created (backdated directly via `PrismaService`, since `createHabit` always stamps `createdAt = now()` with no way to override it through the API); a routine's consistency is computed the same way, using its current checklist length; the `days` argument is clamped into the allowed 7-90 range rather than trusted as-given; and one account's data never leaks into another's. Run with:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run test:e2e
```

You should see the existing suites unchanged, plus "Life analytics (e2e)."

### Post-ship bug fix: routines couldn't be saved at all ("We couldn't save that routine. Try again.")

Found live, testing the Insights increment above — logging a habit/routine to generate real data for the new charts, which is how a genuine pre-existing bug in routines (nothing to do with Insights) surfaced. Every `setRoutine` and `setTodayRoutineCompletion` call failed with a Prisma `Argument \`type\` is missing` error, no matter what was sent. The resolver's `catch {}` blocks were bare (no logging at all), so the real error was invisible until logging was added.

Root cause: `SetRoutineInput.type` and `SetTodayRoutineCompletionInput.type` each had only a GraphQL `@Field(() => RoutineType)` decorator, no class-validator decorator. The app's global `ValidationPipe({ whitelist: true })` (`main.ts`) strips any input property that has no class-validator decorator at all, regardless of what the client sends — so `type` was silently deleted from the request before it ever reached the resolver. Every other enum-typed input field in this codebase already pairs `@Field` with a class-validator decorator like `@IsIn([...])` (established in `CreateHabitInput.frequency`); these two were the only fields missing it, confirmed by grepping every `.input.ts` file for the same pattern.

Fixed by adding `@IsIn([RoutineType.MORNING, RoutineType.EVENING])` to both fields. Also added real error logging (`this.logger.error(...)`) to all three previously-bare `catch {}` blocks in `RoutinesResolver`, so a genuine failure here is never silent again. Syntax-verified afterward (164 backend files, 0 diagnostics).

No new e2e test needed: the existing "setRoutine creates a routine..." and "setTodayRoutineCompletion fully replaces..." tests already send `type` and already assert on it — they were structurally correct all along and simply couldn't have been exercised against a real server in this sandbox before now. They now serve as this fix's regression coverage. If you were seeing "We couldn't save that routine": restart the backend to pick up the fix.
