## Picking up the PWA + offline support increment

One new dependency (`apollo3-cache-persist`, already installed into `apps/web`) — no schema change, no migration, no new environment variable.

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run dev

# in a second terminal
cd apps/web
npm run dev
```

This one genuinely needs a real browser to verify, not just a syntax check — service workers, the install prompt, and offline mode are all browser runtime behavior this sandbox has no way to exercise itself (there's no browser available to this environment, only Node execution). Here's exactly what to check yourself, in Chrome or Edge:

1. **Installable:** open the app, look for an install icon in the address bar (desktop Chrome/Edge) or "Add to Home Screen" in the browser menu (mobile). Install it — it should open in its own standalone window/icon, not a browser tab.
2. **Service worker registered:** DevTools → Application tab → Service Workers. You should see `sw.js` listed as activated and running.
3. **App shell loads offline:** visit `/today`, `/tasks`, and `/journal` once while online (so the service worker caches them), then in DevTools → Network tab, check "Offline," and reload each of those three pages. They should still load (the page shell, not necessarily fresh data).
4. **Offline data + the three offline-capable actions:** while still online, load Today and Journal at least once (so Apollo's persisted cache has something to work with). Check "Offline" in DevTools, then: add a task from Today, complete an existing task, and write a journal entry. Each should appear immediately in the UI, and you should see a small "N changes waiting to sync" strip appear. Uncheck "Offline" — within a few seconds the strip should disappear and the changes should be confirmed as real (reload to prove they actually reached the server, not just the local cache).
5. To see the "couldn't be saved" path: this only happens if a queued item is genuinely rejected by the server once it's replayed (a real validation failure) — not easy to trigger deliberately, safe to skip unless you're specifically testing that code path.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (PWA + offline support)

Same standing sandbox limitations as every increment, plus a new one specific to this increment: browser runtime behavior (service worker registration/caching, the install prompt, actual offline network simulation) cannot be exercised by this sandbox's supertest-based e2e harness at all — that harness talks to the backend over HTTP and has never had, and still doesn't have, any browser automation in it. Syntax-checked every new/changed file (`layout.tsx`, `PwaRegister.tsx`, `apollo-client.ts`, `Providers.tsx`, `offlineQueue.ts`, `SyncManager.tsx`, `OfflineSyncBanner.tsx`, `QuickAddTask.tsx`, `TaskRow.tsx`, `journal/page.tsx`, `today/page.tsx`) via `transpileModule`, then re-ran the full combined backend+test+frontend sweep afterward (217 files, 0 diagnostics). No new backend e2e tests — nothing in this increment touches the backend at all; every mutation the offline queue eventually replays (`createTask`, `completeTask`, `createJournalEntry`) is the exact same existing, already-tested mutation, called from a queue instead of directly. The honest gap: there is no automated test of the offline behavior itself in this project yet (would need a browser-automation framework like Playwright, which doesn't exist here) — the manual verification steps above are the only current way to confirm this increment actually works, and are the right next investment if this app is heading toward a real launch.

## Picking up the Real notification delivery increment

This one needs a real schema change (a new `PushSubscription` table, plus a `deliveredAt` column on `Notification`) and two new environment variables:

```bash
cd apps/backend
npm run prisma:generate
npm run prisma:migrate       # creates push_subscriptions, adds notifications.delivered_at
npm install                  # picks up the new web-push dependency if you haven't already
npm run dev

# in a second terminal
cd apps/web
npm run dev
```

Two real dev secrets are already filled in for you in `apps/backend/.env` so web push works out of the box locally:

- `VAPID_PUBLIC_KEY` / `VAPID_PRIVATE_KEY` — a real, working key pair generated for this project via `web-push`'s own `generateVAPIDKeys()`. Fine for local dev; generate your own pair for a real deployment with `npx web-push generate-vapid-keys` (run from `apps/backend`) and swap both values.
- `RESEND_API_KEY` — left blank on purpose, so email delivery is a safe no-op until you set it. Create a free account at [resend.com](https://resend.com), generate an API key, and set both `RESEND_API_KEY` and `EMAIL_FROM_ADDRESS` (a verified sending address/domain in your Resend account) to turn real email on.

The frontend needs no new environment variable at all — it fetches the VAPID public key live via a `vapidPublicKey` GraphQL query rather than a baked-in `NEXT_PUBLIC_` copy, so the two can never drift out of sync if the key is ever rotated.

To verify push yourself (this is real browser runtime behavior, same category of thing as the PWA increment above — this sandbox can't click an "Allow notifications" browser prompt):

1. Open `/notifications`, click **"Turn on browser notifications,"** and accept the permission prompt. The button should flip to "Turn off browser notifications."
2. Trigger any notification (e.g. generate a plan from Today, or complete/skip a habit near its reminder window) and confirm you see a real OS-level notification pop up, not just the in-app list updating — even works with the tab in the background.
3. Click the OS notification — it should focus (or open) the tab and land on the right page (e.g. `/today` for a plan-ready notification).
4. DevTools → Application → Service Workers should still show `sw.js` active; the Push messaging behavior itself isn't inspectable there, but Chrome's `chrome://serviceworker-internals` will show delivered push events if you want to confirm at that level.
5. For email: set `RESEND_API_KEY`/`EMAIL_FROM_ADDRESS`, turn on the "Email" checkbox on `/notifications`, trigger a notification, and check the inbox at your account's real email address.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Real notification delivery)

Same standing sandbox limitations as every increment (`prisma generate`/`migrate` blocked, no browser automation), plus this increment's own real, unavoidable one: an actual push permission prompt, a real OS-level notification popping up, and a real inbox receiving an email are all things that can only be confirmed in a real browser against real infrastructure — nothing here can substitute for the manual steps above. What *was* verified in this sandbox: the full combined backend+test+frontend syntax sweep (225 files, 0 diagnostics) across every new/changed file (`push/*`, `email/*`, `notifications.service.ts`, `notifications.module.ts`, `app.module.ts`, `PushSubscribeButton.tsx`, `notifications/page.tsx`, `sw.js`, `queries.ts`); 6 new e2e tests exercising the parts that don't need a real browser — `vapidPublicKey` returns the configured key, `registerPushSubscription` creates a row and upserts rather than duplicating on re-registration with the same endpoint, `unregisterPushSubscription` removes it, a non-deferred notification comes back with `deliveredAt` set even with zero registered push subscriptions and email off (proving delivery is attempted and the attempt is recorded, without needing a real push service or a real inbox), and `deliverDueNotifications()` correctly delivers a directly-seeded, already-due, previously-undelivered row (the sweep half of delivery, exercised the same way the Scheduler suite exercises `checkRemindersForUser` directly rather than waiting on `@Cron` itself). No test registers a *real* push subscription with a real endpoint and asserts an actual push arrived — that would require a real browser's `PushManager.subscribe()` result, which is exactly the category of thing this sandbox can't produce, hence the manual verification steps above.

## Picking up the Real browser-automation test suite (Playwright) increment

New workspace, no schema change, no new environment variable:

```bash
npm install                  # from the repo root — installs the new apps/e2e workspace too
cd apps/e2e
npx playwright install chromium   # downloads the browser binary this sandbox couldn't reach
```

Then, with both dev servers already running (`npm run dev` in `apps/backend`, `npm run dev` in `apps/web`, same as every other increment's manual verification section):

```bash
cd apps/e2e
npx playwright test          # headless, all 9 tests
npx playwright test --ui     # interactive UI mode — see each step as it runs
```

A few things worth knowing before you run it:

- **Chromium only, one worker, no parallelism** — see `playwright.config.ts`'s own comment for why: every spec authenticates as the app's one fixed dev-auth account (`NEXT_PUBLIC_DEV_USER_EMAIL` in `apps/web/.env.local`), so there's no per-test account isolation the way the backend's own e2e suite gets, and running two specs at once against the same live account would be a real source of flakiness.
- **`global-setup.ts` completes onboarding for that account automatically** (over the GraphQL API, not by clicking through the quiz) if it hasn't been done yet — reads the dev email straight out of `apps/web/.env.local` so it always matches whatever the web app itself is configured to send.
- **The push-notifications spec needs a real, unrestricted network** — `PushManager.subscribe()` has to reach the browser's own internal push service, which is exactly the kind of request this build sandbox's network allowlist blocks; on your own machine or CI this should just work like any other website asking for notification permission.
- Two data-testid attributes were added purely to make this suite's selectors robust rather than fragile text-matching (`TaskEditRow`'s and `JournalEntryRow`'s row containers) — cosmetically invisible, no behavior change.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Real browser-automation test suite)

This increment's entire point is closing a "cannot verify in this sandbox" gap, so it's worth being precise about what actually happened here versus every prior increment's version of this section. Real, unrestricted Chromium (needed to *execute* these tests against a live page) could not be downloaded in this sandbox — `npx playwright install chromium` fails with the same `403 Connection blocked by network allowlist` error as the earlier attempt to verify a Today screenshot (see "What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself" near the top of this README) — so none of the 9 tests have actually been run end to end here. What *was* verified, and is real signal beyond a plain syntax check: `npm install` from the repo root successfully installed `@playwright/test` (confirmed present in `node_modules`), and `playwright test --list` — Playwright's own test collector, which actually parses `playwright.config.ts` (including resolving `global-setup.ts`) and every spec file — correctly discovered and listed all 9 tests across all 5 spec files with no errors. That's a meaningfully stronger check than the `transpileModule`-based syntax sweep alone (which also passed, 233 files, 0 diagnostics, across `apps/e2e` plus the whole existing backend+test+frontend tree) — it confirms Playwright's real config-and-test-loading machinery accepts every file, not just that the TypeScript itself parses. Selector accuracy (whether `page.getByPlaceholder('Add a task…')` etc. actually match the real rendered DOM) was checked by hand against every component's actual source, not assumed — but only running the suite for real can catch what reading the source can't (timing, real async subscription behavior, actual rendered text). Run it yourself once you can download Chromium; if a selector turns out to be wrong, tell me and I'll fix it.

## Picking up the Accessibility (WCAG AA) pass increment

No schema change, no new environment variable, one new dev dependency (`@axe-core/playwright`, in `apps/e2e`):

```bash
npm install                  # from the repo root
cd apps/e2e
npx playwright test accessibility.spec.ts   # needs Chromium — see the note below
```

A few things worth knowing before you run it:

- **The border-color change is visible, on purpose** — every input, card, and button edge in the app is now noticeably darker than before this pass, in both light and dark mode. That was a real, checked-with-you tradeoff (see the increment's own note above), not an accidental side effect — if it looks different than you remember, it's supposed to.
- **`apps/e2e`'s `node_modules` may need a clean reinstall on your end** — while verifying the new `@axe-core/playwright` dependency in this sandbox, an interrupted install combined with a sandbox file-permission quirk left `apps/e2e/node_modules/playwright-core` in a genuinely broken state (a stray `.playwright-core-*` temp directory alongside it, and after an `rm -rf` attempt, a missing `playwright-core/lib/utilsBundle`). This is a sandbox-only artifact, not a real bug in the added dependency or spec file — a normal `rm -rf apps/e2e/node_modules && npm install` on your own machine starts clean and won't hit this. If you see a `Cannot find module 'playwright-core/...'` error, that's this, and that's the fix.
- **Needs the same Chromium binary the existing Playwright suite needs** — `npx playwright install chromium` (see the Real browser-automation test suite section above) — this sandbox couldn't download it, same as before.
- **Runs against the same 13 pages, same fixed dev-auth account, same already-running dev servers** as the rest of the Playwright suite — nothing new to set up beyond what that increment's own section already describes.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Accessibility pass)

Two genuinely different kinds of work happened in this pass, and they were verified differently. The color-contrast fixes are objective, computed facts — I wrote a small Node script implementing the real WCAG relative-luminance/contrast-ratio formula (not an eyeballed guess) and ran every changed color pair through it before and after, so the ratios quoted in the increment's own README section above (4.38:1→6.78:1, and so on) are real numbers, not estimates. The heading, label, `aria-pressed`, landmark, and live-region changes were all syntax-checked (234 files, 0 diagnostics, across the whole backend+frontend+e2e tree) and reviewed by hand against each component's actual markup, but **none of them have been verified against a real screen reader** (VoiceOver, NVDA, JAWS) — that's the one thing static analysis and a syntax checker structurally cannot confirm, since it's about how assistive technology actually announces the page, not whether the JSX is well-formed. The new `accessibility.spec.ts` axe-core suite itself was written and syntax-checked, and its page list and rule-tag scoping were reasoned through carefully, but it could not actually be *executed* in this sandbox — blocked twice over, first by the pre-existing Chromium-download restriction the earlier Playwright increment already hit, and second by the `apps/e2e` `node_modules` corruption noted above. Run `npx playwright test accessibility.spec.ts` for real on your machine before trusting its "0 violations" claim; if it finds something, that's the suite doing its job, not a false alarm.

## Picking up the Extended Playwright coverage increment

New spec files only — no schema change, no new environment variable, no new dependency:

```bash
npm install                  # from the repo root — apps/e2e's node_modules needs a fresh install
                              # regardless (see the accessibility pass's own note on why it's
                              # currently missing in this sandbox)
npx playwright install chromium
cd apps/e2e
npx playwright test          # headless, every spec — now 15 files
npx playwright test goals.spec.ts habits.spec.ts calendar.spec.ts focus.spec.ts \
  checkin-reflection.spec.ts routines.spec.ts chat.spec.ts memory.spec.ts \
  analytics.spec.ts ai-plan-review.spec.ts   # just the 10 new ones
```

A few things worth knowing before you run it:

- **The Chat and AI plan review specs make a real, un-mocked call to the AI provider** — same OpenAI-backed service every other increment in this app already uses, not a stub. They're written with generous timeouts (up to 60 seconds) for exactly that reason; a slow or rate-limited AI response is expected occasionally, a genuine bug is not.
- **The AI plan review spec is written to cope with a plan already sitting PROPOSED on this shared dev-auth account** — it only clicks "Generate my plan" if that button is actually visible, so a leftover proposed plan from an earlier manual session gets reviewed and accepted directly instead of failing or generating a redundant second one.
- **The check-in/reflection and routines specs are also written to tolerate pre-existing data on this same shared account** — logging mood/energy/sleep, or saving a reflection, or editing the morning routine, all use the same "Log or Update, Save or Update reflection, Create or Update routine" button regardless of whether this is the very first time or the hundredth time this account has done that action.
- **No new data-testid attributes needed** — every new spec found what it needed through real accessible roles, labels, and text (the same `aria-label`s and headings the accessibility pass itself just added), which is a real, if small, payoff of that increment landing first.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Extended Playwright coverage)

Same honest gap as the accessibility pass's own Playwright work: these 10 new spec files were written and syntax-checked (244 files, 0 diagnostics across the whole tree) and every selector was checked by hand against each page's actual current source — the exact button text, `aria-label` strings, and heading text quoted in each spec were copy-checked against the real component files, not guessed. But none of them have actually been *run* in this sandbox. Two separate blockers compound here: the pre-existing Chromium-download restriction (unchanged since the original Playwright increment), and `apps/e2e/node_modules` currently not existing at all in this sandbox (see the accessibility pass's own note on the interrupted install that caused this) — so unlike the very first Playwright increment, not even `playwright test --list` could be attempted as a lighter-weight check this time. This is real, meaningful new risk surface worth being honest about: hand-checked selectors are a much weaker signal than an actual green test run, especially for the two AI-backed specs where real latency and real, only-sometimes-deterministic reply content could plausibly need a selector tweak once run for real. Treat every one of these 10 specs as "should work, not yet proven" until you run them yourself.

## Picking up the Subtask UI increment

No schema change (the columns already existed), no new environment variable, no new backend code at all — this increment is entirely new frontend surface over a backend capability that was already real:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Then, in the app: open `/tasks`, tap **Edit** on any task, tap **Save** (even with no changes — this is just how `TaskEditRow` gets back to its view state, where `SubtaskList` actually renders), and use the new **"Add a subtask…"** field at the bottom of that task's row.

A few things worth knowing:

- **A subtask is a completely ordinary `Task` row under the hood** — it can be edited, cancelled, or even given its own `parentTaskId` pointed elsewhere via a direct API call, even though no UI exposes any of that for a subtask specifically. The UI only ever creates one through `CREATE_SUBTASK` and only ever shows the `id`/`title`/`status` fields — nothing stops you from doing more to one through the GraphQL Playground if you wanted to.
- **Removing a subtask can't be undone from the UI** — "Remove" calls `cancelTask`, and a cancelled subtask is filtered out of `SubtaskList` entirely (not shown crossed-out), the same way a cancelled top-level task disappears from the Tasks screen's Active tab. The task row itself still exists and is still technically reachable — nothing was hard-deleted — there's just no screen that shows a cancelled subtask back to you.
- **Today's task rows only ever show a count, never the add/check/remove controls** — a deliberate scope cut to keep that screen's compact layout intact; open `/tasks` if you actually want to manage a task's subtasks.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Subtask UI)

Verified the same way as every frontend-only increment in this README: a full syntax sweep (246 files, 0 diagnostics, across the whole backend+frontend+e2e tree) and a new `subtasks.spec.ts` Playwright spec that exercises the whole loop (add a subtask, complete it, confirm the count updates in two independent places — the editor's own `SubtaskList` and Today's separate read-only badge — then remove it). Same standing gap as every Playwright spec in this project so far: it was written and syntax-checked, and its selectors were hand-checked against `SubtaskList.tsx`'s and `TaskRow.tsx`'s actual real markup, but it hasn't been *run* — `apps/e2e/node_modules` is still missing in this sandbox (unchanged since the Extended Playwright coverage increment's own note on why). One thing that *is* a stronger-than-usual signal here: the backend half of this increment required zero new code, since `parentTaskId`/`subtasks` have been real, working Prisma/GraphQL fields since the original Tasks increment — this is purely new frontend surface over an already-proven backend capability, not new backend logic that could itself be wrong.

## Picking up the Tasks pagination increment

No new environment variable, no schema/migration change (the underlying `Task` columns and their `parentTaskId: null`/`status` indexes were already there) — one real backend resolver change, plus the two frontend query docs it enables:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend — schema.gql regenerates automatically on
              # startup (autoSchemaFile — see app.module.ts) and will now
              # include the new `statuses` arg on the `tasks` query
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Then, on `/tasks`: the Open and Cancelled tabs now each page independently — a "Load more" button appears under either list once there are more than 20 tasks in that specific tab.

A few things worth knowing:

- **`statuses` is new, `status` still works exactly as it always has** — `COMPLETED_TASKS_QUERY` (the `/more` page) wasn't touched and still uses the older singular arg; this was an additive schema change, not a breaking one.
- **Switching tabs the first time always makes a real network request** — neither tab pre-fetches the other speculatively (`skip` on whichever tab isn't active), so there's a brief "Loading…" the very first time you click Cancelled, same as visiting any other page for the first time.
- **"Load more" always appends to page one's own variables** — same tradeoff `/more`'s existing Undo button already accepts for its own list: editing or cancelling a task from a page-two-or-later row triggers a `refetchQueries` call with no explicit variables, which re-fetches each tab from page one only. If you'd expanded a list past its first page and then edit something, expect the expansion to visually collapse back to 20 — annoying in a very long list, but a real, pre-existing pattern in this codebase, not a new bug this increment introduced.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Tasks pagination)

The backend change (a new `statuses: [TaskStatus!]` connection arg, and the `Prisma` `status: { in: [...] }` filter it maps to) is small, additive, and syntax-checked, but — same standing limitation as every backend increment built in this sandbox — it's never been checked against a real Postgres instance or `schema.gql` regeneration here (no database connection is available in this sandbox at all; see the very first "what's verified" section near the top of this README). The new `tasks-pagination` test inside `tasks.spec.ts` is written to be deterministic regardless of how much pre-existing data is on the shared dev-auth account — it creates 21 tasks in a row and checks specifically that the very first one created (guaranteed to have at least 20 newer siblings, so guaranteed to fall past page one no matter what else already exists) is hidden until "Load more" is clicked — but like every other Playwright spec in this project so far, it hasn't actually been *run*, for the same `apps/e2e/node_modules`-missing reason noted throughout this README's more recent increments. Run `npx playwright test tasks.spec.ts` yourself once you're on a normal machine — this is the one spec in the whole suite most worth actually watching run, since pagination bugs (an off-by-one on `hasNextPage`, a cursor that skips or repeats a row) are exactly the kind of thing that's easy to get subtly wrong and easy to visually confirm once you can actually watch it click "Load more."

## Picking up the Push calendar edits back to Google/Microsoft increment

No schema change, no new environment variable, no new dependency:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend — schema.gql regenerates automatically,
              # picking up updateCalendarEvent's existing shape (unchanged —
              # this increment only changed what happens inside the resolver)
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Then, on `/calendar` (or Today's own event rows): tap **Edit** on any event — native or synced — change its title or times, tap **Save**.

A few things worth knowing:

- **This is the increment most worth testing against a real connected account, specifically** — everything else built in this sandbox that touches Google/Microsoft (the original two-way sync, push-deletes) had at least some prior real-world usage to lean on by the time it reached this conversation; the push-*edit* path is genuinely new code with zero real API calls behind it yet. Connect a real Google or Microsoft account, edit one of its synced events from this app, and go check that it actually changed on the provider's own calendar — that single check is worth more than anything else in this section.
- **An account connected before this increment (or before the original two-way-sync increments) may need to reconnect** — same `RECONNECT_REQUIRED` mechanism push-deletes already established: if the connected account's tokens only ever granted read access, editing (like deleting) fails with a clear message pointing at the provider's card on `/calendar` rather than a generic error.
- **A failed push leaves the local copy untouched** — same ordering discipline `delete()` already followed: the remote PATCH happens first, and the local database row only updates once that succeeds, so a `RECONNECT_REQUIRED` failure never leaves your local copy silently out of sync with what's still on Google or Outlook.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Push calendar edits back to Google/Microsoft)

Verified as far as this sandbox structurally allows for anything OAuth-shaped: a full syntax sweep (246 files, 0 diagnostics), and every new method (`GoogleCalendarClient.updateEvent`, `MicrosoftCalendarClient.updateEvent`, both write services' `updateRemoteEvent`) built by deliberately mirroring an existing, already-working sibling method's exact structure (`deleteEvent`/`deleteRemoteEvent`) rather than writing new request/retry logic from scratch — the same refresh-token handling, the same 401/403/404/410 status-code branching, the same `RECONNECT_REQUIRED` mapping. That's a real, meaningful risk reducer, but it is not the same as a verified API call: this sandbox has no real Google or Microsoft OAuth client credentials, no connected test account, and no network path to either provider's real API at all — none of `updateEvent`'s actual HTTP requests have ever been sent, to Google or to Microsoft, and neither has the original `deleteEvent`/`deleteRemoteEvent` code this mirrors, for that matter (a pre-existing, previously undocumented gap in exactly how "verified" those push-deletes ever really were in this sandbox specifically — worth knowing given this increment leans on them as precedent). The one new Playwright test (`calendar.spec.ts`'s new "editing a native event" case) only exercises the NATIVE path end to end — deliberately, since a real synced-event test would need real OAuth credentials this suite's shared dev-auth account has no way to obtain — and like every other spec in this project, it's written and syntax-checked but hasn't been *run* (the same missing `apps/e2e/node_modules` noted throughout this README's recent increments). Testing the actual Google/Microsoft push yourself, with a real connected account, is the single most valuable verification step open on this whole project right now.

## Picking up the SMS delivery increment

One new column (`User.phoneNumber`), no other schema change, one new optional dependency-free service:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend — schema.gql regenerates automatically,
              # picking up phoneNumber on both User and
              # UpdateNotificationPreferencesInput
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Add your own real values to `.env` to actually try it (leave blank to keep SMS off — `SmsService` just no-ops, the app boots fine either way):

```bash
TWILIO_ACCOUNT_SID=""
TWILIO_AUTH_TOKEN=""
TWILIO_FROM_NUMBER=""
```

Then, on `/notifications`: enter your own phone number in E.164 format (e.g. `+15551234567`), check the **SMS** box, **Save preferences**, then trigger any real notification (generate an AI plan, say) and check your phone.

A few things worth knowing:

- **A malformed phone number is caught twice** — a loose client-side regex check before the mutation is even called (catches an obviously-wrong value fast, with a clear inline message), and a real `@Matches(E164)` validator server-side (`UpdateNotificationPreferencesInput`) that catches anything that slipped past the first check — the second one throws a GraphQL validation error rather than a payload `errors` entry, which the frontend now specifically catches and turns into a plain message too.
- **SMS combines a notification's title and body into one message** — unlike email, there's no separate subject line for SMS, so the text you receive reads `Title: Body` as one string.
- **This is the increment most worth testing against a real account, same as the calendar push-edit increment right before it** — a free Twilio trial account is enough to confirm this end to end; there's no way to verify a real text message arrives without one.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (SMS delivery)

Verified the same way as every third-party-API increment in this project: a full syntax sweep (248 files, 0 diagnostics) and a deliberate choice to build `SmsService` by mirroring `EmailService`'s already-established, working shape (plain `fetch`, no SDK, a clear `isConfigured()`/no-op-when-unset pattern) rather than inventing new integration logic from scratch. That's real signal, but — same as every OAuth/third-party-API increment before it in this sandbox — there are no real Twilio credentials available here, so `SmsService.send`'s actual HTTP request to Twilio's API has never been made. The server-side E.164 validation regex and the client-side pre-check were both tested by hand against a handful of example numbers (valid US/UK numbers, and a few deliberately malformed ones — missing `+`, too short, letters) to confirm the regex itself behaves correctly, which is a real, if narrow, form of verification a syntax check alone doesn't give you. Get a Twilio trial account, set your own phone number on `/notifications`, and confirm a real text actually arrives before trusting this fully.

## Picking up the Extending auto-replanning to WEEK/MONTH scope increment

No schema change, no new environment variable, no new dependency — purely a change to `PlannerService`'s existing automatic-replanning logic:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Nothing new to set up — the same `task.completed`/`calendar.changed` triggers that already worked for DAY scope now also reach WEEK and MONTH automatically. To actually see it: generate (or let auto-generate) a WEEK or MONTH plan once via `WeeklyPlanCard`'s Week/Month toggle on Today, then complete a task or add a calendar event — reopen Today (or switch away and back) afterward to see the refreshed WEEK/MONTH plan, since (see the new "what's not built yet" note above) that card doesn't auto-refetch the moment the background auto-replan finishes.

A few things worth knowing:

- **The cooldowns are intentionally very different per scope** — 10 minutes for DAY (unchanged), 3 hours for WEEK, 12 hours for MONTH. If you're testing this and don't see a second WEEK/MONTH auto-plan after triggering it twice in a row, that's the cooldown working as designed, not a bug — wait out the window, or call `plannerService.maybeAutoReplan(userId, 'auto_task_completed')` directly in a test/script if you want to bypass the wait.
- **No new triggers were added, on purpose** — this increment is specifically about which scopes the two existing triggers reach, not about adding a third kind of trigger (a habit completing, a check-in, and so on all remain open — see "What's not built yet" above).

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Extending auto-replanning to WEEK/MONTH scope)

Verified more thoroughly than most recent increments in this README, since this one has no external OAuth/third-party dependency to be honest about a gap in — a full syntax sweep (248 files, 0 diagnostics) plus two new backend e2e tests added to the existing `Automatic AI re-planning (e2e)` suite: one confirms `completeTask` really does trigger a real, end-to-end WEEK *and* MONTH plan (not just DAY, which the suite's original test already covered), and the other confirms each scope's cooldown is genuinely independent — a WEEK plan firing doesn't block a follow-up DAY attempt, and vice versa. Both were written and syntax-checked but, like every backend e2e test built in this sandbox, have never actually been run — this suite needs a real Postgres connection (`docker compose up -d` + `prisma migrate`), and this sandbox has neither Docker nor a local Postgres install available (confirmed directly: no `docker` binary, no local `postgresql` package, and no permission to install one — `apt-get install` fails with a permission error, not just a missing-package one). Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` yourself once you're on a normal machine with the database running — these two new tests are real logic checks (cooldown math, scope independence), not integration smoke tests, so they're worth actually seeing pass.

## Picking up the Cross-metric correlation in Insights increment

No schema change, no new environment variable, no new dependency — purely additive changes to the existing analytics stack:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Open `/analytics` and scroll to the bottom — the new "Patterns worth noting" card sits below Routine consistency. With a fresh account (or too few logged days) it shows an honest "not enough data yet" message; log mood, energy, and sleep on Today across at least 5 different days with a genuine relationship between two of them, and a real correlation should appear there, sorted strongest first.

A few things worth knowing:

- **Six pairs are checked, not all possible ones** — sleep duration and sleep quality vs. both mood and energy, plus a new daily habit-completion-rate series (the fraction of that day's due habits actually completed, across all your habits) vs. both mood and energy. Mood vs. energy itself is deliberately skipped — see the increment note above for why.
- **A pair only shows up once it clears two bars**: at least 5 days where both metrics have a real value, and an absolute Pearson coefficient of at least 0.3. Both are named constants at the top of `analytics.service.ts` (`MIN_CORRELATION_SAMPLE_SIZE`, `MIN_CORRELATION_ABS_R`) if you want to loosen or tighten them.
- **A perfectly constant series (e.g. always logging the same sleep quality score) correctly produces no correlation for that metric at all**, not a misleading `0` — Pearson's r is mathematically undefined when a series has zero variance, and `computeCorrelation` returns `null` in that case rather than pretending otherwise.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Cross-metric correlation in Insights)

The Pearson correlation formula itself (`computeCorrelation` in `analytics.service.ts`) was independently verified before being trusted in the real code — not just asserted. Five test vectors (a perfect +1 relationship, a perfect -1 relationship, a realistic ~0.93 relationship with a gap in the middle, a zero-variance series, and a shorter series with gaps on both sides) were run through both a standalone copy of the actual TypeScript logic (`node`) and `numpy.corrcoef` on the same inputs (`python3`) — every coefficient matched exactly, and the zero-variance case correctly returned `null` from both implementations rather than a misleading `0`. A new backend e2e test (`Life analytics (e2e)` suite) seeds a real 6-day sleep-duration-vs-mood relationship (sleep via `logSleep`'s own `sleepDate` field, mood via `logMood` + a direct Prisma `loggedAt` backdate, the same escape hatch the habit/routine streak tests above already use) and asserts the live GraphQL response reports exactly `coefficient: 0.94, sampleSize: 6` — a number independently computed via `numpy` before the test was written, not eyeballed after. The same test also asserts three *absences*: no "Sleep quality" correlation (logged as a constant, so zero variance), no "Energy" correlation (never logged), and no "Habit completion" correlation (no habits exist for that test user) — proving the minimum-bar filtering actually excludes what it should, not just that it includes what it should. A full syntax sweep passed (248 files, 0 diagnostics — no new files, since this increment only extended existing ones). What's *not* verified: same standing limitation as every backend e2e test in this project — this sandbox has no Docker, no local Postgres install, and no permission to install one, so the new test above was written and syntax-checked but never actually run. A light Playwright check (`analytics.spec.ts`) confirms the new card's heading and empty-state text render on a fresh account, but — same standing gap as the rest of this project's Playwright suite — `apps/e2e/node_modules` is still missing in this sandbox, so it's untested by an actual browser run here either. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e` yourself once you're on a normal machine with Postgres running to get the first real confirmation.

## Picking up the Lagged correlation increment

No schema change, no new environment variable, no new dependency — purely additive changes to the analytics stack the Cross-metric correlation increment (above) just built:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Open `/analytics` and look at "Patterns worth noting" — any entry with a small "Next-day" badge in front of it is a one-day-lag result (yesterday's metric A vs. today's metric B), not a same-day one. To see one appear for real: log sleep duration and mood across at least 6 different days where *yesterday's* sleep genuinely predicts *today's* mood better than same-day sleep does (or ask me to seed a demo dataset the same way the second new backend e2e test does).

A few things worth knowing:

- **Only the forward direction is checked** — metric A (say, sleep) leading metric B (say, mood) by one day. The reverse (does today's mood predict tomorrow's sleep?) isn't computed at all.
- **A pair can show up at neither lag, one lag, or both** — they're computed and filtered completely independently through the same minimum-sample-size/minimum-strength bars the same-day version already used. Seeing only the lagged version (or only the same-day version) for a given pair is itself a real, meaningful result, not a bug.
- **The lag-1 sample size is always one smaller than the same-day sample size for the same window** — the oldest day in the window has no earlier day to pair with, so it's dropped from every lagged pair, never reused or wrapped around.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Lagged correlation)

The new `shiftForLag` realignment helper was verified the same way the original Pearson formula was — not just asserted. A synthetic dataset was built specifically so metric A leads metric B by exactly one day (`B[i+1] = A[i] * 0.1 + 2`, with the very first day of B set to an unrelated value); running it through the real `shiftForLag` + `computeCorrelation` pair found `r = 1.0` at lag 1 versus `r ≈ -0.61` at lag 0 on the identical raw data — concrete proof the two checks are measuring genuinely different things, not just relabeling the same coefficient. A new backend e2e test seeds this exact dynamic with real mutations (`logSleep`'s own `sleepDate` field plus a direct Prisma `loggedAt` backdate for mood, same escape hatch used throughout this suite): an 8-day dataset engineered (via a small script that tried random day-orderings until it found one) so the same-day sleep-vs-mood correlation is ≈0.02 — nowhere near the 0.3 reporting bar — while the one-day-lag version of that identical pair is ≈0.98, comfortably clearing it. The test asserts both the lagged result's exact numbers (`sampleSize: 7`, `coefficient: 0.98`) *and* the same-day version's absence, which is the harder, more meaningful half of the proof — a strong lag result doesn't imply a same-day one for the same metrics, and this test confirms the code actually behaves that way rather than just asserting a positive case. The original correlation test (`Life analytics (e2e)` suite) was also updated to assert both a same-day *and* a lag-1 result on its existing monotonic dataset, since that dataset happens to clear the bar at both lags — a second, complementary demonstration that both checks run and report independently even when a relationship happens to hold at both. A full syntax sweep passed (248 files, 0 diagnostics — no new files). What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as every backend e2e test in this project — no Docker, no local Postgres, no permission to install one — so both new/updated tests above were written and syntax-checked but never actually run. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` yourself once you're on a normal machine with Postgres running.

## Picking up the New auto-replanning triggers increment

No schema change, no new environment variable, no new dependency — purely additive event wiring:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Nothing new to set up. To see it: with at least one open task on the account, complete a habit, log a mood or energy check-in, or finish a routine (check off every step) — reopening Today afterward (or checking `latestPlanRun.triggerEvent`) should show `auto_habit_completed`, `auto_checkin_logged`, or `auto_routine_completed` respectively, subject to the same per-scope cooldowns every other auto-trigger already uses.

A few things worth knowing:

- **A partial routine completion never triggers anything** — only checking off every current step does; `RoutinesService.setTodayCompletion` checks `completedStepIds.length >= totalSteps` before ever emitting.
- **Mood and energy share one event (`checkin.logged`)** — logging either one triggers the same check, there's no way to tell from the outside which one caused it (nor is there a need to, for this purpose).
- **Sleep logging does not trigger anything, on purpose** — see the code comment on `SignalsService.logSleep`.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (New auto-replanning triggers)

Verified the same way the original two triggers were: three new backend e2e tests in the existing `Automatic AI re-planning (e2e)` suite, each exercising the real mutation (not a direct `maybeAutoReplan` call) end-to-end — `completeHabitLog`, `logMood`, and `setTodayRoutineCompletion` — polling for a real `latestPlanRun` to appear and asserting its `triggerEvent` matches. The routine test specifically also asserts the *negative* case: completing only one of two steps produces no plan run at all, checked immediately with no polling (since nothing was ever emitted, there's nothing asynchronous to wait for) — proving the "only a genuinely finished routine counts" gate actually holds, not just that a finished one works. A full syntax sweep passed (248 files, 0 diagnostics — no new files, purely additive changes to `habits.service.ts`, `signals.service.ts`, `routines.service.ts`, and `planner.service.ts`). What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as every backend e2e test in this project — no Docker, no local Postgres, no permission to install one — so these three new tests were written and syntax-checked but never actually run. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` yourself once you're on a normal machine with Postgres running.

## Picking up the Further auto-replanning triggers increment

No schema change, no new environment variable, no new dependency — purely additive event wiring, same shape as the New auto-replanning triggers increment right above:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Nothing new to set up. To see it: with at least one open task on the account, write a journal entry, complete a focus session (not cancel one), or submit a daily reflection — `latestPlanRun.triggerEvent` should read `auto_journal_entry`, `auto_focus_session_completed`, or `auto_reflection_submitted` respectively, subject to the same per-scope cooldowns every other auto-trigger already uses.

A few things worth knowing:

- **Only a genuine journal entry *creation* triggers this — editing an existing entry doesn't.** `JournalService.update` never emits anything.
- **Only a genuine focus session *completion* triggers this — cancelling one doesn't.** `FocusService.cancel` never emits anything, same "only the real finished signal counts" gate the routine-completion trigger already uses.
- **A same-day reflection resubmit/correction triggers this too, not just the first submission of the day** — no special-casing, same reasoning `HabitsService.completeLog`'s trigger already applies; the per-scope cooldown is what keeps repeated submissions from being noisy, not anything checked at the source.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Further auto-replanning triggers)

Verified the same way every other trigger in this increment family was: three new backend e2e tests in the existing `Automatic AI re-planning (e2e)` suite, each exercising the real mutation end-to-end (`createJournalEntry`, `startFocusSession`/`completeFocusSession`, `submitDailyReflection`) and polling for a real `latestPlanRun` with the right `triggerEvent`. The focus session test also asserts the negative case explicitly — starting and then *cancelling* a session produces no plan run at all, checked immediately with no polling, since nothing should have been emitted in the first place. A full syntax sweep passed (248 files, 0 diagnostics — no new files, purely additive changes to `journal.service.ts`, `focus.service.ts`, `reflection.service.ts`, and `planner.service.ts`). What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as every backend e2e test in this project — no Docker, no local Postgres, no permission to install one — so these three new tests were written and syntax-checked but never actually run. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` yourself once you're on a normal machine with Postgres running.

## Picking up the Insights: task/focus-session/journal trends increment

No schema change, no new environment variable, no new dependency — purely additive reads over existing tables:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Open `/analytics` and scroll past Sleep duration — "Tasks completed" is next, then (past Routine consistency) "Focus sessions" with its new streak card, then "Journal activity." All three show an honest empty state on a fresh account rather than a blank or broken chart.

A few things worth knowing:

- **A day with zero activity is a real zero here, not a "nothing logged" gap** — unlike the mood/energy/sleep charts above them, these three series never show a null/missing point; every day in the window has a real count, even 0.
- **Focus sessions use real elapsed time, not planned duration** — a session someone let run past its planned 25 minutes (or ended early) is counted for what actually happened.
- **These three aren't part of the "Patterns worth noting" correlation card** — a deliberate scope cut (see "What's not built yet" above), not a bug if you go looking for a task-completion-vs-mood correlation and don't find one.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Insights: task/focus-session/journal trends)

Three new backend e2e tests in the `Life analytics (e2e)` suite, following the same real-mutations-plus-one-Prisma-backdate pattern the habit/routine streak tests above already use (`completeTask`/`startFocusSession`+`completeFocusSession`/`createJournalEntry`, each stamping "now" with no way to backdate through the API itself): one confirms real per-day task-completion counts including a genuine zero day, one confirms real focused minutes and a real focus-session streak — specifically checking that a real zero day correctly breaks the streak walk, not just that a non-zero day extends it — and one confirms real per-day journal entry counts. A full syntax sweep passed (248 files, 0 diagnostics — no new files, purely additive changes to `tasks.service.ts`, `focus.service.ts`, `journal.service.ts`, `analytics.service.ts`, `analytics-summary.model.ts`, `analytics.module.ts`, and `analytics/page.tsx`). A light Playwright check (`analytics.spec.ts`) confirms all three new cards' headings and empty-state text render on a fresh account. What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as every backend e2e test in this project — no Docker, no local Postgres, no permission to install one — so the new backend tests were written and syntax-checked but never actually run; and `apps/e2e/node_modules` is still missing here, so the new Playwright test hasn't been run by a real browser in this sandbox either. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e` yourself once you're on a normal machine with Postgres running.

## Picking up the Wiring Insights' newest trends into the correlation engine increment

No schema change, no new environment variable, no new dependency — purely additive candidate pairs over the exact same correlation machinery:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Nothing new to set up — open `/analytics`, and "Patterns worth noting" can now surface a pattern involving "Tasks completed," "Focused minutes," or "Journal entries" alongside the sleep/habit ones that were already there, same card, same rules.

A few things worth knowing:

- **These three are always real numbers, never a missing value** — every day genuinely has a completedCount/completedMinutes/entryCount, even 0, so a correlation involving one of them can reach the full window's sample size where a sleep/mood pair sometimes can't (a day with no sleep logged simply isn't excluded here the way it would be from a sleep-based pair).
- **The correlation engine now checks 24 candidate pairs per load** (12 base pairs × 2 lags) — still cheap; every check is a plain in-memory Pearson calculation over data already fetched for the trend charts themselves, no extra queries.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Wiring Insights' newest trends into the correlation engine)

No new formula to verify — `computeCorrelation`/`shiftForLag`/`describeCorrelation` are the exact same, already-`numpy`-verified functions the sleep/habit pairs use; this increment only added three more series to the candidate list. One new backend e2e test in the `Life analytics (e2e)` suite seeds a real, monotonic tasks-completed-vs-mood relationship (independently confirmed via `numpy.corrcoef` at r ≈ 0.93 before the test was written) and asserts the live GraphQL response reports exactly that pair with `sampleSize: 6, coefficient: 0.93`. A full syntax sweep passed (248 files, 0 diagnostics — no new files). What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as every backend e2e test in this project — no Docker, no local Postgres, no permission to install one — so this new test was written and syntax-checked but never actually run. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` yourself once you're on a normal machine with Postgres running.

## Picking up the Visible settings screen increment

One real schema change this time — a new `timezoneManual` boolean column on `User`, defaulting to `false` so every existing account behaves exactly as before until someone actually visits Settings:

```bash
npm run prisma:generate && npm run prisma:migrate   # in apps/backend, picks up the new User.timezoneManual column
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Open `/settings` (linked from Today, next to Tasks) to see it. A few things worth knowing:

- **Editing the timezone field switches it to manual mode automatically** — there's no separate "enable manual mode" toggle to find first; typing a new value is the action. The "Use browser-detected automatically" button (which only appears once you're in manual mode) is the one way back.
- **Work hours and chronotype are just `updateProfile` under the hood** — same mutation the onboarding quiz and the pre-existing (but never UI-wired) chronotype update already used, not a new, parallel mutation.
- **The "biggest source of overload" onboarding question isn't here** — it was never a distinct column; it's a plain fact on `/memory`, already editable there.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Visible settings screen)

A new backend e2e test confirms `workHoursStart`/`workHoursEnd`/`timezoneManual`/`chronotype` all round-trip through a real `updateProfile` save and a real, separate re-read via `me` (not just echoed back from the mutation's own response) — and a second test confirms a malformed work-hours value is genuinely rejected, the same way `CompleteOnboardingInput`'s identical fields already are. A new Playwright spec (`settings.spec.ts`) drives the real `/settings` page: setting chronotype and work hours and confirming they survive a reload, and separately confirming the timezone field's manual/automatic mode switches correctly and that "use browser-detected automatically" genuinely hands control back (verified by reloading and checking the automatic-mode copy reappears, not just that the button click didn't error). A full syntax sweep passed (250 files, 0 diagnostics — two new files, `apps/web/src/app/settings/page.tsx` and `apps/e2e/tests/settings.spec.ts`). What's *not* verified: the Prisma migration itself has never been run against a real Postgres instance in this sandbox — same standing limitation as every schema change in this project (no Docker, no local Postgres, no permission to install one) — and neither new test has actually been executed here for the same reason. Run `npm run prisma:migrate`, `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e` yourself once you're on a normal machine with Postgres running.

## Picking up the Broader account settings increment

No schema change this time — `displayName` and `subscription` were already real columns/tables (`UpdateProfileInput` already accepted `displayName`; `Subscription` has existed since the very first profile increment), and `deleteAccount` only needed new application-layer logic, not a new migration:

```bash
npm install
npm run dev   # in apps/backend
npm run dev   # in apps/web, separate terminal
```

Open `/settings` to see the new "Name" section (above Timezone), the read-only "Account" card (email + plan), and the "Danger zone" card at the bottom. A few things worth knowing:

- **Deleting your account is real and immediate** — there's no grace period, no "recover within 30 days" window, and no confirmation email; typing `DELETE` and clicking the button really removes everything listed in that card's own warning text, right away.
- **The Plan card was read-only on purpose at the point this section was written** — at the time, there was no Stripe integration anywhere in this project, so there was genuinely nothing to click to upgrade or manage payment yet. That's since changed — see the Real Stripe billing integration entry further below for the real Checkout/Billing Portal picker that replaced this read-only card.
- **`AUTH_MODE=dev` behaves differently after deletion than real Clerk auth does** — under dev auth there's no real session to end, so deleting just clears the local cache and sends you back to `/today`, where the same `x-dev-user-email` identity immediately gets a brand-new, empty account. Under real Clerk auth, it also ends your actual Clerk session and sends you to `/sign-in`.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Broader account settings)

A new backend e2e test seeds real rows directly via Prisma in three of the tables that only ever had a plain `userId` column with no declared relation to `User` (`Task`, `JournalEntry`, `Habit`), calls the real `deleteAccount` mutation, and confirms all three tables are really empty for that user afterward, that the `users` row itself is gone (`findUnique` returns `null`, not just `deletedAt` set), and that the same dev-auth identity gets a genuinely new row (a different `id`) on its very next request — using its own disposable, uniquely-suffixed dev email so it can't interfere with any other test in the suite. A new Playwright coverage gap worth being upfront about: `settings.spec.ts` only tests the delete confirmation *affordance* (the button stays disabled until you type `DELETE` exactly) — it deliberately never clicks the button through to a real deletion, since every Playwright spec in this suite shares one fixed dev-auth identity (`NEXT_PUBLIC_DEV_USER_EMAIL`, baked in at build time) and actually deleting it would break every other spec that assumes that account still exists. The real end-to-end deletion path is only covered by the backend e2e test described above, which can mint its own throwaway identity in a way this Playwright suite currently can't. A full syntax sweep passed (251 files, 0 diagnostics — one new file, `apps/backend/src/users/models/delete-account.payload.ts`). What's *not* verified, same standing limitation as every increment in this project: none of this has actually been run against a real Postgres database here — the whole `$transaction`/cascade design in `UsersService.deleteAccount` was reasoned through by reading `schema.prisma` relation-by-relation (see the increment note above), not by executing it. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` yourself once you're on a normal machine with Postgres running, and seriously consider testing account deletion against a real database before trusting it in anything beyond a demo.

## Picking up the Multi-day / reverse-direction lag correlation increment

Nothing new to set up — no schema change, no new query or mutation, no new frontend route. Open `/analytics`; "Patterns worth noting" can now show a two- or three-day-lag entry (badged "2 days later"/"3 days later" instead of "Next-day"), and can now show a reverse-direction entry for any pair that has one — same card, same rules, same query.

A few things worth knowing:

- **A reverse-direction entry looks like any other entry, just with the labels swapped** — there's no "direction" indicator anywhere in the UI or the schema; "Higher mood tends to come with lower sleep duration the next day" and "Higher sleep duration tends to come with lower mood the next day" are two completely independent `MetricCorrelation` rows, distinguishable only by which label comes first.
- **Same-day is never checked in reverse** — mathematically pointless (a same-day Pearson coefficient is identical either direction), so `lagDays: 0` only ever produces one entry per base pair, exactly as before this increment.
- **The candidate-check count went from 24 to 84 per load** (12 base pairs × 7: one same-day check, plus both directions at each of three lags) — still cheap, still a plain in-memory calculation over data already fetched for the trend charts.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Multi-day / reverse-direction lag correlation)

Two new datasets, found by the same kind of small random-search script every correlation dataset in this project has used, were independently verified twice before being hardcoded into e2e test assertions: once via `numpy.corrcoef`, and a second time via a standalone Node script that directly replicates `shiftForLag`/`computeCorrelation`'s exact slicing and formula (not just the same math in a different tool — the same algorithm, run outside the app). The two-day-lag dataset gives same-day r ≈ 0.03, one-day-lag r ≈ -0.08 (both correctly excluded), and two-day-lag r ≈ 0.92 over 7 paired days (correctly included) — both independent confirmations agreed to many decimal places. The reverse-direction dataset gives a forward one-day-lag (sleep predicting next-day mood) r = 0.0 exactly (excluded) and a reverse one-day-lag (mood predicting next-day sleep) r ≈ -0.87 over 7 paired days (included) — proving the reverse check surfaces something the forward one genuinely misses, not a relabeling of it. Two new backend e2e tests in the `Life analytics (e2e)` suite assert on both datasets' exact rounded coefficients, sample sizes, and the specific wording (`"2 days later"`, `"2-day-apart pairs"`, and the preserved `"next day"` wording for `lagDays: 1`) — plus the negative-result half of each proof (the non-qualifying lags/directions genuinely don't appear in the response). A full syntax sweep passed (251 files, 0 diagnostics — no new files, only edits to `analytics.service.ts`, `analytics-summary.model.ts`, `analytics/page.tsx`, and `app.e2e-spec.ts`). What's *not* verified, same standing limitation as every backend e2e test in this project: neither new test has actually been run against a real Postgres database here — no Docker, no local Postgres, no permission to install one in this sandbox. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` yourself once you're on a normal machine with Postgres running.

## Picking up the Correlating non-mood/energy metrics increment

Nothing new to set up — no schema change, no new query, no new frontend route. Open `/analytics`; "Patterns worth noting" can now show a pattern between any two of sleep duration/quality, habit completion, tasks completed, focused minutes, or journal entries, not just one of them against mood or energy.

A few things worth knowing:

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Correlating non-mood/energy metrics)

A new backend e2e test seeds a real relationship between focused minutes and journal entries — two metrics that are neither Mood nor Energy — independently confirmed via `numpy.corrcoef` at r ≈ 0.97 before being hardcoded, and confirms the live GraphQL response reports exactly that pair with `sampleSize: 6, coefficient: 0.97`, with no Mood or Energy check-ins logged at all during the test (proving the pair is found on its own, not riding along with a mood/energy correlation). A full syntax sweep passed (251 files, 0 diagnostics — no new files, only edits to `analytics.service.ts` and `app.e2e-spec.ts`). What's *not* verified, same standing limitation as every backend e2e test in this project: the new test has never actually been run against a real Postgres database here — no Docker, no local Postgres, no permission to install one in this sandbox. ## Picking up the Editable email + re-enter onboarding increment

Nothing new to set up — no schema/migration change (`timezoneManual`-style column wasn't needed here), no new frontend route. Open `/settings`: the Account card now has a "Change email →" button under real Clerk auth (dev-auth shows explanatory text instead), and a "Redo the onboarding quiz →" link sits under the Save button. Visiting `/onboarding` after already completing it starts on the quiz step directly, with chronotype/work hours/quiet hours pre-filled from your existing answers.

A few things worth knowing:

- **The email-resync bug fix can't be exercised through this app's own e2e suite at all** — `AUTH_MODE=dev`'s `authProviderId` is literally `dev:${email}`, derived from the same header the e2e tests set, so changing the dev email between requests always creates a *new* user (different `authProviderId`), never triggers the resync path on an *existing* one. The resync path only matters under real Clerk auth, where `authProviderId` is Clerk's own stable user ID and the email can change independently of it — covered by the unit tests (`users.service.spec.ts`) and the standalone `_manual_verify_email_resync.ts` script instead (see "What's verified" below).
- **The "Change email" button has never been clicked against a real Clerk account here** — same standing OAuth-integration limitation as every Clerk-dependent piece of this project; `openUserProfile()` is real, correctly-registered Clerk SDK usage, but "correct code following Clerk's documented API" isn't the same as "confirmed with a real account."
- **The "biggest source of overload" answer is the one onboarding field that never pre-fills** — deliberate, not a bug; see the increment's own README entry above for why.
- **Redoing onboarding with the exact same overload answer twice in a row still only produces one memory fact** — the upsert is on content-independent identity (a fixed key), not on whether the new answer differs from the old one; the new e2e test happens to use two *different* answers to make the "updated in place" proof more convincing, but even an identical resubmission would still upsert onto the same row.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Editable email + re-enter onboarding)

The email-resync fix was verified two ways: `users.service.spec.ts` gained two new Jest test cases (stale-email resync, and fallback-to-stale-row if the resync `update` call itself fails) plus a new assertion on the existing "returns the existing user" test confirming `update` is *not* called when the email hasn't changed; and, since `jest` itself times out in this sandbox on this project (see the project's own standing note on this), a standalone plain-Node script (`_manual_verify_email_resync.ts`) importing the real `UsersService` class with mocked Prisma calls was run via `npx ts-node-transpile-only`, printing `ALL PASSED` across all 8 assertions covering all 4 scenarios (same email/no-op, different email/resync, failed resync/fallback, no existing user/create) — a genuine, executed run, not just a syntax check. The overload-fact-duplication fix was verified with a new backend e2e test: completes onboarding once with one overload answer, confirms exactly one matching memory fact exists; completes it again on the same user with a *different* overload answer; confirms there's still exactly one matching fact, it's the *same row* (`id` unchanged), and its content reflects only the new answer, not the old one. A full syntax sweep passed (252 files, 0 diagnostics — one new file, `_manual_verify_email_resync.ts`, plus edits to `users.service.ts`, `users.service.spec.ts`, `memory.service.ts`, `onboarding.service.ts`, `apollo-client.ts`, `Providers.tsx`, `settings/page.tsx`, `queries.ts`, and `onboarding/page.tsx`). What's *not* verified: the new e2e test has never actually run against a real Postgres database here (same standing sandbox limitation as every backend e2e test in this project — no Docker, no local Postgres), and the Clerk `openUserProfile()` button has never been clicked against a real Clerk account, for the reasons above. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` yourself once you're on a normal machine with Postgres running, and click the button for real once you have a Clerk-mode deployment.

## Picking up the No visible auto-plan indicator increment

Nothing new to set up — no schema change, no new query beyond adding `triggerEvent` to two existing selection sets, no new frontend route. Open `/today`: if you complete a task, log a mood/energy check-in, finish a habit or routine, write a journal entry, complete a focus session, or submit a daily reflection — any of the eight real auto-replan triggers — and it provokes a fresh DAY-scope plan within that trigger's 10-minute cooldown window, the resulting plan card now shows a small "Auto-generated" pill next to "Proposed plan" (or next to the "last plan was applied" text once you accept it). Tapping "Generate my plan" yourself never shows it — that's `manual_request`, not one of the `auto_*` triggers.

A few things worth knowing:

- **The badge is intentionally plain text, not an icon** — matching the existing "Next-day" lag badge's own established convention of never relying on color alone to convey state.
- **It's easy to miss in normal use, on purpose** — the badge doesn't call attention to itself with color or animation; it's there for anyone who goes looking for "did I do this or did the app," not a notification.
- **`WeeklyPlanCard`'s copy of the badge inherits that card's own existing refetch-timing gap** — see the "auto-generated WEEK/MONTH plan doesn't show up until it next refetches" bullet below; the badge is real and correct the moment it does render, it just might not render immediately.
- **A small, unrelated typing-only diagnostic was fixed along the way**: `PushSubscribeButton.tsx`'s `applicationServerKey` argument stopped type-checking under the TypeScript version now installed in this sandbox (5.9.3, newer than this project was last checked against) — a real tightening of `ArrayBufferLike`'s typing upstream, not a runtime bug, fixed with a narrow `as BufferSource` cast and a comment explaining why. Unrelated to this increment's own scope, fixed only because it showed up as a real diagnostic during this increment's own syntax sweep and this project's standing discipline is to keep that sweep at 0, not to leave known drift sitting around.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (No visible auto-plan indicator)

The underlying fact the badge depends on — that every real auto-trigger writes the correct `auto_*` string, and a manual request writes `manual_request` — was already independently proven by eight existing backend e2e tests in `app.e2e-spec.ts`'s "Automatic re-planning" suite, unchanged by this increment. What's new here is purely frontend: `AiPlanCard.tsx`/`WeeklyPlanCard.tsx` gained an `isAutoGenerated` helper and a badge in two render branches each, and `queries.ts` gained the one new field. `ai-plan-review.spec.ts` gained a negative-result assertion (the badge is confirmed absent after a real manual generate-and-accept flow). A full syntax sweep passed (252 files, 0 diagnostics — edits to `queries.ts`, `AiPlanCard.tsx`, `WeeklyPlanCard.tsx`, `ai-plan-review.spec.ts`, and the unrelated `PushSubscribeButton.tsx` fix noted above). What's *not* verified: the badge has never actually been seen rendering in a real browser against a real auto-generated plan — that would need a real Postgres, a real auto-trigger actually firing, and a real page load, none of which this sandbox can do together. The JSX was checked by hand against the exact conditions each real trigger sets (`triggerEvent` values, `PROPOSED`/`ACCEPTED`/`EDITED` statuses), which is the same "checked by hand, not confirmed by a real run" caveat every other Playwright-adjacent piece of this project carries — worth actually loading `/today` after completing a task on a normal machine to see it for real.

## Picking up the Goal progress view increment

Nothing new to set up — no migration (`taskCount`/`completedTaskCount` are computed, not columns), no new frontend route. Open `/goals`: any goal with at least one linked task now shows "N of M tasks done" plus a small progress bar; a goal with nothing linked yet shows a plain "No tasks linked yet" line instead. Link a task to a goal from Today's own goal picker (in the quick-add box, only shown once at least one active goal exists), complete it, and reload `/goals` to watch the numbers move.

A few things worth knowing:

- **A cancelled task never counts against a goal's total** — `taskCount` only ever counts PENDING/IN_PROGRESS/COMPLETED tasks, the same "cancelled is set apart, not held against you" rule the Tasks screen's own Open/Cancelled split already follows. A goal with 3 real tasks and 2 abandoned ones still shows "of 3," not "of 5."
- **The count is always computed fresh, on every read, from real task rows** — there's no persisted counter to go stale or fall out of sync with reality; the tradeoff is one extra `groupBy` query per goals-list read, which is cheap at this app's scale (a handful of goals, a handful of tasks each).
- **A subtask counts the same as its parent task toward a goal's progress if it happens to have its own `goalId`** — subtasks don't inherit their parent's goal link (see the Subtask UI increment's own "what's not built yet" note), so this only comes up if you deliberately link a subtask to a goal directly, which the UI doesn't currently prompt for either way.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Goal progress view)

A new backend e2e test seeds three real tasks under one goal, completes one (`completeTask`), cancels one (`cancelTask`), and leaves the third untouched, then asserts the live GraphQL response reports exactly `{taskCount: 2, completedTaskCount: 1}` — a concrete proof of both the cancelled-exclusion rule and the completed-only-counting rule, not just a read of the code that's supposed to implement them. A second assertion confirms a brand-new goal reports `{taskCount: 0, completedTaskCount: 0}` immediately, without needing a task to exist first. Two new Playwright specs cover the same two shapes from the UI side: the empty-state nudge text and the absence of a progress bar on a task-free goal, and the full path (create a goal, link a task to it from Today, complete the task, confirm `/goals` shows "1 of 1 task done" and the progress bar's `aria-valuenow` reads `1`). The progress bar's own color contrast was independently computed, not assumed — `accent` against `background`/`surface` in both light and dark mode all clear WCAG 1.4.11's 3:1 non-text minimum with real margin (5.55:1 / 5.19:1 light, 4.98:1 / 4.61:1 dark). A full syntax sweep passed (252 files, 0 diagnostics — edits to `goal.model.ts`, `goals.service.ts`, `queries.ts`, `goals/page.tsx`, `goals.spec.ts`, and `app.e2e-spec.ts`; no new files). What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as every backend e2e test and Playwright spec in this project — neither the new backend test nor the two new Playwright specs have actually run against a real Postgres/real browser here. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e`, yourself once you're on a normal machine.

## Picking up the Real billing/subscription management increment

Nothing new to set up — no migration (`Subscription.tier`/`status`/`currentPeriodEnd` were all real columns already), no new frontend route. Open `/settings`, scroll to the Account card's new "Plan" section: three buttons (Free/Plus/Pro), the current one shown pressed/highlighted. Click a different one — it switches immediately, no confirmation step, and a "Renews" row appears under Email/Status once you're on a paid tier.

A few things worth knowing:

- **No card form, no fake payment collection, anywhere** — a deliberate choice, not a missing feature. Building a form that asks for a card number and does nothing real with it would look like a real charge is happening when nothing is; this project has made this same call before (see the Editable email increment's own reasoning for not rebuilding email verification locally).
- **`status` never leaves `ACTIVE` in this build** — `PAST_DUE`/`CANCELED` are real enum values a real payment failure or a real cancellation flow would produce; neither exists here, so they're reachable in the schema but not from this UI.
- **The picker's own first draft had a real, caught contrast bug** — see the increment's own README entry above for the exact numbers (4.13:1 measured, replaced with a pairing verified at 5.55:1). Worth mentioning if asked "how do you know your new UI is accessible" — by computing it, the same way every color decision in the original accessibility pass was, not by eyeballing it.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Real billing/subscription management)

A new backend e2e test moves a real account's `Subscription` row from Free to Plus and back, asserting `changeSubscriptionTier`'s exact real behavior in both directions: moving to Plus sets a real `currentPeriodEnd` between 29 and 31 days out (computed from `Date.now()` at assertion time, not a hardcoded date), and moving back to Free clears it to `null` — plus a fresh `me` re-read confirming it's really persisted, not just echoed back from the mutation. A new Playwright spec confirms the picker's `aria-pressed` state actually changes on click and survives a reload, then switches back to Free at the end, since this shared dev-auth account is read by every other spec in the suite too. The contrast fix itself was verified with the same relative-luminance formula and script used throughout this project's accessibility work, not eyeballed. A full syntax sweep passed (253 files, 0 diagnostics — one new file, `change-subscription-tier.payload.ts`, plus edits to `users.service.ts`, `users.resolver.ts`, `subscription.model.ts` import only, `queries.ts`, `settings/page.tsx`, `settings.spec.ts`, and `app.e2e-spec.ts`). What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as every backend e2e test and Playwright spec in this project — neither has actually run against a real Postgres/real browser here. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e`, yourself once you're on a normal machine.

## Picking up the Linking habits to goals increment

This one needs a real migration — `Habit.goalId` is a new column, added directly to `schema.prisma` the same way `phoneNumber`/`timezoneManual` were earlier in this project's history, and not yet run against a real database anywhere. Run this on your own machine before anything else:

```
cd apps/backend
npx prisma migrate dev --name add_habit_goal_link
```
