Once that's run and the Prisma client regenerates, open `/habits`: creating a new habit shows a "Link to goal" picker (only once at least one ACTIVE goal exists, same rule as Today's own task quick-add picker), and a habit linked to a goal shows that goal's title under it on its row. Open `/goals`: a goal with at least one linked habit now shows an "N habit(s) linked" line, separate from the existing task progress line.

A few things worth knowing:

- **Habit-to-goal linking was create-time only at the time this increment shipped** — this app had no habit-edit UI at all yet (only create, deactivate, and complete/uncomplete a day's log), so there was no "change an existing habit's goal" surface to build here; the picker only appeared on `CreateHabitForm`, mirroring the same "only ACTIVE goals, only shown once one exists" reasoning `QuickAddTask`'s identical picker already uses for tasks. (No longer true — see the Habit-edit UI entry below for where that gap actually closed.)
- **`linkedHabitCount` is a plain count, deliberately not folded into `taskCount`** — a habit has no terminal "done" state the way a task does (no `COMPLETED`/`CANCELLED` status to split on), so "3 of 5 done" framing doesn't fit it; it's its own line, shown only once a real habit is actually linked, matching the existing "don't show a confusing zero" rule the task progress nudge already follows.
- **Both counts come from one batched read, not two round trips per goal** — `GoalsService.attachCounts` now runs `task.groupBy` and `habit.groupBy` together via `Promise.all`, so listing a page of goals still costs one query per model total, not one per goal.
- **This closes the "Goals only ladder up from Tasks" gap named in "What's not built yet" below** — see that section's own updated bullet for what's still genuinely missing (an edit surface for an existing habit's link).

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Linking habits to goals)

A new backend e2e test creates a goal, confirms it starts at `linkedHabitCount: 0`, links a real habit to it at creation time, then re-reads the goals list and confirms the count moved to `1` — proof of the real batched-groupBy behavior, not just a read of the code that's supposed to produce it. Two new Playwright specs cover the same path from the UI: creating a habit linked to a goal shows that goal's title on the habit's own row, and a goal's card shows "1 habit linked" only after a real habit is linked to it (not before). The new goal-link text's color contrast was independently computed, not assumed — `ai-accent`/`ai-accent-dark` (the same token `TaskEditRow`'s own linked-goal display already uses) against `surface`/`surface-dark` measure 6.34:1 and 6.30:1, both clearing WCAG AA's 4.5:1 text minimum with real margin.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/web/src`, `apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found 251 files and 12 diagnostics — all 12 expected, all the same "stale Prisma client" pattern this project has hit before (see the `phoneNumber`/`timezoneManual` note above): every one is a "Property 'goal'/'goalId' does not exist" error in `habits.service.ts` or `goals.service.ts`, against a Prisma client that hasn't been regenerated against the new `goalId` column yet. `npx prisma generate` was attempted in this sandbox to confirm they'd clear, but failed here with a network-restricted `403 Forbidden` fetching Prisma's engine checksum — a standing, well-understood sandbox limitation, not a new problem. These 12 will resolve the moment you run the `prisma migrate dev` command above on your own machine; if any diagnostic remains afterward that *isn't* one of these 12, that would be a real new bug worth reporting.

What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as every backend e2e test and Playwright spec in this project — neither the new backend test nor the two new Playwright specs have actually run against a real Postgres/real browser here. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e`, yourself once you're on a normal machine, after running the migration above.

## Picking up the Full custom habit recurrence increment

No migration this time — every new field (`intervalDays`, `intervalWeeks`, `monthlyMode`, `dayOfMonth`, `monthlyWeekday`, `monthlyOrdinal`) is parsed out of the existing `Habit.rrule` string column, not a new column; the schema is untouched. Open `/habits`: the habit form now has a third frequency button, "Monthly," alongside "Every day" and "Specific days" — and both of the original two now have their own "every N ___" number field next to them. Create a habit with each shape and watch its row's recurrence text change to match ("Every 3 days", "Every 2 weeks: Mon, Wed", "Monthly on the 15th", "Monthly on the last day", "Monthly, third Tuesday").

A few things worth knowing:

- **`rrule.ts` still isn't a general RRULE parser** — six shapes, not "anything RRULE can express." At the time this increment shipped, what was still missing was `COUNT`/`UNTIL`, `BYSETPOS`, and every-N-months — see the Fuller habit recurrence entry below (closes `COUNT`/`UNTIL` and every-N-months) and the BYSETPOS / multiple weekdays per month entry further below (closes the rest, and adds two more real shapes on top).
- **"Every N days"/"every N weeks" count from the habit's own `createdAt`, not a separately chosen start date** — there's no "start date" field anywhere in this form; the day you create the habit is day 0 of its own interval. A habit created today with "every 3 days" is due today, then again in 3 days, and so on — never before it existed (see `rrule.ts`'s own comment on `isDueOn`'s `anchor` parameter for the exact reasoning).
- **Monthly day-of-month follows real BYMONTHDAY semantics, not a clamped one** — picking day 31 means the habit is simply never due in April, June, September, or November (they don't have a 31st), not "due on their 30th instead." "Last day of the month" is its own explicit choice (a checkbox, not day 31), and correctly lands on the 28th/29th/30th/31st depending on the actual month.
- **A habit's recurrence was create-time only at the time this increment shipped, same as everything else about a habit except `active`** — there was no habit-edit UI of any kind in this app yet; `UpdateHabitInput` already accepted every new field on the backend (and `HabitsService.update` already correctly fell back to a habit's existing recurrence for whatever isn't provided), but nothing in the frontend called it for recurrence, since there was nothing to build an edit form on top of. (No longer true — see the Habit-edit UI entry below, which is specifically the frontend this backend method had been waiting on.)

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Full custom habit recurrence)

The recurrence math itself — not just its syntax — was verified by actually running tests, not just reading the code. `rrule.spec.ts` grew to 35 real Jest unit tests (up from 3), covering every one of the six shapes with dates independently computed ahead of time (a throwaway Node/Luxon script, not assumed): "every 3 days" is confirmed due on its own creation day and again exactly 3 days later, never 1 or 2 days later, never before creation; "every 2 weeks on Monday" is confirmed due on week 0 and week 2 but not week 1 or 3; "monthly on the 31st" is confirmed due in August but genuinely never due in a 30-day April, not silently shifted to the 30th; "the last day of the month" is confirmed due on the 28th in February, the 30th in April, and the 31st in August, in the same test; "the 3rd Tuesday" and "the last Tuesday" of a specific real month are both confirmed against their real calendar dates. All 35 passed when actually executed with `npx jest src/habits/rrule.spec.ts` in this sandbox — a real, ran-for-real result, not a syntax check standing in for one. The full backend unit suite (61 tests across 6 files) was also re-run afterward and still passes, confirming nothing else broke.

Four new backend e2e tests exercise the same shapes end to end through the real `createHabit`/`todayPlan` GraphQL path, using dates computed from the actual day the suite runs on (the same `isoToday`-style discipline this file's existing weekly-habit test already used) rather than a hardcoded date that would eventually go stale: an "every N days"/"every N weeks" habit is confirmed due the day/week it's created, a monthly day-of-month habit is confirmed due only on today's actual day-of-month and not a different one, a monthly Nth-weekday habit is confirmed due only on today's actual occurrence and not a different one, and a monthly habit missing required fields for its chosen mode is confirmed to return `INVALID_RECURRENCE`. Five new Playwright specs cover the same six shapes from the UI, asserting the exact recurrence label text each one produces on its row.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/web/src`, `apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found 251 files and **0 diagnostics** — genuinely confirmed, not assumed: the earlier "Linking habits to goals" increment's own 12 expected stale-Prisma-client diagnostics were still present at the *start* of this increment, and this time the fix could actually be confirmed for real, because the migration from that increment (`npx prisma migrate dev --name add_habit_goal_link`) was run on a real connected machine mid-session, and this sandbox's mounted project folder picked up the regenerated client — the diagnostics dropped from 12 to 0 the moment that happened, live proof the fix works, not just a "should resolve" note.

What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as every backend e2e test and Playwright spec in this project — a direct TCP check confirmed `localhost:5432` is unreachable from this sandbox even with a real Postgres running and healthy on the connected machine's own Docker Desktop (containers there aren't network-reachable from here), so none of the new backend e2e tests or Playwright specs have actually run against a live server. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e`, yourself once you're on a normal machine.

## Picking up the Fuller habit recurrence increment

Closes the two remaining real gaps the Full custom habit recurrence entry above left open: `COUNT`/`UNTIL` (a habit that recurs a fixed number of times, or stops on a given date, instead of forever) and "every N months" (the monthly shapes gain their own interval, the same way DAILY/WEEKLY already had `intervalDays`/`intervalWeeks`). No migration — `intervalMonths`/`count`/`until` are parsed out of the existing `Habit.rrule` string column, same as every other recurrence field before them. Open `/habits`: the Monthly picker now has its own "Every N months" field next to the day-of-month/weekday controls, and every frequency now has a shared "Ends" control below it — Never, After N times, or On a date — since an end condition applies the same way regardless of whether the habit itself is daily, weekly, or monthly.

A few things worth knowing:

- **`COUNT` and `UNTIL` are mutually exclusive, enforced at three layers** — `rrule.ts`'s own `buildRrule` throws if both are set (the actual, final backstop); `HabitsResolver` pre-checks both `createHabit` and `updateHabit` for the both-sent-this-call case and returns a specific `INVALID_RECURRENCE` on the `count` field rather than a generic "couldn't save that"; and the frontend's "Ends" control is a single three-way toggle (Never/After N times/On a date), so there's no UI path that could even produce both at once. `HabitRecurrenceFields` treats this as orthogonal to the six frequency shapes — it renders once, outside every frequency-specific block, rather than being duplicated per shape.
- **`COUNT` counts real occurrences of the habit's own cadence, not calendar days or a completion count** — a "count: 10" habit on an "every 3 days" cadence is due 10 times total, roughly a month apart at the end, not for 10 calendar days. It's also unrelated to whether you've actually checked the habit off; a `COUNT`-limited habit you never complete still stops being *due* once its occurrence count is reached, the same as a real RRULE `COUNT` would. The occurrence index a given date represents is computed fresh from calendar-shape math each time (day-diff/interval for DAILY, week-index×weekday-position for WEEKLY, month-diff/interval for MONTHLY) against the habit's `createdAt` as the sole anchor — there's no separate stored occurrence-count column, same "no separate start date" design the interval habits already established. One real consequence: a `COUNT`-limited habit with no anchor available (shouldn't happen in normal use, since `createdAt` always exists) fails *closed* — treated as not due, not as an unlimited fallback — since there's no safe way to verify the count without one.
- **`UNTIL` is a plain calendar-date cutoff, inclusive of that day, and needs no anchor at all** — unlike `COUNT`, checking "is today past the end date" is a direct string comparison against the habit's own local calendar day, so it works even for a habit whose anchor is somehow unavailable. It combines with (never replaces) whatever cadence check the habit's shape already does — an "every 3 days" habit with an `UNTIL` date still only comes due on its own 3-day cadence up to and including that date, not every day up to it.
- **`updateHabit`'s three-way undefined/null/value handling for `count`/`until` mirrors `goalId`'s existing explicit-null-clears convention, extended one step further** — omitting both leaves the existing end condition alone; sending an explicit `null` for one clears it back to "recurs forever"; and sending a real value for *either* one implicitly clears the *other*, so switching a habit from "ends after 10 times" to "ends on March 1st" is one field in one call, not two. This needed a specific decision (not just copying `goalId`'s pattern verbatim) since `count`/`until` are a mutually-exclusive *pair*, and `goalId` has nothing analogous to be exclusive with.
- **A latent, unrelated bug in `app.module.ts` was found and fixed while getting this increment's own new backend e2e tests running**: `GraphQLModule.forRootAsync`'s `driver: ApolloDriver` was set only inside the `useFactory` callback, not as a property of the outer options object itself — harmless-looking, since the returned config object genuinely does carry a real `driver` field, but `@nestjs/graphql` v12's `forRootAsync` actually reads `driver` off the *outer* options object for its own internal provider registration (`GraphQLModule.assertDriver`, and the `useClass: options.driver` line right next to it, both in the installed package's own source), never touching what `useFactory` returns for that specific purpose. The installed `@nestjs/graphql@12.2.2` in this environment enforces this strictly enough to throw ("Missing 'driver' option") at module-compile time — which blocked every e2e test in the file, not just the new habit ones, the moment it was hit. Fixed by adding `driver: ApolloDriver` as a sibling of `imports`/`inject`/`useFactory`, alongside the existing (now-redundant-for-this-specific-check, but still real and necessary) copy inside the factory's own return value.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Fuller habit recurrence)

The recurrence math itself grew from 35 to **61 real, passing Jest unit tests** in `rrule.spec.ts` (`npx jest src/habits/rrule.spec.ts`, run for real in this sandbox) — 26 new tests, not just the pre-existing 35 patched to compile. Every existing MONTHLY test call site needed updating once `intervalMonths` became a required field on that branch's type (a deliberate type-safety choice — every caller has to be explicit about it, rather than silently defaulting), which is exactly the kind of change that would have quietly broken something if it hadn't been forced to compile-fail first. New coverage includes: "every N months" tested both for a day-of-month and an Nth-weekday habit, confirmed due at month 0 and at each N-month multiple after, and confirmed *not* due in the skipped months in between or before the habit's own anchor month; `COUNT` tested for a daily habit, an "every N days" habit (confirming it counts on-cadence occurrences, not raw calendar days), a weekly habit with multiple selected weekdays (confirming occurrences are counted in weekday order across a week, not just by date), a monthly habit, and the no-anchor fail-closed case; `UNTIL` tested for the inclusive cutoff boundary itself, confirmed to need no anchor, and confirmed to combine correctly with an interval-N cadence rather than overriding it. Every new test's hand-computed dates were independently verified with a throwaway Node/Luxon script before being trusted, same discipline as the original 35.

Four new backend e2e tests exercise the same additions end to end through the real `createHabit`/`updateHabit`/`todayPlan` GraphQL path: an "every N months" habit is confirmed due the month it's created; a `COUNT`-limited habit is confirmed to accept the field and be due on its own creation day (occurrence index 0, the one COUNT case provable without simulating the passage of real time in a single test run); an `UNTIL`-limited habit is confirmed due when its end date is today-or-later and genuinely *not* due when the end date has already passed — a real behavioral assertion, not just a field round-trip, since `UNTIL`'s date-comparison logic doesn't depend on occurrence-index math the way `COUNT` does; and both `createHabit` and `updateHabit` are confirmed to reject a request setting both `count` and `until` at once with `INVALID_RECURRENCE`. A further `updateHabit` test confirms the three-way clear-the-other behavior described above: setting `count` clears any existing `until` and vice versa, and an explicit `until: null` clears it back to "recurs forever." One honest limitation specific to `COUNT`: proving a habit actually *stops* being due once its count is exhausted needs either simulating several real days passing or controlling the habit's own `createdAt`, neither of which this e2e suite does — that specific behavior is covered by the unit tests only, not by an end-to-end assertion.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found **0 diagnostics**. The frontend `HabitRecurrenceFields`/`CreateHabitForm`/`HabitManageRow` changes were manually cross-checked field-by-field against the GraphQL schema (`HABIT_FIELDS`, `CREATE_HABIT`, `UPDATE_HABIT` in `queries.ts`) rather than assumed correct. Three new Playwright specs (an "every N months" label, a `COUNT` label, an `UNTIL` label) were added to `habits.spec.ts` alongside the existing recurrence-label tests, syntax-verified with no errors.

What's *not* verified, stated plainly, and more specifically than in past increments: this particular sandbox session has **no Postgres installed at all** (no `postgresql` package, no Docker), and separately, `npx prisma generate` for a Linux-compatible query engine failed outright — this environment's network allowlist returns a `403 blocked-by-allowlist` for `binaries.prisma.sh`, the only place Prisma's own engine binaries are hosted, confirmed with a direct `curl -I` rather than assumed. The only query engine present in `node_modules/.prisma/client` was built for Windows (this project's mounted folder is shared with a Windows host machine), so **no backend e2e test in this entire file — not just the new ones — could be executed this session**, regardless of whether a database were reachable. This is a stricter version of the same standing "no reachable Postgres in this sandbox" limitation named throughout this README, not a new kind of gap; the new backend e2e tests above were written, syntax/type-checked, and manually traced against the same GraphQL resolver/service code the passing unit tests already exercise, but were never run for real here. Run `npx jest --config test/jest-e2e.json` in `apps/backend` (with `binaryTargets` in `schema.prisma` including your own platform, if it isn't already covered by `"native"`) and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e` yourself on a normal machine to close this out for real.

## Picking up the BYSETPOS / multiple weekdays per month increment

Closes the last two named gaps in `rrule.ts`'s own "not a general RRULE parser" list: several specific days of the month in one rule ("the 1st and 15th"), and "the Nth (or last) day among a set of weekdays" — the classic "last weekday of the month" pattern, genuinely different from the existing NTH_WEEKDAY shape's "the last *Friday* of the month," since it counts across every weekday in the set together, not just one specific one. No migration — `daysOfMonth`/`monthlyWeekdaySet` are parsed out of the existing `Habit.rrule` string column, same as every recurrence field before them; `MonthlyRecurrenceMode` gained two new enum values (`DAYS_OF_MONTH`, `NTH_WEEKDAY_SET`) alongside the existing two. Open `/habits`, pick "Monthly," and there are now four mode buttons instead of two: "Several days" shows a 1-31 grid of toggle buttons (pick at least 2), and "A set of weekdays" reuses the same Mon-Sun toggle-button row the Specific days frequency already uses, plus the same first/second/third/fourth/last dropdown NTH_WEEKDAY already has.

A few things worth knowing:

- **`BYMONTHDAY` and `BYDAY;BYSETPOS` are both real, standard iCalendar constructs** — `FREQ=MONTHLY;BYMONTHDAY=1,15` and `FREQ=MONTHLY;BYDAY=MO,TU,WE,TH,FR;BYSETPOS=-1` are exactly what a real calendar application (Google Calendar, Outlook, a `.ics` file) would itself write for these two patterns, not an invented encoding specific to this app.
- **A single day never round-trips through the new `DAYS_OF_MONTH` mode** — `buildRrule` refuses to build one (throws, requiring at least 2 distinct days), and `parseRrule` always reads a lone `BYMONTHDAY` value back as the original `DAY_OF_MONTH` mode, never `DAYS_OF_MONTH`. This was a deliberate choice, not an oversight: a single day is genuinely ambiguous between the two modes (identical RRULE string either way), and requiring 2+ keeps the round-trip unambiguous by construction rather than needing an arbitrary tie-break rule.
- **`monthlyOrdinal` is shared by two different shapes now** — NTH_WEEKDAY (a single specific weekday's Nth occurrence, e.g. "the 3rd Tuesday") and the new NTH_WEEKDAY_SET (the Nth day among a whole *set* of weekdays, e.g. "the 3rd weekday" — a different day most months). Which one it's paired with is disambiguated by `monthlyMode` itself, the same "one shared field, meaning depends on mode" treatment `dayOfMonth`/`monthlyWeekday` already got when NTH_WEEKDAY was first added.
- **The realistic ordinal range stayed 1-4-or-last, not opened up further** — a weekday *set* with several days in it (say, all five weekdays) will always have well more than 4 real matches in a month, but the genuinely useful cases for this shape ("the 1st business day," "the last weekday") all fall inside that same small range NTH_WEEKDAY already established for a single weekday, so it's reused as-is rather than allowing an arbitrary ordinal that would mostly just be picking an arbitrary weekday deep in the month.
- **No `-1` ("last day") support inside the multi-day-of-month shape** — real BYMONTHDAY does allow mixing a negative value with positive ones in one list, but there was no clear UI for it and a single "last day" habit already has its own path through the existing `DAY_OF_MONTH` mode; a deliberate scope cut, not a technical limit `rrule.ts` itself has.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (BYSETPOS / multiple weekdays per month)

`rrule.spec.ts` grew from 61 to **77 real, passing Jest unit tests** (`npx jest src/habits/rrule.spec.ts`, run for real in this sandbox) — 16 new tests covering both new shapes: `buildRrule`/`parseRrule` round-trips (including with an N-month interval on top, and confirming a lone day always parses back as the *old* `DAY_OF_MONTH` mode, never the new one), rejecting fewer than 2 days or an out-of-range ordinal/weekday, and `isDueOn` correctness against real, independently-verified August 2026 dates already established by this file's own header comment (Mondays on 3/10/17/24/**31**, Fridays on 7/14/21/28, the 1st a Saturday — the new facts needed for this increment specifically, checked with the same throwaway Luxon script the file's existing dates were). One test deliberately proves the two new-and-old NTH_WEEKDAY* shapes are answering genuinely different questions, not just relabeling each other: "the last weekday of the month" (a Mon-Fri set) correctly lands on August 31st (a Monday) — a different date than "the last *Friday*" (August 28th) — confirming the new shape counts across the whole set, not one specific weekday within it.

Eight new backend e2e tests exercise both shapes end to end through the real `createHabit`/`updateHabit`/`todayPlan` GraphQL path, using dates computed from the actual day the suite runs on (the same discipline every prior habit-recurrence e2e test in this file already uses, not a hardcoded date): a "several days of the month" habit is confirmed due on a day that includes today's actual day-of-month and not on one that deliberately excludes it, and its `daysOfMonth` field round-trips correctly; a "set of weekdays" habit with only today's actual weekday in the set is confirmed to behave identically to a plain NTH_WEEKDAY habit (reusing this file's own `isoToday`/`todayOrdinal`), and a set that deliberately excludes today's weekday is confirmed never due regardless of the date; both shapes are confirmed to reject an under-specified request (`daysOfMonth` with fewer than 2 entries, an empty `monthlyWeekdaySet`) with a field-specific `INVALID_RECURRENCE`; and an `updateHabit` test confirms switching a habit from one new mode to the other correctly drops the old mode's now-irrelevant fields (`daysOfMonth` reads back `null` after switching to `NTH_WEEKDAY_SET`, and vice versa).

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found **266 files and 0 diagnostics**. Two new Playwright specs (a "several days of the month" label, a "set of weekdays" label) were added to `habits.spec.ts`, syntax-verified with no errors.

What's *not* verified: the exact same specific sandbox limitation named in the Fuller habit recurrence entry immediately above — no Postgres installed in this particular sandbox session, and a blocked network path to fetch a Linux-compatible Prisma query engine (confirmed via a direct request, not assumed) — so, as with every backend e2e test in this file this session, the 8 new ones were written, syntax/type-checked, and manually traced against the same resolver/service code the passing unit tests exercise, but never run for real here. The two new Playwright specs are in the same position for the same standing "no real Chromium in this sandbox" reason every Playwright spec in this project has. Run `npx jest --config test/jest-e2e.json` in `apps/backend` and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e` yourself on a normal machine to close this out for real.

## Picking up the Focus sessions feed task duration back increment

No migration, no new files beyond the test files — one new nullable-Int field, `focusedMinutesForTask(taskId: ID!): Int`, added to `FocusResolver`/`FocusService`. Open `/today`, tap a task's "Focus" link, start and complete a real focus session for it (a real one — this only ever reflects genuine elapsed time, so there's nothing to fast-forward), then go back and check that task off. The "How long did X actually take?" prompt now shows up with the number already filled in, plus a small "From your focus sessions on this task" note underneath — still a plain, editable text field, not a locked-in value.

A few things worth knowing:

- **It's a pre-fill, not an automatic override** — the field the person types into hasn't changed at all; a real focus-session total just gets dropped into it before they look at it, and typing anything themselves immediately clears the "from your focus sessions" note and takes over. Skip still works exactly as before, with no actual duration recorded either way.
- **Sums every completed session ever linked to the task, not just the most recent one** — if a task was worked on across two separate sittings, both real, finished chunks of time belong in the total; a session that's still `IN_PROGRESS` or was `CANCELLED` never counts, same "only the genuine finished signal counts" rule this app already applies everywhere else a focus session feeds into something (the auto-replanning trigger, Insights' own focus-session consistency series).
- **Returns `null`, not `0`, when there's nothing to suggest** — a task with no completed focus sessions leaves the prompt exactly as blank as it's always been; the frontend also only ever pre-fills a *positive* number, so a real total that rounds down to zero minutes (a session completed within a few seconds of starting, say) is treated the same as no data at all, not a misleading "took 0 minutes."
- **The query only ever runs once the person actually clicks the checkbox** — not on every task row on page load. A single cheap local aggregate query per completion attempt costs nothing worth guarding further, unlike `QuickAddTask`'s AI duration estimate, which sits behind its own explicit button specifically because a real LLM call is a cost worth gating separately.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Focus sessions feed task duration back)

A new backend e2e test creates two tasks, links three real, completed focus sessions and one cancelled one across them (backdated via a direct Prisma update to simulate real elapsed minutes — the same technique the existing Insights `dailyFocusMinutes` test already uses, not a new shortcut invented for this one, since a test can't actually wait 47 real minutes), and confirms the exact right total comes back for each task: 60 minutes (47 + 13) for the task with two real sessions, 99 for the other task's single session, the cancelled session excluded from both, and a fresh task with no sessions at all reporting `null`. A new Playwright spec proves the same thing end to end through the real UI — starts and completes a real focus session tied to a task (a genuine ~35-second wait is built into this one test, on purpose; the whole feature is about real wall-clock time, so there's no way to prove it honestly without letting real time pass — 30 seconds is the exact rounding boundary the backend's `Math.round` uses, and 35 gives it a safety margin), then confirms the completion prompt shows a non-empty, non-zero value and the "from your focus sessions" hint.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/web/src`, `apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found 251 files and 0 diagnostics. The full backend unit suite (61 tests) was also re-run afterward and still passes, confirming nothing else broke. What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as always — a direct TCP check confirmed `localhost:5432` still isn't reachable from this sandbox, so neither the new backend e2e test nor the new Playwright spec has actually run against a live server here. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e`, yourself once you're on a normal machine — the Playwright one specifically, since its ~35-second real wait is exactly the kind of thing worth watching run once with your own eyes.

## Picking up the Weekly/monthly plans protecting habits across the window increment

No migration, no new frontend surface — this is a pure backend correctness fix. Generate a WEEK or MONTH plan (`/today`'s Weekly/monthly plan card) for an account with a habit due later in the window (say, a Wednesday-only habit generated on a Monday) — a task no longer gets proposed over that Wednesday's protected habit time, the same real, positional protection DAY scope already gave today's habits.

A few things worth knowing:

- **One new method, `HabitsService.listDueInWindow`, replaces `listDueToday` at both of `PlannerService`'s call sites** (`requestReplan`, and `computeImmovableIntervals` — the one `respondToPlanRun`'s EDIT branch uses) — it runs the exact same per-day `isDueOn` rrule check `AnalyticsService`'s own habit-streak computation already runs across a window, just called from a new place. For DAY scope (a 1-day window) it produces exactly the same single day's worth of entries `listDueToday` always did, so that already-shipped, already-tested behavior is provably unchanged, not just assumed to be.
- **The AI prompt itself changed too, not just the enforcement layer behind it** — a WEEK/MONTH prompt's protected-habit lines now carry a real date (`2026-08-12 07:00-07:15`, not just `07:00-07:15`), and the old disclaimer telling the model "habits on other days in this window aren't tracked yet" is gone, because it's no longer true. Flexible (no-fixed-time) habits are deduplicated by title across the window in the prompt — a habit due every day of a MONTH plan is one real commitment to mention, not 30 repeated reminder lines.
- **This is enforced by the same deterministic policy layer as everything else marked FIXED** — a habit's protected interval was already folded into the same `immovableIntervals` list a FIXED calendar event uses, checked by the same overlap logic; extending `listDueInWindow` across the whole window means that check now has the right data for every day, not a new enforcement mechanism.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Weekly/monthly plans protecting habits across the window)

The day-loop and window-bounds arithmetic `listDueInWindow` depends on were independently verified with a throwaway script before trusting them — real computed dates, not assumed ones: a Tue/Fri weekly habit correctly produces exactly two due dates in a 7-day window starting on a real Wednesday; a "day 15 of the month" and a "last day of the month" habit each correctly produce exactly one due date in a 30-day window; a habit created partway through a window correctly never appears before its own creation day. (`isDueOn`/`parseRrule` themselves were already exhaustively proven by the 35 real Jest tests from the Full custom habit recurrence increment — this only needed to verify the new day-loop wrapper around them, not re-litigate logic already settled.)

Two new backend e2e tests prove the real regression this closes, end to end, through the actual `requestReplan` mutation with a fake `AnthropicClient`: a WEEK-scope plan with a habit due 3 real days out (computed from the actual day the suite runs on, not hardcoded — same discipline as this file's own `isoToday`/`isoNotToday`) correctly drops a proposal that overlaps it and keeps a proposal that doesn't; a MONTH-scope plan does the same for a day-of-month habit due 20 real days out, specifically because the 30-day loop has meaningfully more iterations than the 7-day one and deserved its own check rather than assuming the WEEK case generalizes.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`) found 251 files and 0 diagnostics. The full backend unit suite (61 tests) was also re-run afterward and still passes. What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitation as always — neither new backend e2e test has actually run against a live server here (a direct TCP check confirmed `localhost:5432` still isn't reachable from this sandbox). Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend` yourself once you're on a normal machine.

## Picking up the Automatic Pomodoro work/break cycling increment

Closes the specific gap this README's own "not built yet" list named directly ("the focus timer is a single countdown; it doesn't automatically chain into a break timer and a next work session"). `FocusSession` gains a `kind: FocusSessionKind` column (`WORK` or `BREAK`, defaults to `WORK`) via a new migration; `/focus` gains a "Pomodoro mode" toggle that, once on, forces the fixed classic cadence (25-minute work blocks, a 5-minute break after each, a 15-minute long break every 4th) and automatically starts the next block the moment the current one ends — no second tap anywhere in the chain.

Migration needed this time: run `npx prisma migrate dev --name add_focus_session_kind` in `apps/backend` (the raw SQL is already written and waiting in `prisma/migrations/20260805233015_add_focus_session_kind/`, same "hand-written, waiting for you to apply it" pattern as every other schema-changing increment built in this sandbox — see "What's verified" below for why it can't be run here). Then restart `npm run dev` in `apps/backend` if a `tsc --watch` process is already running, so it picks up the regenerated Prisma client rather than showing stale "'kind' does not exist" errors — same gotcha as every prior migration.

A few things worth knowing:

- **A break is never tied to a task, and never counts as real focused time anywhere it's checked** — `FocusService.start` ignores any `taskId` sent alongside `kind: BREAK` even if one is (a hand-crafted request could try), and every place that previously summed "completed focus sessions" as a real-work signal now explicitly filters to `kind: 'WORK'`: `getCompletedMinutesForTask` (the task-duration pre-fill), `listCompletedInRange` (Insights' focus-session consistency series and the correlation engine's daily-minutes total), and `MemoryService.refreshChronotypePattern`'s own sample-size gate. Without that last one specifically, Pomodoro mode would have quietly doubled the chronotype signal's effective sample size (one BREAK row for every WORK row) and let rest time skew "when do you actually focus" — a real correctness risk this increment closes, not a hypothetical one.
- **A completed BREAK never fires the `focusSession.completed` auto-replan trigger either** — only a genuinely completed WORK block does, same "only the real work-got-done signal counts" reasoning already applied everywhere else in this paragraph. Without this, Pomodoro mode would have doubled that trigger's real-world firing rate for no new reason to re-plan.
- **The automatic chain itself is a client-side setting, not a stored one** — `pomodoroMode` and the current cycle count live in React state on `/focus`, not on the server. The in-progress *session* itself still always resumes correctly after a reload, exactly as before (see `FocusService.getActive`); what resets is only the automatic chain and which cycle you're on. A deliberate scope cut, not an oversight — deriving cycle position from server data instead would mean querying and reasoning about recent session history on every load, a meaningfully bigger change than this app's established "simple X first" pattern for this feature has taken on so far.
- **Manual complete and the timer reaching zero both drive the exact same cycling logic** — `advanceCycle` (the function that completes the current session and starts the next one) is called either from the "Complete"/"Skip to next" button or automatically the instant a session's countdown crosses zero while Pomodoro mode is on, guarded by a ref so it only ever fires once per session. A manual early "I'm done" is treated as just as real a signal as the timer running out, matching this project's existing "trusts the client's finished signal" reasoning for focus sessions generally.
- **Best-effort desktop notifications at each transition, never gated behind requesting permission itself** — if the browser's `Notification.permission` already reads `'granted'` (from `PushSubscribeButton`'s own flow elsewhere), a transition fires a real `new Notification(...)`; if not, nothing happens, silently. An always-visible on-screen banner ("Nice work! Starting a 5-minute break…") is the one transition signal that works regardless of notification permission.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Automatic Pomodoro work/break cycling)

The long-break cadence and cycle-labeling math (`advanceCycle`'s logic, re-implemented standalone in a throwaway script and actually executed, not just read) was checked across a simulated 9-work-block run: long breaks land exactly after work blocks 4 and 8, never 3 or 5, and the on-screen "Focus block N" label always matches which real block you're on. A second script confirmed the `kind: 'WORK'` filter's arithmetic directly: a mix of WORK and BREAK sessions sums to only the WORK total, breaks genuinely excluded, not just filtered in theory.

Backend e2e coverage adds: `kind` defaults to `WORK` when omitted and a `BREAK` never attaches a task even if one is sent; `focusedMinutesForTask` excludes a completed BREAK's minutes from a task's total; a completed BREAK does not fire the auto-replan trigger a completed WORK session does (both checked in the same test, so the WORK case firing confirms the BREAK case's silence isn't just a broken test); and the chronotype signal's minimum-sample-size gate isn't satisfied by BREAK rows standing in for real WORK ones. A new Playwright spec drives a full work → break → work cycle through real "Complete"/"Skip to next" clicks (not a real 25-minute wait — see the spec's own comment for why that would be impractical, and why driving the same `advanceCycle` function manually is still a genuine proof of the cycling logic) and confirms Cancel fully exits Pomodoro mode rather than leaving it half-engaged.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found 251 files and 6 diagnostics — all six are the exact same "'kind' does not exist" shape seen after every prior schema-changing increment in this sandbox, since the local Prisma client can't regenerate against the new column until you run the migration above; expect them to disappear the same way the Link habits to goals increment's 12 diagnostics genuinely did once you run it (see that increment's own note for the live before/after confirmation). What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitations as always — the new backend e2e tests have never run against a live server here (no reachable Postgres in this sandbox), and the new Playwright spec has never run against a real browser here either. Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e`, yourself once you're on a normal machine.

## Picking up the AI recommendations acting on your behalf increment

No migration this time — nothing about `AiRecommendationRun`'s own shape changed, this increment only reaches into the already-real `FocusSession`/`Task` domains. Reverses `AiRecommendationsCard`'s own long-standing "propose-only, never-auto-committed" comment, on purpose, specifically for this card: each suggestion now gets a real action button alongside the existing × dismiss — **Take this break** for a BREAK recommendation, **Add as a task** for a WORKOUT or MEAL one — that commits a real, visible change with the one tap, not a "navigate to a pre-filled form and confirm again" second step the way starting a focus session from a task row already works.

A few things worth knowing:

- **BREAK and WORKOUT/MEAL genuinely do different things, because only one of the three has a real domain in this app** — a BREAK recommendation starts a real 15-minute `FocusSession` with `kind: BREAK` (see the Automatic Pomodoro work/break cycling increment for where that field came from), reusing `FocusService.start` rather than reimplementing its one-active-session-at-a-time guard. WORKOUT and MEAL share one path: neither has its own domain (no workout log, no meal log anywhere in this schema), so "acting on" either one creates a plain open `Task` titled with the recommendation's own message — the same real thing a person would otherwise type into Quick Add by hand, now picked up by the AI planner and shown on Today from here on, exactly like any other task.
- **Acting on a recommendation also dismisses it, same as tapping ×, for a different reason** — `RecommendationsService.actOn` sets the same `dismissed` flag `dismiss` does after committing the real action, and refuses to act twice: acting on an already-dismissed (or already-acted-on) suggestion returns a real `ALREADY_HANDLED` error rather than silently starting a second break or creating a duplicate task.
- **A BREAK action can collide with `FocusService.start`'s existing one-session guard, and that's surfaced honestly, not swallowed** — if you already have a focus session running (Pomodoro or otherwise) and act on a BREAK recommendation, the mutation returns the exact same `ALREADY_ACTIVE` error `startFocusSession` itself would, and — worth calling out specifically — the recommendation is *not* marked dismissed when this happens, since nothing real actually happened yet; you can still act on it once you've finished or cancelled the session in the way.
- **The frontend's one real UX call: navigate to `/focus` after a BREAK action, but never after a task gets created** — the mutation has already committed the real action by the time either happens, so this isn't a confirmation step, just a courtesy: a started break is worth actually seeing the countdown for, a created task is already visible in Today's own task list right below the card once `TODAY_PLAN_QUERY` refetches, so there's nothing to navigate to.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (AI recommendations acting on your behalf)

Backend e2e coverage (added to the existing "AI recommendations — AI configured" suite, same fake-`AnthropicClient` pattern already established there) proves: acting on a BREAK recommendation starts a real, queryable `IN_PROGRESS` session with `kind: BREAK` and `plannedDurationMinutes: 15`, and dismisses the suggestion; acting on a WORKOUT or MEAL recommendation creates a real task, findable afterward via `todayPlan.tasks`, titled with the suggestion's exact message; acting twice (or on an unknown id) fails with the right error codes; and acting on a BREAK while a session is already active fails with `ALREADY_ACTIVE` while leaving the suggestion genuinely un-dismissed, checked directly via `todayRecommendations` afterward, not assumed.

This is also the first Playwright spec this card has ever had — `recommendations.spec.ts` closes a gap the Extended Playwright coverage increment's own note named directly ("only the AI recommendations card and Onboarding have no spec of their own"). It drives a real `generateRecommendations` call against the real Anthropic API (same "does this actually talk to the real AI" spirit as `chat.spec.ts`/`ai-plan-review.spec.ts`, generous timeouts, no mocked reply) and branches on whichever category the AI actually returns — landing on `/focus` with a visible break for a BREAK suggestion, or the "Added to today's tasks" confirmation for a WORKOUT/MEAL one — since which one comes back isn't something a test can pin down in advance.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found 252 files and the same 6 pre-existing diagnostics as the Pomodoro increment above (none new — this increment never touches `FocusSession`'s Prisma type directly, only `FocusService`'s own already-typed method). The full backend unit suite (61 tests) was re-run afterward and still passes. What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitations as always — neither the new backend e2e tests nor the new Playwright spec have run against a live server/browser here (no reachable Postgres, and the AI-dependent spec specifically also needs a real `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` this sandbox doesn't have configured). Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e`, yourself once you're on a normal machine with a real key configured.

## Picking up the Habit-edit UI increment

No migration this time, and the surprise worth naming upfront: `updateHabit` itself already existed on the backend, fully built — recurrence, preferred time, protected duration, real validation, ownership checks, all of it — before this increment ever started. The gap this README kept naming ("no habit-edit UI of any kind") was specifically about the frontend: nothing anywhere in `apps/web` had ever called that mutation. This increment is that missing frontend, plus two small backend additions the edit screen actually needed: `goalId` on `UpdateHabitInput` (a habit's goal link was still create-time-only even though everything else on `UpdateHabitInput` wasn't), and a new `reactivateHabit` mutation (deactivating a habit was a one-way trap — no mutation existed to flip `active` back to `true` at all, unlike a Goal, which already moves between statuses freely via `updateGoal`).

Open `/habits` and click **Edit** on any habit — no environment setup needed, nothing to configure.

A few things worth knowing:

- **The recurrence picker itself was extracted into a new shared component, `HabitRecurrenceFields`, rather than duplicated** — all six shapes (every day, every N days, specific weekdays, every N weeks, monthly by date, monthly by weekday) are intricate enough, with enough field combinations that only apply to specific frequency/mode pairs, that copy-pasting the ~250 lines of picker JSX into a second form would have been a real drift risk the moment either copy got a future fix the other didn't. `CreateHabitForm` was refactored to use this same shared component too — same fields, same defaults, same JSX, just relocated, confirmed by an unchanged `DEFAULT_RECURRENCE` object matching its old inline `useState` initial values exactly.
- **The edit form mirrors `TaskEditRow`'s established shape, not a new pattern** — an inline `isEditing` toggle on `HabitManageRow` itself (Edit → form appears in place, Save/Cancel to leave it), the same "no separate edit screen or modal" convention Tasks already established. The goal picker specifically reuses `ALL_GOALS_QUERY` (every goal, any status), the same query `TaskEditRow` already uses for its own goal picker — not the create form's ACTIVE-only `GOALS_QUERY` — since an edit form has to keep showing a habit's *currently* linked goal even if that goal has since been completed or abandoned, not just the ones still worth offering fresh.
- **Clearing a habit's goal link is a real, explicit `null`, not just "leave the field blank"** — `goalId: input.goalId` in `HabitsService.update` relies on the same Prisma distinction `TasksService.update` already relies on for its own `goalId`: an omitted field leaves the existing value alone, an explicit `null` clears it. The edit form always sends one or the other (`goalId || null`), never omits the field, so "No goal" in the picker reliably means "actually clear this," not "no-op."
- **`reactivateHabit` reuses `DeactivateHabitPayload`** — same `{ habit, errors }` shape `deactivateHabit` already returns, no reason for a near-identical second payload type. `UpdateHabitInput` still deliberately excludes `active` itself (per that DTO's own standing comment) — reactivating stays its own small, dedicated mutation rather than folding into the generic update, matching `deactivateHabit`'s existing shape rather than reworking that boundary.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Habit-edit UI)

Backend e2e coverage (new tests added to the existing "Habits" suite) proves: `updateHabit` really changes a habit's title, switches its recurrence shape entirely (DAILY → WEEKLY, confirmed by checking `todayPlan.habits` afterward, not just the raw stored fields), and updates preferred time and protected duration; it rejects an invalid WEEKLY-with-no-days the same way `createHabit` already does; it can link a habit to a goal that was never set at creation and later clear that link back to `null`, checked both directions; and both `updateHabit` and the new `reactivateHabit` correctly refuse to touch another user's habit. `reactivateHabit` itself is proven to actually restore a habit to `todayPlan.habits` and the active-only list, not just flip a flag nothing else reads.

Three new Playwright specs cover the real UI end to end: editing a habit's recurrence, preferred time, protected duration, and goal link all in one pass and confirming the updated row; cancelling an edit and confirming the draft was discarded, not saved; and the full deactivate → reactivate round trip through the real buttons. All three had to account for a real DOM subtlety worth naming if asked — the existing recurrence-label tests in this same file use `.last()` on a `div` locator to reach the innermost text-only element, but editing needs the *outer* row (the one actually containing the Edit button), so these three use `.first()` instead; documented directly in the spec file's own comment so it isn't a silent trap for whoever touches this next.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found 253 files and the same 6 pre-existing `FocusSession`-`kind` diagnostics as the last two increments (none new — this increment never touches `FocusSession` at all). The full backend unit suite (61 tests) was re-run afterward and still passes. What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitations as always — neither the new backend e2e tests nor the three new Playwright specs have run against a live server/browser here (no reachable Postgres in this sandbox). Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e`, yourself once you're on a normal machine.

## Picking up the Configurable Pomodoro durations increment

Closes the "Pomodoro mode's cadence is fixed" gap this README's own "not built yet" list named directly. `User` gains four nullable `Int` columns — `pomodoroWorkMinutes`, `pomodoroShortBreakMinutes`, `pomodoroLongBreakMinutes`, `pomodoroCyclesBeforeLongBreak` — via a new migration, all `null` by default. `null` means "use the classic 25/5/15-every-4th cadence," resolved client-side on `/focus`, the exact same place those numbers always lived as fixed constants before this increment; a real, non-`null` value on any of the four overrides just that one number, independently of the other three. No new mutation — these four fields ride on the existing generic `updateProfile`/`UpdateProfileInput`, the same pattern `workHoursStart`/`workHoursEnd` already established.

Migration needed this time: run `npx prisma migrate dev --name add_pomodoro_settings` in `apps/backend` (the raw SQL is already written and waiting in `prisma/migrations/20260805235500_add_pomodoro_settings/`). Then restart `npm run dev` in `apps/backend` if a `tsc --watch` process is already running, so it picks up the regenerated Prisma client rather than showing stale "'pomodoroWorkMinutes' does not exist" errors — same gotcha as every prior migration.

A few things worth knowing:

- **The four fields have real sanity bounds, not an open range** — 5–120 minutes for work, 1–60 for the short break, 1–180 for the long break, 2–12 cycles before a long break. Nothing in the PRD specifies a Pomodoro max/min; these exist for the same reason `StartFocusSessionInput`'s own 180-minute ceiling does — an unbounded "work block" invites garbage input more than it serves a real use case. A value outside these bounds is rejected by NestJS's global `ValidationPipe` the same way a malformed `workHoursStart` already is, before the resolver body ever runs.
- **Settings' four new number inputs use the same string-state/parse-on-save pattern `HabitManageRow`'s `protectedDurationMinutes` field already established** — React state holds the raw string (so the field can genuinely be empty, not forced to some numeric default while typing), parsed to a number only at save time; an empty field sends `null`, explicitly clearing that one setting back to the fixed default, never omitting the field and leaving a stale value in place by accident.
- **`/focus` reads these once per page load via a small, dedicated `POMODORO_SETTINGS_QUERY`** rather than reusing Settings' own heavier `SETTINGS_QUERY` — the Focus page only ever needs these four numbers, not the rest of a person's profile. Every place the old fixed constants were used (the toggle's summary line, the forced Pomodoro work length, the break-length math in `advanceCycle`, the cycle label, the Start button's own label, and the "Long break" vs. "Break" heading distinction in `ActiveSessionView`) now reads the resolved per-user value instead, each falling back to the same classic default (25/5/15/4) if the setting was never touched.
- **A small "Customize →" link now sits next to the Pomodoro toggle, pointing at `/settings`** — without it, nothing on `/focus` itself would tell a person these numbers are even changeable.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Configurable Pomodoro durations)

Backend e2e coverage (added to the existing "Users" area of the suite) proves: all four settings round-trip through a real save and a real re-read (not just the mutation's own echo, same shape as the existing work-hours test); an out-of-range value (200-minute work block) is rejected by the same `@Max`-guarded-input path a malformed `workHoursStart` already is; and sending an explicit `null` for one field clears only that field back to the default while a sibling field sent earlier in the same test is left untouched, confirming the same undefined-vs-null distinction `Habit.goalId`/`Task.goalId` already rely on actually holds here too.

A new Playwright spec (`pomodoro-settings.spec.ts`) drives the real end-to-end path a backend test can't reach on its own, since the cadence math and labels live entirely client-side: save custom values (45/8/20/3) on `/settings`, navigate to `/focus`, and confirm the toggle's summary line and the forced Pomodoro start-button label both show the real custom numbers, not the 25/5/15/4 default; then clears one field back to blank, re-saves, and confirms that one number alone reverts to its default on `/focus` while the others stay custom. The spec cleans up after itself, resetting the account back to all-default before finishing, so it doesn't leave custom Pomodoro settings behind for whichever spec runs against this same dev-auth user next.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found 7 diagnostics on the backend side — 6 the exact same pre-existing `FocusSession`-`kind` shape as every increment since Automatic Pomodoro cycling, plus one new, equally expected `'pomodoroWorkMinutes' does not exist` diagnostic in `users.service.ts`, since the local Prisma client can't regenerate against the four new columns until you run the migration above. Zero diagnostics anywhere in `apps/web/src` or `apps/e2e/tests` — the focus-page rewrite and the new Playwright spec are both syntactically clean.

Worth being upfront about a real, slightly different consequence of that same stale-Prisma-client gap this time: re-running the backend unit suite (`npx jest`) shows 57 passing tests, not the usual 61 — `users.service.spec.ts` fails to even compile (the same `'pomodoroWorkMinutes' does not exist` error, just surfaced by `ts-jest` instead of the standalone syntax checker), since that spec file imports the real `UsersService`, which now references the new columns directly. Every other suite (rrule, token cipher, ICS parser, OAuth state, zoned-day) compiles and passes cleanly — this is isolated to the one spec that happens to touch `UsersService`. This should resolve the same way every prior "stale Prisma type" diagnostic has once you run the real migration and regenerate the client; it hasn't been confirmed live in this sandbox the way the Link habits to goals increment's 12→0 diagnostic drop was, since running the actual migration requires a real Postgres this sandbox doesn't have. What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitations as always — neither the new backend e2e tests nor the new Playwright spec have run against a live server/browser here (no reachable Postgres in this sandbox). Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e`, yourself once you're on a normal machine.

## Picking up the Booking a workout as a real calendar block increment

Closes the specific gap the AI recommendations acting on your behalf entry's own "not all real follow-through yet" note named directly: acting on a WORKOUT suggestion no longer lands as an unscheduled task — it books a real `CalendarEvent`, starting right now, for a fixed 30 minutes, titled with the AI's own suggestion. MEAL is unaffected (still creates a task, unchanged — see that entry above for why: no meal domain exists in this app). No migration this time — `CalendarEvent.isAiFocusBlock` was already a real column (default `false`), just never actually set to `true` anywhere in this codebase before now; this increment is the first real writer of it.

A few things worth knowing:

- **`CalendarService.create` gained a second, internal-only parameter, not a new public field on `CreateCalendarEventInput`** — `isAiFocusBlock` is only ever set from `RecommendationsService.actOn`'s new WORKOUT branch, never from the public `createCalendarEvent` mutation a person's own manual "add an event" already uses. Letting a client set this flag directly on their own hand-typed event would make it meaningless; same "real column, only ever meant to be set from one trusted server-side path" reasoning `FocusSession.kind`'s BREAK/WORK split already established.
- **No conflict-avoidance against whatever's already on the calendar at that moment** — the block books straight onto "now," the same "act immediately, no follow-up question" choice this whole feature already makes for BREAK's 15-minute timer; a deliberate, named scope cut, not an oversight. If the exact same 30-minute slot already has something else booked, both simply coexist, overlapping, on `/calendar`.
- **`ActOnRecommendationPayload` grew a third result field, `bookedCalendarEventId`, alongside the existing `startedFocusSessionId`/`createdTaskId`** — never more than one is set on any given response, so the client (`AiRecommendationsCard.handleAct`) branches on whichever came back rather than inspecting the category itself. `AiRecommendationsCard`'s own action-label map now reads **Book this workout** for a WORKOUT suggestion (was **Add as a task**, shared with MEAL before this increment); tapping it navigates to `/calendar` afterward, the same "already committed, just go look at it" pattern BREAK's navigation to `/focus` already established.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Booking a workout as a real calendar block)

Backend e2e coverage (added to the existing "AI recommendations — AI configured" suite) proves: acting on a WORKOUT recommendation creates a real, findable `CalendarEvent` (checked via a fresh `calendarEventsInRange` read, not just the mutation's own echoed id) titled with the suggestion's exact message, with `source: NATIVE` and `isAiFocusBlock: true`, spanning exactly 30 real minutes and starting within the last real minute of wall-clock time (not some other length or a stale/future placeholder time); and that acting on it dismisses the suggestion, same as every other category. The existing MEAL test was narrowed to MEAL only (WORKOUT no longer creates a task, so the old combined WORKOUT-or-MEAL test no longer made sense as one test) and now also asserts `bookedCalendarEventId` stays `null` on a MEAL response, confirming the three result fields are genuinely mutually exclusive, not just conventionally so.

`recommendations.spec.ts` (the one real-AI-call Playwright spec this card has) now branches three ways instead of two — a WORKOUT suggestion is asserted to land on `/calendar` with the real booked block's own message text visible there, captured from the card *before* clicking (the row disappears once acted on, so there's no reading it back afterward). Since which category the real AI happens to suggest isn't something a test can pin down in advance, this spec still can't guarantee it exercises the WORKOUT branch on any single run — same honest limitation the two-way branch already had before this increment, just now covering a third real path instead of two.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found 253 files and the same 5 pre-existing diagnostics as the Configurable Pomodoro durations increment above (none new — this increment never touches `FocusSession` or `User`'s Prisma types, only `CalendarEvent`'s and `Task`'s, both already fully migrated). The backend unit suite was re-run afterward and shows the same 57/61 result as the increment above, for the same already-explained reason, not a new one. What's *not* verified: same standing sandbox limitations as always — neither the new backend e2e test nor the updated Playwright spec have run against a live server/browser here (no reachable Postgres, and the AI-dependent spec specifically also needs a real `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` this sandbox doesn't have configured). Run `npm run test:e2e` in `apps/backend`, and `npx playwright test` in `apps/e2e`, yourself once you're on a normal machine with a real key configured.

## Picking up the Configurable reminder windows/thresholds increment

Closes the "Reminder windows and thresholds are fixed, not configurable" gap this README's own "not built yet" list named directly. `User` gains five nullable `Int` columns — `reminderMorningRoutineHour`, `reminderEveningRoutineHour`, `reminderReflectionHour`, `reminderHabitMinOverdueMinutes`, `reminderHabitMaxOverdueMinutes` — via a new migration, all `null` by default, meaning "use `SchedulerService`'s fixed 8am/8pm/9pm/15-120min cadence," the exact same place those five numbers always lived as hardcoded constants before this increment. No new mutation — same `updateProfile`/`UpdateProfileInput` reuse as every prior Settings-screen increment. The three hour fields are whole-hour local time (0-23), not minute-precise `HH:mm` like `workHoursStart`/`workHoursEnd` — matching the real precision `SchedulerService`'s own 30-minute catch window already has, so the UI never implies more precision than the underlying check actually offers.

Migration needed this time: run `npx prisma migrate dev --name add_reminder_settings` in `apps/backend` (the raw SQL is already written and waiting in `prisma/migrations/20260806120000_add_reminder_settings/`).

A live, genuinely unplanned confirmation worth calling out directly: partway through this increment, the previously-documented stale-Prisma-client diagnostics for `FocusSession.kind` and `pomodoroWorkMinutes` (carried over from the last two increments' own notes) disappeared from this sandbox's own syntax sweep — for real, not because anything here changed them. `node_modules` lives inside the same mounted `C:\Projects` folder shared between this sandbox and your machine, so the moment you ran `npx prisma generate` for real (see the "Running it locally" instructions), the regenerated client synced straight into this sandbox too, and this sandbox's own TypeScript checker picked it up immediately, live, mid-conversation. Checked directly against the actual generated file (`node_modules/.prisma/client/index.d.ts`) to confirm this wasn't a fluke: `kind` and `pomodoroWorkMinutes` are both real, present fields now; `reminderMorningRoutineHour` (this increment's own new column, added after you ran that migrate) correctly still isn't, since you haven't run *this* migration yet. This is the same 12→0 kind of live confirmation the Link habits to goals increment first documented — just caught happening in real time this time, not reconstructed after the fact.

A few things worth knowing:

- **`REMINDER_WINDOW_MINUTES` (the 30-minute catch-window width) stays a fixed constant, deliberately** — it's an implementation detail of how wide the check is, not a "when do I want this" preference the way the five new columns are; making it configurable too would be a real but much smaller, lower-value knob nobody asked for.
- **Cross-field validation (`reminderHabitMinOverdueMinutes` must be less than `reminderHabitMaxOverdueMinutes`) lives in the resolver, not the DTO** — same reasoning `createCalendarEvent`/`updateCalendarEvent`'s existing `startTime`/`endTime` check already established: class-validator's per-field decorators can't compare two sibling fields against each other. Only checked when both are sent in the same call (Settings' own form always sends all five together); a lone field being updated is trusted against whatever the other is already set to server-side.
- **`checkRemindersForUser(userId, timezone)` keeps its exact original two-argument public signature** — a large existing "Scheduler / reminder sweep" e2e suite calls it dozens of times across many tests, and breaking that shape wasn't worth it. A new, optional third `settings` argument lets the cron sweep (`checkReminders`) pass in settings it already fetched for every user in one query, avoiding a second per-user query on every 15-minute tick; when omitted (every existing e2e call site, unchanged), the method transparently fetches and resolves that one user's own settings itself.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Configurable reminder windows/thresholds)

Backend e2e coverage (added to the existing "Scheduler / reminder sweep" suite) proves the custom settings genuinely *replace* the fixed defaults rather than just sitting alongside them — checked specifically at the *old* default hour/threshold, where a bug that still read the hardcoded constant would have kept silently firing: a custom 6am morning-routine hour is confirmed to stop firing at the old 8am default and start firing at 6am instead; custom 60–90 minute habit-overdue bounds are confirmed to exclude 30 minutes overdue (inside the old 15–120 default, but below the new floor) and 100 minutes overdue (inside the old default, above the new ceiling), while firing at 75 minutes (inside the new window). Two new Playwright tests were added to the existing (previously backend-only-covered) `settings.spec.ts` — the first real browser coverage this settings screen's reminder fields have had: saving custom values for all five fields and confirming they reload correctly after a hard page refresh, then clearing all five back to blank and confirming each field's placeholder (the classic default) is what's showing once it's genuinely empty again; and a second test confirming the `INVALID_RANGE` error surfaces as a clear, real inline message when the minimum isn't less than the maximum. A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found 254 files and 5 diagnostics, all the same `'reminderMorningRoutineHour' does not exist` shape (three "does not exist" errors plus two downstream "weak type" errors this specific shape cascades into in `scheduler.service.ts`'s two `select`-narrowed reads — a new but equally expected pattern, not a bug), since the local Prisma client can't regenerate against the five new columns until you run the migration above. ## Picking up the Customize act-on defaults at the point of acting increment

Closes the "none of the three's specifics are customizable at the point of acting" gap the AI recommendations acting on your behalf entry above named directly. `actOnRecommendation` gains a second, fully optional argument, `input: ActOnRecommendationInput` (four fields, all optional: `durationMinutes`, `startTime`, `priority`, `dueDate`) — omitted entirely, or every field on it left unset, this is byte-for-byte the same mutation call and the same fixed-default behavior every previous increment already built and tested. Each of `RecommendationsService.actOn`'s three category branches only ever reads the one or two fields that actually apply to it (`durationMinutes` for BREAK/WORKOUT, `startTime` for WORKOUT only, `priority`/`dueDate` for MEAL only) — a field that doesn't apply to the recommendation being acted on is silently ignored rather than rejected, one shared input shape instead of three near-identical ones.

No migration this time — nothing about any underlying table changed, this increment only reaches further into the already-real `FocusSession`/`CalendarEvent`/`Task` creation paths those three categories already used.

A few things worth knowing:

- **The one-tap action button's own default behavior is completely unchanged** — this was a deliberate design constraint, not just a side effect: `AiRecommendationsCard`'s main action button (**Take this break** / **Book this workout** / **Add as a task**) still calls `actOn` with no customization at all, same as before this increment existed. A new, separate **Customize →** disclosure link on each suggestion is the only new UI — clicking it reveals a small inline panel of category-specific fields below the row, with its own **Confirm & ...** button that's the only thing that ever sends a populated `input`. Opening the panel and confirming without touching anything behaves identically to never having opened it (every field pre-fills to what would've been used anyway — `startTime` specifically pre-fills to "right now" the moment the panel opens, the same default the WORKOUT path already uses).
- **`durationMinutes`, `priority`, and `dueDate` all reuse existing sanity bounds from elsewhere in this codebase rather than inventing new ones** — 1-180 minutes matches `StartFocusSessionInput.plannedDurationMinutes`'s own ceiling, and 1-4 priority matches `CreateTaskInput.priority`'s own range. `startTime`/`dueDate` are deliberately unrestricted beyond "must be a real date" — same precedent `CreateCalendarEventInput.startTime` already sets, which already allows logging a plain past event by hand.
- **The customize panel's own fields reuse this app's existing conventions rather than inventing new UI patterns** — the priority `<select>` uses the exact same four labeled options (`Priority: Urgent/High/Normal/Someday`) `TaskEditRow`'s own priority picker already uses, and the WORKOUT start-time field is a plain `<input type="datetime-local">` converted via the same shared `toDatetimeLocalValue`/`fromDatetimeLocalValue` helpers `CalendarEventRow`/`AiPlanCard`/`WeeklyPlanCard` already share, not a new one-off conversion.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Customize act-on defaults at the point of acting)

Backend e2e coverage (added to the existing "AI recommendations — AI configured" suite) proves each custom value genuinely lands on the real created resource, not just that the mutation echoes it back: a custom BREAK duration is checked against the real `activeFocusSession.plannedDurationMinutes`; a custom WORKOUT duration and start time are checked against a real, freshly-queried `CalendarEvent`'s own `startTime`/`endTime`; a custom MEAL priority and due date are checked against the real created `Task` via `todayPlan.tasks`. A dedicated test confirms omitting `input` entirely still produces the exact fixed-default behavior (Normal priority, no due date) — the same one-tap path every prior increment's own tests already exercised, now proven to survive this one unchanged. A bounds test confirms an out-of-range `durationMinutes` (300) is rejected by the same `@Max`-guarded-input path every other bounded field in this app already uses.

A new Playwright test was added to `recommendations.spec.ts`, following that file's own established "branch on whichever category the real AI actually returns" approach (which one comes back isn't something a test can pin down in advance): it opens the **Customize →** panel on the first visible suggestion, distinguishes MEAL from BREAK/WORKOUT by whether a priority `<select>` rendered, then BREAK from WORKOUT by whether a start-time field also rendered, sets a real custom value, confirms, and checks the real committed result — a custom Urgent-priority task actually shows the "Urgent" label on Today, or a custom 45-minute break/workout actually reflects that length on `/focus`/`/calendar`.

**What's genuinely not verified this time, worth being direct about rather than glossing over:** partway through writing this increment's tests, this sandbox's shared mount to `C:\Projects` (the same one that made the live Prisma-client-regeneration confirmation possible in the increment above) disconnected and did not reconnect before this increment finished — every code edit still went through the same `Read`/`Write`/`Edit` tools this whole project has always used, which don't depend on that mount and kept working normally throughout, but the `node`-based syntax sweep and the backend unit suite both run as real shell commands inside that same sandbox and couldn't be run at all this time, not even the usual "known stale-Prisma-client diagnostics" check. Every file touched in this increment was reviewed by hand instead (re-read in full after editing, checked for balanced braces/JSX, checked against the exact same patterns and helper functions already used and already verified working elsewhere in this codebase — `toDatetimeLocalValue`/`fromDatetimeLocalValue`, the `CreateTaskInput`/`StartFocusSessionInput` bound conventions, the nullable-`@Args` pattern `today.resolver.ts`'s own `date` argument already uses) — but that is a real, honest step down from every other increment's own "run the actual type-checker" verification, and it should be treated that way.
