## Picking up the Configurable daily reflection questions increment

Closes the "daily reflection questions are fixed" gap named in "What's not built yet" above — but deliberately only the wording, not the shape. Three new nullable `User` columns (`reflectionWentWellLabel`, `reflectionChallengingLabel`, `reflectionCarryForwardLabel`) let you rename what's *displayed* for each of the three fixed reflection questions from a new "Daily reflection questions" section on `/settings`; `null` (the default for every account) falls back to the classic wording ("What went well today?" / "What was challenging?" / "What do you want to carry into tomorrow?"), resolved client-side in `/reflection`, the same place those strings always lived before this increment.

A few things worth knowing:

- **What deliberately didn't change, and why**: the JSON shape stored on a submitted `DailyReflection` is still always `{wentWell, challenging, carryForward}` — no fourth custom question, no renaming the underlying keys. `ReflectionService`'s AI summary prompt (`"What went well today: ...\nWhat was challenging: ...\nWhat they want to carry into tomorrow: ..."`) and Insights both hard-code those same three keys and that same fixed prompt wording, so changing the shape would have meant touching AI summarization and analytics too — a materially bigger increment than "let people rename three labels," and out of scope for this one. A new backend e2e test proves this boundary holds for real: it sets custom labels, submits a reflection, and confirms the AI summary prompt still reads the classic fixed wording verbatim.
- **The "Saved for today" short inline labels weren't touched** — `/reflection`'s read-only summary still always says "Went well: " / "Challenging: " / "Carrying forward: ", not your custom wording. Those are compact inline prefixes, not the actual question prompts (which do use your custom wording, both while filling out the form and — via the same `wentWellLabel`/`challengingLabel`/`carryForwardLabel` props — nowhere else), and were judged not worth threading through for three short inline strings that don't ask a question in the first place.
- **Same stale-generated-Prisma-client-types workaround as the last two increments** — `users.service.ts`'s `updateProfile()` now casts its whole Prisma `data:` object to `as any`, with a comment explaining why, exactly like `onboarding.service.ts` already does for `onboardingWizardStep`. This should be removed once `npx prisma generate` runs for real against a reachable engine.
- **`UpdateProfileInput`'s three new fields use `@Length(1, 150)`, not `@Length(1, 200)`** — deliberately shorter than `overloadSource`'s 200-character cap, since these are meant to be short question prompts (a UI label), not a free-form sentence.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Configurable daily reflection questions)

Two new backend e2e tests: one confirms a custom label saved via `updateProfile` doesn't change the wording sent to the AI summary prompt (submitting a reflection and asserting the captured prompt still contains the classic fixed strings verbatim); the other confirms all three columns default to `null` for a fresh account and that an explicit `null` genuinely clears a previously-saved custom label back to that default, the same null-round-trip pattern already proven for every other configurable field on this input. A new Playwright spec (`reflection-labels.spec.ts`) drives the real round trip end-to-end: set three custom labels on `/settings`, save, confirm `/reflection`'s form now shows those exact labels (and that the classic default wording is genuinely gone, not just supplemented — `getByLabel('What went well today?')` has zero matches), then clear all three fields and confirm the classic defaults come back — mirroring `pomodoro-settings.spec.ts`'s own "custom on Settings, verified on the concern page, cleared back to default" shape, including its own account cleanup at the end so `checkin-reflection.spec.ts`'s pre-existing reflection test (which locates the form by the classic default label text) keeps working against the same shared dev-auth account.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found **269 files and 0 diagnostics**.

What's *not* verified: the same standing sandbox limitation as every e2e test this session — no Postgres reachable, no reachable Prisma engine download — so the new migration, the `as any` cast's correctness, the two new backend e2e tests, and the new Playwright spec were all written and syntax/type-checked but never run for real here. Worth running `npx prisma generate` for real before trusting this against a live database, same standing advice as every increment that's touched `schema.prisma` this session.

## Picking up the Journal sentiment analysis increment

Closes the "Journal sentiment analysis / AI Memory feed" gap named in "What's not built yet" above — the fourth automatic AI Memory learning signal, and the only one not named in the PRD's original §9 list. `JournalEntry.sentimentScore` (a real column that's existed since the Journaling domain shipped, but nothing ever populated) is now scored for real, right when an entry is saved, whenever `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` is configured. A sustained run of clearly positive or clearly negative entries also writes a `journal_sentiment` AI Memory fact, injected into the planner/chat prompt the same way `chronotype`/`intervention_response`/`task_duration_accuracy` already are.

A few things worth knowing:

- **No schema migration needed** — `JournalEntry.sentimentScore` and `AiMemoryFact.factType` (a plain string, not an enum) already existed; this increment is pure application logic on top of columns that were already there, the first increment in a while that didn't need a new migration or hit the stale-generated-Prisma-client-types issue the last several increments all did.
- **`AnthropicClient.analyzeSentiment` is a forced single-tool-call, same shape as `proposeSchedule`** — a small `score_sentiment` tool with one required `score: number` field, `tool_choice` forced to it, so the response's shape is guaranteed by Anthropic's own API rather than parsed from prose. The returned value is still clamped to `[-1, 1]` before use, though — a tool schema guarantees the field is present and numeric, not that the model actually respected the stated range, the same "shape guaranteed, sanity is not" caveat `SCHEDULE_TOOL`'s own comment already documents for `proposeSchedule`.
- **`refreshJournalSentimentPattern` mirrors the exact statistical shape `refreshChronotypePattern`/`refreshTaskDurationAccuracyPattern`/`refreshInterventionResponsePattern` already establish** — a small sample-size floor (3), a bounded recent-history window (10), and a real threshold (±0.3 average, on the same -1 to 1 scale) below which nothing gets written, the same "don't manufacture a fact from noise" discipline as the other three. Only the most recent *scored* entries count — an entry from before AI was configured, or one whose scoring call failed, is silently skipped rather than treated as a 0.
- **Scoring only happens on create, not on edit** — `updateJournalEntry` never re-scores or touches `sentimentScore`, matching the "a fresh signal, not a correction to an old one" distinction this file's own comment already draws for the `journal.entryCreated` auto-replan trigger just below it. Editing an old entry's wording doesn't retroactively change how it was scored.
- **Best-effort, same as every other AI-adjacent write in this app** — a failed `analyzeSentiment` call (or AI simply not configured) leaves `sentimentScore` `null` and the entry still saves normally; a failed `refreshJournalSentimentPattern` call leaves whatever fact already existed untouched. Neither failure mode can ever block the actual journal entry the person is waiting on, same principle `ReflectionService.submit`'s own AI-summary try/catch already documents.
- **Frontend: a small, honest label, not a mood dashboard** — each journal entry now shows "Felt good" / "Mixed" / "Felt heavy" next to its timestamp when `sentimentScore` is a real number, using the same ±0.3 boundary the backend's own trend fact uses (so the label a person sees lines up with what the AI itself is reacting to) — and shows nothing at all, not a placeholder, when there's no score yet. No new page, no trend chart — that's the "doesn't feed Insights' correlation engine" gap named in "What's not built yet" above.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Journal sentiment analysis)

Six new backend e2e tests, all against a fake `AnthropicClient` (no real network call, no real API key needed): a scored entry stores the real value on `sentimentScore`; a failed scoring call leaves it `null` without failing entry creation; a sustained run of negative-scored entries writes a fact containing "trended emotionally negative" (verified reaching another AI prompt's context, the same `estimateTaskDuration`-as-a-probe trick the Chronotype AI Memory signal suite already established); the mirror case for a sustained positive run; a mixed/near-neutral run writes nothing; and too few scored entries writes nothing even with clearly one-sided scores. No new Playwright coverage — unlike Pomodoro/reminder/reflection-label settings (pure app state, testable end-to-end with no external dependency), sentiment scoring is a real network call to Anthropic, and there's no fake-provider seam reachable from a black-box browser test the way there is inside the Nest testing module; the existing `journal.spec.ts` test already covers that writing and editing an entry still works exactly as before, with or without a score attached.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`, `apps/backend/test`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/e2e/tests`) found **269 files and 0 diagnostics**.

What's *not* verified: the same standing sandbox limitation as every e2e test this session — no Postgres reachable — so the six new backend e2e tests were written and syntax/type-checked but never run for real here. Also genuinely unverified, and unverifiable in this sandbox regardless of the Postgres issue: `analyzeSentiment`'s real request/response shape against Anthropic's actual API has never been exercised — it follows `proposeSchedule`'s exact proven shape (same tool-use forcing, same response parsing), but "the same shape as code that already works" is not the same as "verified with a real API call, with a real key," the same honest caveat this README already gives the push-calendar-edit path. Worth testing against a real `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` before trusting the actual sentiment scores it returns.

## Picking up the Real-time calendar updates (webhooks) increment

Closes the "Real-time calendar updates (webhooks)" gap named in "What's not built yet" above. A connected Google account now registers a real `events.watch` channel; a connected Microsoft account now registers a real Graph `/subscriptions` resource. Either provider's own servers then POST a change notification to this backend the moment something changes on their side, which triggers the exact same incremental sync (`syncToken`/delta-link based) "Sync now" already runs — so a change made directly in Google Calendar or Outlook now shows up here without anyone tapping anything.

This one needs a real, publicly reachable deployment to do anything — add this to your `.env`:

```bash
BACKEND_PUBLIC_URL="https://your-real-public-domain.example.com"
```

Optional, same graceful-degradation pattern as every other integration in this app: leave it unset and nothing changes from before this increment — `registerWebhook` returns immediately, no channel/subscription is ever created, and every connected account just keeps working exactly as it did on manual "Sync now" alone. A `localhost` or otherwise-private address technically passes validation but can never actually receive a real webhook — that's a deployment/networking fact this app has no way to detect about itself, not something worth failing boot over (see `env.validation.ts`'s own comment on this exact point).

A few things worth knowing:

- **What actually gets registered, and when** — right after a fresh `connect()`'s own initial sync, best-effort (a failed or skipped registration never blocks the connection itself). `CalendarAccount` gained four new nullable columns for this: `webhookChannelId` (Google's channel `id` / Microsoft's `subscriptionId` — one generic column, provider-specific meaning, same pattern `syncToken` already established for the sync cursor itself), `webhookResourceId` (Google-only, needed to later stop the channel), `webhookVerificationToken` (Google's channel `token` / Microsoft's `clientState` — an app-generated secret checked on every incoming notification so a request can't be spoofed by guessing a channel id), and `webhookExpiresAt`.
- **Renewal is a real daily cron job, `SchedulerService.renewCalendarWebhooks`** — both providers cap how long a channel/subscription lasts (Google: this app requests 7 days and uses whatever it's actually granted; Microsoft: Graph's own hard ~2.94-day/4230-minute ceiling for `/me/events`), so anything expiring within the next 48 hours gets renewed once a day. Google has no "extend in place" endpoint, so its renewal is stop-the-old-channel-then-register-a-new-one; Microsoft's `subscriptions.patch` does support extending in place, so its `webhookChannelId` stays the exact same subscription id for the account's whole connected lifetime.
- **Microsoft's synchronous validation handshake is the trickiest part of this, and it's real** — Graph doesn't just accept a `notificationUrl` and hope; as part of the very `POST /subscriptions` call, Graph sends a request to that URL with a `validationToken` query param and requires a plain-text 200 echoing it back within 10 seconds *before* the subscription is even created. `MicrosoftCalendarWebhookController` handles this unconditionally, before it even looks at the request body — get this wrong (wrong content-type, wrapped in JSON, too slow) and Graph refuses to create the subscription at all, silently leaving that account back on manual sync only.
- **Both webhook endpoints are unauthenticated on purpose, like the OAuth callback and Stripe webhook controllers before them** — `webhooks/google/calendar` and `webhooks/microsoft/calendar` are plain REST, not GraphQL, and can't carry this app's own auth headers since Google/Microsoft's own servers are the caller. `CalendarAccountsService.syncByChannel`/`MicrosoftCalendarAccountsService.syncBySubscription` are what actually verify a notification is genuine (channel/resource id + token, or subscription id + clientState, all checked against what's actually stored) before triggering anything.
- **Neither provider's notification carries real event data** — just enough headers/fields to know *that* something changed and *which* registration it's for. Both webhook controllers always respond with success (Google: 200; Microsoft: 202, per its own documented expectation) regardless of what happens inside, even if the triggered sync itself fails — a missed real-time push is recoverable (the next push, or a manual "Sync now," catches up), and repeated non-2xx responses are how both providers eventually give up and disable a channel/subscription entirely, which would be a strictly worse outcome than one missed sync.
- **A small, honest "real-time sync active" badge** — shown on the Google/Microsoft connect cards in Settings when a real, not-yet-expired channel/subscription is on file (a new `realtimeSyncEnabled` field, computed from `webhookExpiresAt`, not a raw stored column). Absent, not shown as "off," when it isn't — same honest-empty-state pattern every other optional signal in this app already follows.

### What's verified vs. what you should verify yourself (Real-time calendar updates)

This increment has meaningfully stronger real verification than most AI/third-party-integration increments this session, because the piece that matters most — the verification and registration *logic* — doesn't actually require a real database or a real Google/Microsoft account to test, just the network boundary mocked out. **31 new unit tests across 6 new spec files were written *and actually executed* here, via `npx jest`, and all 31 pass for real:**

- `google-calendar-client.webhook.spec.ts` (5 tests) / `microsoft-calendar-client.webhook.spec.ts` (5 tests) — real request shape (URL, method, headers, JSON body) and response parsing for `watchEvents`/`stopChannel` and `createSubscription`/`renewSubscription`/`deleteSubscription`, against a mocked `global.fetch`. No Prisma, no Nest bootstrap needed — these two client classes take no constructor dependencies at all.
- `calendar-accounts.service.webhook.spec.ts` (7 tests) / `microsoft-calendar-accounts.service.webhook.spec.ts` (6 tests) — `registerWebhook` does nothing when `BACKEND_PUBLIC_URL` is unset, registers at the exact expected address and persists what the provider returns when it is set, and correctly refreshes an expired access token and retries once; `syncByChannel`/`syncBySubscription` correctly refuse to sync on a channel-id/resource-id/token (or subscription-id/clientState) mismatch and correctly do sync on a real match — all against a fully mocked `PrismaService`, `ConfigService`, and OAuth/client collaborators, the same `Test.createTestingModule` + `useValue` mock shape `users.service.spec.ts` already established for this codebase, just with more collaborators to mock.
- `google-calendar-webhook.controller.spec.ts` (4 tests) / `microsoft-calendar-webhook.controller.spec.ts` (4 tests) — both controllers constructed directly as plain classes (no Nest DI at all) with a mocked accounts service: Google's `resourceState: 'sync'`/missing-header early-return branches never call `syncByChannel`; Microsoft's validation-token echo is answered correctly and unconditionally, before the body is ever inspected; a real notification batch delegates once per valid entry, skipping any missing `subscriptionId`/`clientState`; and a thrown sync error on either side still resolves with a success response, never surfacing as a failure to the provider.

A full syntax sweep of everything touched (`apps/backend/src`) found **277 files and 0 diagnostics** (checked together with the rest of this session's accumulated files).

What's *not* verified, and can't be from this sandbox regardless of the unit-test coverage above: no real Google or Microsoft account has ever actually received a real webhook POST from this code, because that needs a genuinely public HTTPS deployment this sandbox doesn't have — the request/response *shapes* are proven correct against each provider's own documented API contract (mirroring `google-calendar-client.ts`/`microsoft-calendar-client.ts`'s own already-proven `listEvents`/`deleteEvent`/`updateEvent` shapes), but "the same shape as code that already works, plus a passing mocked-fetch unit test" is still not the same as a real end-to-end delivery. No new backend `test/app.e2e-spec.ts` or Playwright coverage was added on top of the 31 real unit tests above — a GraphQL-level e2e addition would have needed real Postgres (unreachable here, same standing limitation as every e2e test this session) and would have tested strictly less than what the unit tests above already prove for real; a Playwright addition has nothing to click, since the only new UI is a passive badge driven by backend state no browser test can produce without real provider credentials. Before trusting this in production: deploy somewhere with a real public URL, set `BACKEND_PUBLIC_URL`, connect a real Google and/or Microsoft account, make a change directly in that calendar, and confirm it shows up here within a few seconds without tapping "Sync now."
